Conformal field theory on the Riemann sphere and its boundary version for SLE Thanks: Nam-Gyu Kang was partially supported by Samsung Science and Technology Foundation (SSTF-BA1401-51), a KIAS Individual Grant (MG058103) at Korea Institute for Advanced Study, and the National Research Foundation of Korea (NRF-2019R1A5A1028324). Thanks: Nikolai G. Makarov was supported by NSF grant no. 1500821.
Nam-Gyu Kang, Nikolai G. Makarov

Abstract
. From conformal field theory on the Riemann sphere, we implement its boundary version in a simply-connected domain using the Schottky double construction. We consider the statistical fields generated by background charge modification of the Gaussian free field with Dirichlet boundary condition under the OPE multiplications. We prove that the correlation functions of such fields with symmetric background charges form a collection of martingale-observables for (forward) chordal/radial SLE with force points and spins. We also present the connection between conformal field theory with Neumann boundary condition and the theory of backward SLE.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 60J67, 81T40; Secondary 30C35

1.
Introduction and results

Since Belavin, Polyakov, and Zamolodchikov
[
7
]
introduced an operator algebra formalism to relate some critical models to the representation theory of Virasoro algebra, conformal field theory has been applied in string theory and condensed matter physics.
In mathematics, conformal field theory inspired the development of algebraic theories, e.g., the theory of vertex algebras.
Also, it has been used to derive several exact results for the conformally invariant critical clusters in two-dimensional lattice models.

During the last two decades, some outstanding predictions from statistical physics have been proved using Schramm-Loewner evolution (SLE).
For instance, the remarkable achievements in this area include the work of Lawler-Schramm-Werner on Mandelbrot conjecture (see
[
23
]
and references therein), Smirnov’s work on percolation model and Ising model at criticality (see
[
35
,
36
]
), and the work of Schramm-Sheffield on contour lines of the two-dimensional discrete Gaussian free field (see
[
31
]
).
Under quite general conditions, it requires just one martingale-observable, which determines the law uniquely to prove the scaling limit convergence of interface curves in lattice models.

In this paper we use the method of conformal field theory to study a certain family of chordal/radial SLE martingale-observables.
To be precise, we develop a version of conformal field theory on the Riemann sphere and apply the Schottky double construction to this theory to implement its boundary version in a simply-connected domain
D
with marked boundary/interior points.
This theory is based on non-random (background charge) modification of the Gaussian free field
\Phi
in
D
with Dirichlet boundary condition.
The theory with Neumann boundary condition is presented in Section
9
.
We state Ward’s equations in terms of Lie derivatives for the fields in the OPE family
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
generated by the modified Gaussian free field
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
with a (symmetric) background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}
placed at the marked points.
Using the BPZ-Cardy equations for the fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
for a symmetric background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}
(so that the associated partition function
Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is real-valued)
with a specific charge at the growth point
p
of SLE, we show that the correlation functions of the fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
form a collection of martingale-observables for chordal/radial
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
associated with the partition function
Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

The partition function
Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
we consider is defined in terms of the Coulomb gas correlation function
C[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
or the correlation function of the effective one-leg operator.
In the physics literature, the partition function in conformal field theory is expressed as the correlation function of the boundary condition changing operator, see (
[
11
, §11.3.1]
).
The other approaches to define the partition function in the context of SLE (up to a multiplicative constant depending possibly on
D
) can be found in
[
14
,
22
]
.
After introducing the partition functions of free field and the partition functions of SLE, Dubédat showed that they coincide and use these identities to prove local/global couplings of SLEs and free fields in
[
14
]
.
The SLE partition function can be viewed as the total mass of natural measure on SLE curves and used to describe the restriction property, e.g., see
[
22
]
.

It is a well-known statement in the physics literature (e.g., see
[
9
,
12
]
) that under the insertion of the normalized one-leg operator
\Lambda_{p}/(\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{p})
rooted at
q\in D\cup\partial D
all correlation functions of fields in the OPE family
\mathcal{F}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}
are martingale-observables for
\mathrm{SLE}(\kappa)
in
D
from
p\in\partial D
to
q.

(Here
\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{p}
is the correlation function of
\Lambda_{p}
and a specific background charge
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}
is placed at
q.
)
We construct the one-leg “operator”
\Lambda_{p}
rooted at
q
as one of the OPE exponentials of the linear combination of bosonic fields with proper charges at
p,q
so that it is a primary field with the desired conformal dimensions at
p
and
q
producing a level two degenerate vector at
p.

Considering general non-random pre-pre-Schwarzian modifications of the Gaussian free field, we extend this result to the chordal/radial SLE with forces and spins.

In constructing such OPE exponentials, it is essential to require the so-called neutrality conditions on the charges.
We clarify and explain two neutrality conditions: one condition for the linear combination of bosonic fields to be a well-defined (Fock space) field; the other condition for the (Coulomb gas) correlation function to be conformally invariant.
To reconcile these two neutrality conditions, we place the “background charge(s)”
\boldsymbol{\beta}
at the marked point(s).
We also derive the neutrality condition on the background charges using an extension of the Gauss-Bonnet theorem to the flat metric with conical singularities at the marked points.
It appears that our approach can be extended to much more general settings like general Riemann surfaces with marked points (e.g., see
[
8
]
for this approach in a doubly-connected domain) and various patterns of insertion (e.g.,
N
-leg operators with the method of screening, see
[
1
]
).
However, we do not claim that what we develop here is the only relevant conformal field theory to SLE theory.
For example, as another relevant theory, a twisted conformal field theory can be related to radial SLE.
Its bosonic observable with central charge
c=1
corresponds to a discrete observable of the harmonic explorer with
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-symmetry.

1.1.
A spherical conformal field theory and its boundary version

The fields we treat in this paper are statistical fields constructed from the Gaussian free field
\Phi
with Dirichlet boundary condition via differentiation and Wick’s formula.
In the last section we develop a version of conformal field theory constructed from the Gaussian free field
N
with Neumann boundary condition.
The
Gaussian free field

\Phi
in a simply-connected domain
D
can be viewed as a Fock space field and its 2-point correlation is given by

\mathbf{E}\,\Phi(z_{1})\Phi(z_{2})=2G(z_{1},z_{2}),

where
G
is the Dirichlet Green’s function for
D.

A
Fock space field
is a linear combination of
basic fields
, which are, by definition, formal expressions written as Wick’s product (
\odot
-product) of derivatives of
\Phi
and Wick’s exponentials
\mathrm{e}^{\odot\alpha\Phi}\,(\alpha\in\mathbb{C})
of
\Phi.

If
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}
are Fock space fields and
z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}
are distinct points in
D,
then
a
correlation function

\mathbf{E}[X_{1}(z_{1})\cdots X_{n}(z_{n})]

can be defined by Wick’s calculus.
See Section
3
or
[
18
, Lecture 1]
for more details.
(A more traditional notation for the correlation function is
\langle X_{1}(z_{1})\cdots X_{n}(z_{n})\rangle.
)
The points
z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}
are called the nodes of the (tensor) product
\mathcal{X}=X_{1}(z_{1})\cdots X_{n}(z_{n})
of
X_{j}(z_{j})
’s and we write
S_{\mathcal{X}}
for
\{z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}\}.

We extend the collection of Fock space fields by adding finite linear combinations of formal bosonic fields

\Phi[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]:=\sum\tau_{j}^{+}\Phi^{+}(z_{j})-\tau_{j}^{-}\Phi^{-}(z_{j})

with the neutrality condition

(
\mathrm{NC}_{0}
)

\sum_{j}(\tau_{j}^{+}+\tau_{j}^{-})=0

and its Wick’s exponential
\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]}.

Here
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+}
and
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}
are divisors in
D
which takes the value
0
at all points except for finitely many points:

\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\pm}=\sum\tau_{j}^{\pm}\cdot z_{j}.

The centered Gaussian formal fields
\Phi^{+},\Phi^{-}
have formal correlations

\mathbf{E}[\Phi^{+}(z_{1})\Phi^{+}(z_{2})]=\log\frac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}},\qquad\mathbf{E}[\Phi^{+}(z_{1})\Phi^{-}(z_{2})]=\log(1-z_{1}\bar{z}_{2})

in
\mathbb{D}
and the relations:
\Phi^{-}=\overline{\Phi^{+}},\Phi=\Phi^{+}+\Phi^{-}.

•
A spherical conformal field theory.
We consider a compact Riemann surface
S
of genus-zero with (finitely many) marked points
q_{k}.

We introduce the formal fields
\Psi^{\pm}
(
\Psi^{-}=\overline{\Psi^{+}}
) on
S
with formal correlations

\mathbf{E}[\Psi^{+}(z_{1})\Psi^{+}(z_{2})]=\log\frac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}},\qquad\mathbf{E}[\Psi^{+}(z_{1})\Psi^{-}(z_{2})]=0

on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}.

(A bi-variant field
\Psi^{\pm}(z,z_{0})=\Psi^{\pm}(z)-\Psi^{\pm}(z_{0})
can be defined as a multivalued correlation functional valued field.)
We then modify
\Psi^{+}
(and
\Psi^{-}
in a similar way) by adding a non-random pre-pre-Schwarzian form (PPS form)
\psi^{+}
of order
(ib,0)
with logarithmic singularities at
q_{k}
’s.
Here
b
is the modification parameter.
Such
\psi^{+}
resolves a background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}=i\partial{\bar{\partial}}\psi^{+}/\pi=\sum\beta_{k}\delta_{k}.

We write
\psi^{+}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\psi^{+},

\Psi^{+}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\Psi^{+}+\psi^{+}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

A version of the Gauss-Bonnet theorem leads to the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}):

\sum_{k}\beta_{k}=2b.

For
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\sum\tau_{j}\cdot z_{j}
satisfying
(\mathrm{NC}_{0})
we define the OPE exponential
\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]
(whose meaning is explained in Subsection
3.4
) of
\Psi^{+}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]
by

\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]:=\frac{C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\tau}+\boldsymbol{\beta}]}{C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]}\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Psi^{+}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]},\quad\Psi^{+}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]:=\sum\tau_{j}^{+}\Psi^{+}(z_{j}),

where the (formal) Coulomb gas correlation function
C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
is a
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
-differential
(\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\sum\lambda_{j}\cdot z_{j},\lambda_{j}=\sigma_{j}^{2}/2-\sigma_{j}b)
such that
its evaluation on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
is given by

(C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}})=\prod_{j<k}(z_{j}-z_{k})^{\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}},

where the product is taken over finite
z_{j}
’s and
z_{k}
’s.
One can view
C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
as the formal correlations

C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]=\mathbf{E}\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\sigma_{1}\Psi^{+}(z_{1})}\cdots\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\sigma_{n}\Psi^{+}(z_{n})}

by Wick’s rule of centered jointly (generalized) Gaussians:

\mathrm{e}^{\odot\alpha_{1}\xi_{1}}\cdots\mathrm{e}^{\odot\alpha_{n}\xi_{n}}=\mathrm{e}^{\sum_{j<k}\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k}\mathbf{E}[\xi_{j}\xi_{k}]}\mathrm{e}^{\odot\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}\xi_{j}}.

•
Schottky double construction.

We consider a simply-connected domain
D
with marked boundary/interior points
q_{k}\in\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
and the Schottky double
S
of
D
with the canonical involution
z\mapsto z^{*}.

For a divisor
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\sum\sigma_{j}\cdot z_{j},
let us denote

\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}=\sum\sigma_{j}\cdot z_{j}^{*},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\sum\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\sigma\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j}\cdot z_{j}.

Suppose that a (double) background charge
(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})

(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\pm}=\sum\beta_{k}^{\pm}\cdot q_{k})
satisfies the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}):

\sum(\beta_{k}^{+}+\beta_{k}^{-})=2b.

For this pair
(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})
and a double divisor
(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-})
satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}),
we define the modification
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
of
\Phi

(\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}_{*},\boldsymbol{\tau}=\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}_{*})
by

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Psi_{\sqrt{2}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\sqrt{2}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}^{-}}[\boldsymbol{\tau},\boldsymbol{\tau}_{*}],

where

\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]:=\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+}]+\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{-}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]
and
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{-}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]=\overline{\Psi^{+}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}[\overline{\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}}]}.

We also define the multi-vertex field
V_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\beta}+\boldsymbol{\tau}]
and the OPE exponential
\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]
by

V_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\beta}+\boldsymbol{\tau}]=C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}+\boldsymbol{\tau}]\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\tau}]},\qquad\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=\frac{C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\tau}+\boldsymbol{\beta}]}{C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]}\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\tau}]}.

In
[
18
]
we study the standard chordal theory with
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}=2b\cdot q,

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}=\boldsymbol{0}
(
q\in\partial D
).
In this case, the modification
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
reads as

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\Phi+\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\qquad\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=-2b\,\arg w^{\prime},

where
w:(D,q)\to(\mathbb{H},\infty)
is a conformal map from
D
onto the upper half-plane
\mathbb{H}.

For the standard radial theory (
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}=b\cdot q,

q\in D
) without further marked points or spins, the modification
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
reads as

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\Phi+\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\qquad\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=-2b\,\arg\frac{w^{\prime}}{w},

where
w:(D,q)\to(\mathbb{D},0)
is a conformal map from
D
onto the unit disc
\mathbb{D}.

The corresponding conformal field theory is
\mathrm{Aut}(D,q)
-invariant.
The non-random harmonic function
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
on a punctured domain
D^{*}:=D\setminus\{q\}
is multivalued.

The
bosonic
fields
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]
with the neutrality condition generate the
OPE family

\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},
the algebra (over
\mathbb{C}
) spanned by
1
and the derivatives of
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},

\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]
(
\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\cap\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\tau}=\emptyset
) under the OPE multiplication
*.

(If a single-valued field
X
is holomorphic, then the OPE product
X*Y
of two fields
X
and
Y
is the zeroth coefficient of the regular part in the operator product expansion
X(\zeta)Y(z)
as
\zeta\to z.
)
For example,
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
contains

1,J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:=\partial\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\partial J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}*(\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}*\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}),
etc.,
and the
Virasoro field

T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},

T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=-\frac{1}{2}J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}*J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+ib\partial J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

In Section
6
we extend the OPE family by adding the OPE functionals at the punctures where the background charges are placed.
For example, for
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}=b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*},

(q\in D)

(J_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}})_{q}=J(q)+\frac{ib}{2}N_{w}(q),\qquad N_{w}=\frac{w^{\prime\prime}}{w^{\prime}}

and
\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]
(
\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\cap\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\tau}\neq\emptyset
) belong to the extended OPE family.

•
Insertion formulas.

We explain how the insertion of Wick’s exponential
V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]:=\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\tau}]}
of the Gaussian free field gives rise to the change of background charge modifications.
A particular insertion procedure plays an essential role in establishing the connection between chordal/radial SLE theory with force points (and spins) and conformal field theory in a simply-connected domain with certain symmetric background charges.

Theorem 1.1
.

Given two background charges
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,},\boldsymbol{\beta}
with the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}),

the image of
\mathcal{F}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}
under the insertion of
V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}]
is
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

•
Ward’s equations and BPZ-Cardy equations.

We mostly concern ourselves with a symmetric background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu.

One of our main goals is to present the precise relation between conformal field theory
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
and
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
for such a symmetric background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta},
see Theorem
1.5
below.

Let
A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:=T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

For a meromorphic vector field in
\bar{D},
we define

W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(v)=2\,\mathrm{Re}\,W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}(v),\qquad W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}(v)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial D}vA_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{D}({\bar{\partial}}v)A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},

where
{\bar{\partial}}v
in the last integral should be understood in the sense of distributions and the first integral should be taken in the sense of the Cauchy principal value if necessary.
The following theorem enables us to represent the action of the Lie derivatives operators
\mathcal{L}_{v}
by inserting the Ward functionals into correlation functions of fields in the extended OPE family.

Theorem 1.2

(Ward’s identities)
.

Let
X_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
be a tensor product of fields in the extended OPE family
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

If the nodes of
X_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
do not intersect with the poles of
v,
then we have

\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{L}_{v}X_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\mathbf{E}\,W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(v)X_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

The proof of Theorem
1.2
is based on the following residue form of Ward identity

(1.1)

\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}(z)X_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{(z)}vA_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}X_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

for a field
X_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
in the OPE family with a proper meaning of equality.
Here
\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}
is the
\mathbb{C}
-linear part of the Lie derivative
\mathcal{L}_{v}.

We emphasize that (
1.1
) has a different nature at
z
in
\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}\cup\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}
and at each node
z
of
X_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

As Fock space functionals, (
1.1
) holds at each node
z
of
X_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},
see Lemma
7.1
.
On the other hand, (
1.1
) holds
within correlations
at
z
in
\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}\cup\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-},
see Lemma
7.2
.

Ward’s equations (see Theorem
1.3
below) describe the insertion of the Virasoro field within correlations of fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
in terms of the Lie derivative operators and the puncture operators
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:=C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}].

Let
k_{z}
(resp.
v_{z}
) be the chordal (resp. radial) Loewner vector field with pole at
z:

k_{z}(\zeta)=\frac{1}{z-\zeta},\quad\Big(\textrm{resp. }v_{z}(\zeta)=\zeta\frac{z+\zeta}{z-\zeta}\Big)

in the identity chart of
\mathbb{H}
(resp. in the identity chart of
\mathbb{D}
).

Theorem 1.3

(Ward’s equations)
.

Given a symmetric background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}
with the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}),

let
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
and let
X
be the tensor product of
X_{j}
’s.
Then

\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)\,X=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{+}_{k_{z}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}X+\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{-}_{k_{\bar{z}}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}X,

where all fields are evaluated in the identity chart of
\mathbb{H}

and

2z^{2}\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)\,X=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{+}_{v_{z}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}X+\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{-}_{v_{z^{*}}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}X,

where all fields are evaluated in the identity chart of
\mathbb{D}.

Let
\boldsymbol{\beta}
be a symmetric background charge with the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
and a specific charge
a
at a marked boundary point
p\in\partial D.

We consider a symmetric divisor
\boldsymbol{\tau}=a\cdot p+\cdots
satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0})

and let
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}=\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\tau}.

By Theorem
1.1
,
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is the image of
\mathcal{F}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}
under the insertion of

\frac{\Lambda_{p}}{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{p}},

where
\Lambda_{p}:=\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]
is the one-leg operator and
\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{p}=C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]/C_{(b)}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]
is its correlation.
Let
\mathcal{X}
be a string in
\mathcal{F}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}.

We assume that
\zeta
is not a node of
\mathcal{X}.

Denote

\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}\,\mathcal{X}:=\frac{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{\zeta}\mathcal{X}}{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{\zeta}}.

We define the partition function associated with a symmetric background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}
by

Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:=\big|C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]\big|

and let

Z_{\zeta}=Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\zeta}},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\zeta}=\boldsymbol{\beta}+a\cdot\zeta-a\cdot p.

The following version of BPZ-Cardy equations play a crucial role in understanding precise relation between conformal field theory and SLE.

Theorem 1.4
.

Given a symmetric background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}
with
\boldsymbol{\beta}(p)=a
and the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}),
let
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
and let
X
be the tensor product of
X_{j}
’s.
Suppose that the parameters
a
and
b
satisfy

2a(a+b)=1.

If
\xi\in\mathbb{R},
then in the identity chart of
\mathbb{H},
we have

\frac{1}{2a^{2}}\Big(\partial_{\xi}^{2}+2\big(\partial_{\xi}\log Z_{\xi}\big)\partial_{\xi}\Big)\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X=\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X,

where
\partial_{\xi}=\partial+{\bar{\partial}}
is the operator of differentiation with respect to the real variable
\xi
and
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}
is taken over the finite notes of
X
and
\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}\setminus\{\xi\}.

In the
(\mathbb{D},0)
-uniformization we have

-\frac{2}{a^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\big(\partial_{\theta}\log Z_{\zeta}\big)\partial_{\theta}\Big)\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}X=\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta}}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}X,\qquad(\zeta=\mathrm{e}^{i\theta},\theta\in\mathbb{R}),

where the Lie derivative
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta}}
is taken over the finite notes of
X
and
\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\zeta}\setminus\{\zeta\}.

1.2.
SLE and martingale-observables

Since Schramm introduced SLE in
[
30
]
as the only possible candidates for the scaling limits of interface curves in critical 2-D lattice models, SLE has been used with remarkable success to prove some important conjectures in statistical physics.
For example, see the work of Lawler-Schramm-Werner (
[
23
,
25
]
), Schramm-Sheffield (
[
31
,
32
]
), and Smirnov (
[
35
,
36
]
).

•
Standard chordal/radial SLEs.

For a simply-connected domain
(D,p,q)
with a marked boundary point
p
and a marked interior point
q,
radial Schramm-Loewner evolution (SLE) in
(D,p,q)
with a positive parameter
\kappa
is the conformally invariant law on random curves from
p
to
q
satisfying the so-called “domain Markov property” (see (
1.3
) below).
Let us review the basic definitions of radial
\mathrm{SLE}(\kappa)
in technical terms. For each
z\in D,
let
g_{t}(z)
be the solution (which exists up to a time
\tau_{z}\in(0,\infty]
) of the equation

(1.2)

\partial_{t}g_{t}(z)=g_{t}(z)\frac{\zeta_{t}+g_{t}(z)}{\zeta_{t}-g_{t}(z)},

where
g_{0}:(D,p,q)\to(\mathbb{D},1,0)
is a given conformal map,
\zeta_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{t}},\theta_{t}=\sqrt{\kappa}B_{t},
and
B_{t}
is a standard Brownian motion with
B_{0}=0.

Then for all
t,

w_{t}:(D_{t},\gamma_{t},q)\to(\mathbb{D},1,0),\qquad w_{t}(z):=g_{t}(z)/\zeta_{t}=g_{t}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-i\sqrt{\kappa}B_{t}}

is a well-defined conformal map from

D_{t}:=\{z\in D:\tau_{z}>t\}

onto the unit disc
\mathbb{D}.

It is known that the SLE stopping time
\tau_{z}
(defined to be the first time when the solution of Loewner equation (
1.2
) does not exist) satisfies

\lim_{t\uparrow\tau_{z}}w_{t}(z)=1.

The
radial SLE curve

\gamma
is defined by the equation

\gamma_{t}\equiv\gamma(t):=\lim_{z\to 1}w_{t}^{-1}(z)

and satisfies the “domain Markov property,”

(1.3)

\mathrm{law}\,\left(\gamma[t,\infty)\,|\,\gamma[0,t]\right)\,=\,\mathrm{law}\,\gamma_{D_{t},\gamma_{t},q}[0,\infty).

The sets
K_{t}:=\{z\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}:\tau_{z}\leq t\}
are called the
hulls
of the SLE.

For a simply-connected domain
(D,p,q)
with two marked boundary points
p,q,
the chordal SLE map
g_{t}
with a positive parameter
\kappa
satisfies the equation

(1.4)

\partial_{t}g_{t}(z)=\frac{2}{g_{t}(z)-\xi_{t}},

where
g_{0}:(D,p,q)\to(\mathbb{H},0,\infty)
is a given conformal map and
\xi_{t}=\sqrt{\kappa}B_{t}.

Then for all
t,

w_{t}(z):=g_{t}(z)-\xi_{t}

is a well-defined conformal map from the domain
D_{t}:=\{z\in D:\tau_{z}>t\}

onto
\mathbb{H},

where the SLE stopping time
\tau_{z}
satisfies

\lim_{t\uparrow\tau_{z}}w_{t}(z)=0.

The
chordal SLE curve

\gamma
is defined by the equation

\gamma_{t}\equiv\gamma(t):=\lim_{z\to 0}w_{t}^{-1}(z).

•
Martingale-observables.

Many results in the SLE theory and its applications depend on the explicit form of certain martingale-observables.
A non-random
conformal
field
f
is an assignment of a (smooth) function

(f\,\|\,\phi):~\phi U\to\mathbb{C}

to each local chart
\phi:U\to\phi U.

For a non-random conformal field
M
of
n
variables either in
\mathbb{H}
(in the chordal case) or in
\mathbb{D}
(in the radial case), let us define
M
in any simply-connected domain
(D,p,q)
with marked points
p\in\partial D,q\in\partial D\cup D
by

(M_{D,p,q}\,\|\,\mathrm{id})=(M\,\|\,w^{-1}).

Here
w:(D,p,q)\to(\mathbb{H},0,\infty)
is a conformal map in the chordal case (
q\in\partial D
) and
w:(D,p,q)\to(\mathbb{D},1,0)
is a conformal map in the radial case (
q\in D
).
We say that
M
is a
martingale-observable
for chordal/radial
\mathrm{SLE}(\kappa)
if for any
z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}\in D,
the process

M_{t}(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})=M_{D_{t},\gamma_{t},q}(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})

(stopped when any
z_{j}
exits
D_{t}
) is a local martingale on chordal/radial SLE probability space.
For instance, we can use the identity chart of
D,
and then for
[h^{+},h^{-}]
-differentials
M
with conformal dimensions
[h_{q}^{+},h_{q}^{-}]
at
q,
we have

M_{t}(z)=(w_{t}^{\prime}(z))^{h^{+}}(\overline{w_{t}^{\prime}(z)})^{h^{-}}(w_{t}^{\prime}(q))^{h_{q}^{+}}(\overline{w_{t}^{\prime}(q)})^{h_{q}^{-}}M(w_{t}(z)).

The notion of martingale-observables can be extended naturally to chordal/radial
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
with force points and spins for a general symmetric background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}=(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})
with the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
and a specific charge
a
at
p,
see Theorem
1.5
below.
The difference
\lambda_{b}(\beta_{k}^{+})-\lambda_{b}(\overline{\beta_{k}^{+}})
is called the (conformal)
spin
of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
at
q_{k}.

Here
\lambda_{b}(\sigma):={\sigma^{2}}/2-\sigma b
is the conformal dimension of charge
\sigma.

•
SLEs with force points and spins.

We now generalize the standard chordal/radial SLEs to
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
with finitely many force points and spins.
Let

(1.5)

a=\pm\sqrt{2/\kappa},\quad b=a(\kappa/4-1)

so that
a
and
b
satisfy
2a(a+b)=1.

By definition, for a given symmetric background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}
on
S=D^{\mathrm{double}}
satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
with a specific charge
a
at
p,

the chordal (resp. radial)
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
map
g_{t}
satisfies the chordal Loewner equation (
1.4
) (resp. the radial Loewner equation (
1.2
)) driven by the real process
\xi_{t}:

(1.6)

\mathrm{d}\xi_{t}=\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\lambda(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,\quad\lambda(t)=(\lambda\,\|\,g_{t}^{-1}),\quad\lambda=\kappa\,\partial_{\xi}\log Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}},

(resp. by the real process
\theta_{t}:

(1.7)

\mathrm{d}\theta_{t}=\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\lambda(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,\quad\lambda(t)=(\lambda\,\|\,g_{t}^{-1}),\quad\lambda=\kappa\,\partial_{\theta}\log Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\zeta}},\quad\zeta=\mathrm{e}^{i\theta},\quad\zeta_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{t}}.)

The chordal
\mathrm{SLE}(\kappa,\boldsymbol{\rho})
driven by

\mathrm{d}\xi_{t}=\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\sum_{k}\frac{\rho_{k}\,\mathrm{d}t}{\xi_{t}-q_{k}(t)},\qquad\mathrm{d}q_{k}(t)=\frac{2}{q_{k}(t)-\xi_{t}}\,\mathrm{d}t

has the background charge

\boldsymbol{\beta}=a\cdot p+\sum_{k}\beta_{k}\cdot q_{k}+(2b-a-\beta)\cdot q,

where
\beta_{k}=\rho_{k}/\sqrt{2\kappa}
and
\beta=\sum_{k}\beta_{k}.

It is known that the chordal
\mathrm{SLE}(\kappa,\boldsymbol{\rho})
is almost surely a continuous path, see
[
26
, Theorem 1.3]
.
The radial
\mathrm{SLE}_{\eta}(\kappa,\boldsymbol{\rho})

driven by

\zeta_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{t}}:

(1.8)

\mathrm{d}\theta_{t}=\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\eta\,\mathrm{d}t+i\sum_{k}\frac{\rho_{k}}{2}\cot\frac{\theta_{t}-\vartheta_{k}(t)}{2}\,\mathrm{d}t,\quad\mathrm{d}q_{k}(t)=q_{k}(t)\frac{\zeta_{t}+q_{k}(t)}{\zeta_{t}-q_{k}(t)}\,\mathrm{d}t,\quad q_{k}=\mathrm{e}^{i\vartheta_{k}}

has the background charge

\boldsymbol{\beta}=a\cdot p+\sum_{k}\beta_{k}\cdot q_{k}+\Big(b-\frac{a+\beta+i\delta}{2}\Big)\cdot q+\Big(b-\frac{a+\beta-i\delta}{2}\Big)\cdot q^{*},\qquad\delta=\eta a.

In
[
27
]
, Miller and Sheffield identified the radial
\mathrm{SLE}_{\eta}(\kappa,\boldsymbol{\rho})
with the flow lines of a (formal) vector field
\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\sigma\cdot z,-\sigma\cdot z],

where
\sigma
is chosen such that the conformal spin
s:=\lambda^{+}-\lambda^{-}=-2\sigma b=1.

We now briefly explain how the chordal
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
process can be produced from the standard chordal
\mathrm{SLE}(\kappa)
or
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}]
(
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}:=a\cdot p+(2b-a)\cdot q
) by the density
\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}}[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}].

By Proposition 14.3 in [KM13] or the special case of Theorem
1.5
below, the ratio of partition functions

M:=\frac{Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}{Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}}}=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}}[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}]

is a martingale-observable for
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}]
with

\frac{\mathrm{d}M_{t}}{M_{t}}=\sqrt{\kappa}\,\partial_{\xi}|_{\xi=\xi_{t}}(\log\frac{Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}}}{Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}^{0}}}\,\|\,g_{t}^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}.

From the fact that
Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}^{0}}=1
in the
(\mathbb{H},\infty)
-uniformization, it follows that
the drift term

(1.9)

\frac{\mathrm{d}\langle\xi,M\rangle_{t}}{M_{t}}=\kappa\,\partial_{\xi}\big|_{\xi=\xi_{t}}(\log Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}}\,\|\,g_{t}^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}t

from Girsanov’s theorem corresponds to the drift in (
1.6
), see
[
33
, Theorem 6]
.

In Subsection
8.3
we present a certain collection of chordal/radial SLE martingale-observables not by Itô’s calculus but conformal field theory.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\beta}
be a symmetric background charge with the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
and a specific charge
a
at
p
satisfying (
1.5
).
Suppose
\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is a string in the OPE family
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
of
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},
then the non-random field

M=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

is a martingale-observable for chordal/radial
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}].

In the physics literature, particular forms of this version appeared in
[
4
,
5
,
10
,
20
,
29
]
.
In
[
14
]
Dubédat coupled
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
with
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

1.3.
Examples of SLE martingale-observables

In Subsection
8.4
we present several examples of radial SLE martingale-observables.

•
Radial
\mathrm{SLE}(0)
observables.
The case
\kappa=0
reveals some aspects of “field Markov property.”
Indeed,
\mathrm{SLE}(0)
curves are hyperbolic geodesics, and martingale-observables (of one variable) are non-random fields
F\equiv F_{D,p,q}
with the property

F\big|_{D_{t}}=F_{D_{t},\gamma_{t},q}.

In a sense one can think of them as integrals of the motion
t\mapsto\{D_{t},\gamma_{t},q\}
in the corresponding Teichmüller space.
The reader is invited to check that

\arg\big((1-w)w^{-3/2}w^{\prime}\big),\qquad S_{w}+\frac{3}{8}\Big(\frac{w^{\prime}}{w}\Big)^{2}\Big(1-\frac{4w}{(1-w)^{2}}\Big)

are
\mathrm{SLE}(0)
observables.
Here

S_{w}=N_{w}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}{N_{w}^{2}},\,N_{w}=(\log w^{\prime})^{\prime}

are Schwarzian and pre-Schwarzian derivatives of
w,
respectively.
(For the first
\mathrm{SLE}(0)
observable, consider the radial version of Schramm-Sheffield martingale-observables,

a\Big(\arg\frac{(1-w)^{2}}{w}-2\big(\frac{\kappa}{4}-1\big)\arg\frac{w^{\prime}}{w}\Big),\qquad(a=\sqrt{2/\kappa}),

(see the first example in Subsection
8.4
) and normalize them so that the limit exists as
\kappa\to 0.

For the second
\mathrm{SLE}(0)
observable, consider the 1-point functions of the Virasoro fields,

\frac{c}{12}S_{w}+h_{1,2}\frac{w^{\prime 2}}{w(1-w)^{2}}+h_{0,1/2}\frac{w^{\prime 2}}{w^{2}},

where the central charge
c
and the conformal dimensions
h_{1,2},h_{0,1/2}
are given by

c=\frac{(3\kappa-8)(6-\kappa)}{2\kappa},\quad h_{1,2}=\frac{6-\kappa}{2\kappa},\quad h_{0,1/2}=\frac{(6-\kappa)(\kappa-2)}{16\kappa}.

See (
5.25
).)
Recently, in
[
2
]
multiple
\mathrm{SLE}(0)
observables are used to show Peltola and Wang’s theorem (
[
28
]
):
multiple
\mathrm{SLE}(0)
curves are contained in the real locus of real rational functions with prescribed real critical points.

•
Radial
\mathrm{SLE}(\kappa)
observables.

For
\kappa>0,
the usual way to find martingale-observables of a given conformal type is using Itô’s calculus.
A couple of well-known important examples are referred to below.

Example

(
\kappa=2
)
.

The scalar (i.e.,
[0,0]
-differential)

M=\frac{1-|w|^{2}}{|1-w|^{2}}=\frac{P_{\mathbb{D}}(1,w)}{P_{\mathbb{D}}(1,0)}=\frac{P_{D}(p,z)}{P_{D}(p,q)}

played an important role in the theory of loop erased random walk (LERW), see
[
25
]
.
Here
P_{D}
is the Poisson kernel of a domain
D.

Example

(
\kappa=6
)
.

The martingale-observable

M_{t}(z)=\mathrm{e}^{t/4}\frac{\sqrt[3]{1-w_{t}(z)}}{\sqrt[6]{w_{t}(z)}}

is a scalar with respect to
z
and a
[\frac{1}{8},\frac{1}{8}]
-differential with respect to
q.

Lawler, Schramm, and Werner applied the optional stopping theorem to the martingale
M_{t}(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})
and estimated the probability that the point
\mathrm{e}^{i\theta}
is not swallowed by the
\mathrm{SLE}_{6}
hull
K_{t}
at time
t
to be

\mathbf{P}[\,\mathrm{e}^{i\theta}\notin K_{t}\,]\asymp\mathrm{e}^{-2th_{q}}\sqrt[3]{\sin\frac{\theta}{2}},\qquad h_{q}=\frac{1}{8},\qquad(0\leq\theta<2\pi).

The exponent
2h_{q}=1/4
is one of many exponents in
[
24
]
.
See the example (derivative exponents on the boundary) in Subsection
8.5
with
\kappa=6
and
h=0.

•
Lawler-Schramm-Werner’s derivative exponents.

Examples of 1-point rooted vertex observables include Lawler-Schramm-Werner’s derivative exponents (
[
24
]
) of radial SLEs on the boundary: given
h

\mathbf{E}[|w^{\prime}_{t}(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})|^{h}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{\mathrm{e}^{i\theta}}>t\}}]\asymp\mathrm{e}^{-2h_{q}t}\Big(\sin^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}a\sigma},

where
\sigma
and
h_{q}
are given by

\sigma=\frac{a}{4}\big(\kappa-4+\sqrt{(\kappa-4)^{2}+16\kappa h}\big),\qquad h_{q}=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{8}+\frac{a\sigma}{4}.

•
Friedrich-Werner’s formula.

In the chordal case with
\kappa=8/3

(c=0),
it is well known (
[
15
]
) that the
n
-point function
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}\,[T(x_{1})\cdots T(x_{n})\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{H}}\,]
of Virasoro field
T\equiv T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\boldsymbol{\beta}=2b\cdot q
coincides with Friedrich-Werner’s function
B(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}):

B(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\mathbf{P}(\mathrm{SLE}(8/3)\textrm{ hits all }[x_{j},x_{j}+i\varepsilon\sqrt{2}])}{\varepsilon^{2n}}.

We derive the radial version of this formula.
See Theorem
8.3
.

•
Restriction property.

We also use the one-leg operator
\Lambda
to present a field theoretic proof of the restriction property of radial
\mathrm{SLE}(8/3)
: for all hull
K,

(1.10)

\mathbf{P}(\mathrm{SLE}(8/3)\textrm{ avoids }K)=|\Psi_{K}^{\prime}(1)|^{\lambda}(\Psi_{K}^{\prime}(0))^{\mu},\qquad\Big(\lambda=\frac{5}{8},\,\mu=\frac{5}{48}\Big),

where
\Psi_{K}
is the conformal map
(\mathbb{D}\setminus K,0)\to(\mathbb{D},0)
satisfying
\Psi_{K}^{\prime}(0)>0.

In particular, we explain the restriction exponents
\lambda
and
\mu
in terms of conformal dimensions of the one-leg operators and their effective versions:

\lambda=h(\Lambda):=\frac{a^{2}}{2}-ab=\frac{6-\kappa}{2\kappa},\qquad\mu=H_{q}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}):=\frac{a^{2}}{4}-b^{2}=\frac{(\kappa-2)(6-\kappa)}{8\kappa},

where
H_{q}=h_{q}^{+}+h_{q}^{-}
and

\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}=\mathcal{P}_{q}\,\Lambda=V\big[a\cdot z+\big(b-\frac{a}{2}\big)\cdot q+\big(b-\frac{a}{2}\big)\cdot q^{*}\big],\qquad(\mathcal{P}_{q}=C_{(b)}[b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*}]).

See Subsection
4.4
.

1.4.
Neumann boundary condition and backward SLEs

In Section
9
we sketch an implementation of a version of conformal field theory with Neumann boundary condition.
The Gaussian free field
N(z,z_{0})
in
D
with Neumann boundary condition can be constructed from the Gaussian free field
\Psi(z)
on
S=D^{\mathrm{double}}:

N(z,z_{0})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(\Psi(z,z_{0})+\Psi(z^{*},z_{0}^{*})\big).

For a background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}
on
S
with the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}),
we introduce the background modification
N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
of
N.

We present the connection between the OPE family
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{N}
of
N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
and the backward chordal/radial
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}].

Suppose that the parameters
a
and
b
are related to the SLE parameter
\kappa
as

a=\pm\sqrt{2/\kappa},\qquad b=-a(\kappa/4+1).

Let
\boldsymbol{\beta}
be a symmetric background charge with the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
and a specific charge
a
at a marked boundary point
p\in\partial D.

The backward chordal
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
map
f_{t}
from
\mathbb{H}
satisfies the equation

(1.11)

\partial_{t}f_{t}(z)=-\frac{2}{f_{t}(z)-\xi_{t}}

driven by the real process
\xi_{t}:

\mathrm{d}\xi_{t}=\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\lambda(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,\quad\lambda(t)=(\lambda\,\|\,f_{t}^{-1}),\quad\lambda=\kappa\,\partial_{\xi}\log Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}},

where the partition function
Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}}
is given by
Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}}=C_{(-ib)}[-i\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}]
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}=\boldsymbol{\beta}+a\cdot\xi-a\cdot p.

Its radial counterpart can be defined in a similar way, see Subsection
9.5

We say a non-random conformal field
M
is a martingale-observable for backward chordal/radial
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]

if for any
z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{H},
the process

M_{t}(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})=(M\,\|\,f_{t}^{-1})(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})

is a local martingale on backward chordal/radial SLE probability space.

Theorem 1.6
.

The correlations of fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{N}
form a collection of martingale-observables for backward chordal/radial
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}].

In this theory, the central charge
c
is given by

c=1+12b^{2}=13+12(\kappa/8+2/\kappa)\geq 25.

2.
Coulomb gas correlations

In this section we introduce the Coulomb gas correlations as the (holomorphic) differentials
with conformal dimensions

\lambda_{j}={\sigma_{j}^{2}}/2-\sigma_{j}b

at
z_{j}
’s (including infinity) and with values

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j<k\\
z_{j},z_{k}\neq\infty\end{subarray}}(z_{j}-z_{k})^{\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}},\qquad(z_{j}\in\widehat{\mathbb{C}})

in the identity chart of
\mathbb{C}
and the chart
z\mapsto-1/z
at infinity.
After explaining this definition, we prove that under the neutrality condition
\sum\sigma_{j}=2b
the Coulomb gas correlation functions are conformally invariant with respect to the Möbius group
\mathrm{Aut}(\widehat{\mathbb{C}}).

2.1.
Coulomb gas correlations on the Riemann sphere

Let

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\sum\sigma_{j}\cdot z_{j},

where
\{z_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
is a finite set of (distinct) points on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
and
\sigma_{j}
’s are real
numbers, (“charges” at
z_{j}
’s),

\sigma_{j}=\sigma_{z_{j}}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}(z_{j}).

We can think of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
as a divisor (a function
\boldsymbol{\sigma}:\widehat{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{R}
which takes the value
0
at all points except for finitely many points) or as an atomic measure:

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\sum\sigma_{j}\cdot\delta_{z_{j}}.

So

\int\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\sum\sigma_{j}.

(Some of
\sigma_{j}
’s can be zero, and in any case
\sigma_{z}=0
if
z
is not one of
z_{j}
’s.
Sometimes we allow
\sigma_{j}
’s to be complex.
We need this case e.g., for the one-leg operators with spin
s\in\mathbb{C},
see Subsection
8.2
.)
For a divisor
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
in
\mathbb{C},
i.e.,
\sigma_{\infty}=0,
we define the correlation function
C_{\mathbb{C}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
by

(2.1)

C_{\mathbb{C}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]\equiv C_{\mathbb{C}}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{z})=\prod_{j<k}(z_{j}-z_{k})^{\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}}.

This is a holomorphic function of
\boldsymbol{z}
in
\mathbb{C}^{N}_{\textrm{distinct}}=\{\boldsymbol{z}=(z_{1},\cdots,z_{N})\in\mathbb{C}^{N}\,|\,z_{j}\neq z_{k}\textrm{ if }j\neq k\}.

Typically it is multivalued except in special cases that all
\sigma
’s are integers.
(If they are all even, then the order in the product (
2.1
) does not matter.)
If all
\sigma_{j}=1,
then the correlation function is the Vandermonde determinant.

In general, we need to interpret formulas in terms of single-valued branches.
(Sometimes, single-valuedness is referred to as “physically.”)
If all
z_{j}
’s are in the real line
\mathbb{R},
then it is physical.
If
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is symmetric or anti-symmetric with respect to
\mathbb{R}
in
\mathbb{C},
then the correlation function is single-valued.
See Examples (b) and (c) in Subsection
2.5
.

We can extend this definition to divisors on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
by simply ignoring the
charge at infinity: if
\boldsymbol{\sigma}:\widehat{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{R},
then

C_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]:=C_{\mathbb{C}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}|_{\mathbb{C}}].

2.2.
Conformal weights and neutrality condition

Fix a (real) parameter
b.

We say that a divisor
\boldsymbol{\sigma}:\widehat{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies the neutrality condition (
\mathrm{NC}_{b}
) if

(2.2)

\int\boldsymbol{\sigma}=2b.

Note that there is a 1-to-1 correspondence between divisors on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying (
\mathrm{NC}_{b}
) and arbitrary divisors in
\mathbb{C}:

\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mapsto\boldsymbol{\sigma}|_{\mathbb{C}}.

Let

\lambda_{b}(\sigma)=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}-\sigma b\quad(\sigma\in\mathbb{R}).

Using this function
\lambda:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},
we define the “weights” or “dimensions”
\lambda_{j}
at
z_{j}
by

\lambda_{j}=\lambda_{b}(\sigma_{j})\equiv\frac{\sigma_{j}^{2}}{2}-\sigma_{j}b.

It is obvious that
\lambda_{b}(\sigma)=\lambda_{b}(2b-\sigma).

2.3.
Coulomb gas correlation functions as differentials

Let us recall the definitions of conformal fields and certain transformation laws such as differentials.
A local coordinate chart on a Riemann surface
M
is a conformal map
\phi:U\to\phi(U)\subset\mathbb{C}
on an open subset
U
of
M.

By definition, a non-random
conformal
field
f
is an assignment of a (smooth) function

(f\,\|\,\phi):~\phi(U)\to\mathbb{C}

to each local chart
\phi:U\to\phi(U).

A non-random conformal field
f
is a
differential
of weights or (conformal) dimensions
[\lambda,\lambda_{*}]
if for any two overlapping charts
\phi
and
\widetilde{\phi},
we have

f=(h^{\prime})^{\lambda}(\overline{h^{\prime}})^{\lambda_{*}}\widetilde{f}\circ h,

where
h=\widetilde{\phi}\circ\phi^{-1}:~\phi(U\cap\widetilde{U})\to\widetilde{\phi}(U\cap\widetilde{U})
is the transition map, and
f
(resp.
\widetilde{f}
) is the notation for
(f\,\|\,\phi),
(resp.
(f\,\|\,\widetilde{\phi})
).

Pre-pre-Schwarzian forms
of order
(\mu,\nu)
(or
\mathrm{PPS}(\mu,\nu)
forms),
pre-Schwarzian forms
of order
\mu
(or
\mathrm{PS}(\mu)
forms), and
Schwarzian forms
of order
\mu
are fields with transformation laws

f=\widetilde{f}\circ h+\mu\log h^{\prime}+\nu\,\log\overline{h^{\prime}},\qquad f=h^{\prime}\widetilde{f}\circ h+\mu N_{h},\qquad f=(h^{\prime})^{2}\widetilde{f}\circ h+\mu S_{h},

respectively, where

N_{h}=(\log h^{\prime})^{\prime},\qquad S_{h}=N_{h}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}{N_{h}^{2}}

are pre-Schwarzian and Schwarzian derivatives of
h.

The transformation laws can be extended to the random field: e.g., a field
X
is called a
[\lambda,\lambda_{*}]
-differential if the non-random field
z\mapsto\mathbf{E}[X(z)\mathcal{Y}]
is a
[\lambda,\lambda_{*}]
-differential in
z
for each
\mathcal{Y}.

Let
S
be a compact Riemann surface of genus zero, and let

\boldsymbol{\sigma}:S\to\mathbb{R}

be a divisor satisfying (
\mathrm{NC}_{b}
).
We define the Coulomb gas correlation function

C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}](\equiv C_{S}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}])\equiv C^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{z})

as a (multivalued) holomorphic differential in
z_{j}
’s such that

(a)

the conformal dimensions at
z_{j}
’s are the numbers
\lambda_{j}
’s;

(b)

if
S=\widehat{\mathbb{C}},
then
C_{\mathbb{C}}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{z})
is the value of
C^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(z)
in the identity charts at finite
z_{j}
’s and the chart
z\mapsto-1/z
at infinity (in the case
\sigma_{\infty}\neq 0)
.

Alternatively, we can restate (
b
) as follows: if
\phi:S\to\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
is a uniformizing conformal map, then

\{C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]\,\|\,\phi\}=C_{\mathbb{C}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}\circ\phi^{-1}].

(By convention,
\|\phi
refers to the chart
-1/\phi
at the pre-image
\phi^{-1}(\infty)
of infinity.)

Examples
.

(a) If
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=2b\cdot z,
then

C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
is a (scalar) function of
z
namely,
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]\equiv 1.

(b) If
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\sigma\cdot z_{1}+(2b-\sigma)z_{2},
then

C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]=(z_{1}-z_{2})^{-2\lambda},\qquad\lambda=\lambda_{b}(\sigma)=\frac{1}{2}\sigma(\sigma-2b)

for finite
z_{1},z_{2}
and
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]\equiv 1
for
z_{2}=\infty.

We note that
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
is a
\lambda
-differential in
z_{1},z_{2},
and a more traditional notation would be

\bigg(\frac{dz_{1}\,dz_{2}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}\!\bigg)^{\lambda}.

Möbius invariance of the bi-differential

\frac{dz_{1}\,dz_{2}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}

is of course well known, and could be used as an alternate way to derive our theorem.

2.4.
Möbius invariance

To justify the above definition of Coulomb gas correlation function, we need to verify that the differentials
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
are Möbius invariant on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}.

Since translation invariance of
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
is obvious, we need to verify the invariance of
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
under the dilation-rotations
\tau(z)=az
and the inversion
\tau(z)=-1/z.

First we assume
\sigma_{\infty}=0
and denote

C(\boldsymbol{z})=\prod_{j<k}(z_{j}-z_{k})^{\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}},\quad(\boldsymbol{z}=(z_{1},\cdots,z_{N}))

(so that
C=C_{\mathbb{C}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
).
For a Möbius map
\tau,
we also denote
\tau\boldsymbol{z}=(\tau z_{1},\cdots,\tau z_{N}).

Lemma 2.1
.

If
\boldsymbol{\sigma}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
, and if
\tau
is a Möbius map such that
\tau(z_{j})\neq\infty,
then

(2.3)

C(\boldsymbol{z})=C(\tau\boldsymbol{z})\prod_{j}\big(\tau^{\prime}(z_{j})\big)^{\lambda_{j}}.

Proof.

For a dilation-rotation
\tau(z)=az\,(a\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}),
we have

C(\tau\boldsymbol{z})=C(\boldsymbol{z})a^{\sum_{j<k}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}}\textrm{ and }\prod_{j}\big(\tau^{\prime}(z_{j})\big)^{\lambda_{j}}=a^{\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}},

so conformal invariance of
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
under the dilation-rotations means that

\sum_{j<k}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}^{2}=\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}b.

This identity holds by the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
– both sides are equal to
\frac{1}{2}(\sum_{j}\sigma_{j})^{2}.

For the inversion
\tau(z)=-1/z,
we have

C(\tau\boldsymbol{z})=C(\boldsymbol{z})\prod_{j<k}(z_{j}z_{k})^{-\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}}\textrm{ and }\prod_{j}\big(\tau^{\prime}(z_{j})\big)^{\lambda_{j}}=\prod_{j}z_{j}^{2\sigma_{j}b-\sigma_{j}^{2}}.

Thus (
2.3
) reduces to

\prod_{j<k}(z_{j}z_{k})^{\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}}\prod_{j}z_{j}^{\sigma_{j}^{2}}=\prod_{j}z_{j}^{2\sigma_{j}b}.

This holds by
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
because the exponents of
z_{j}
on both sides coincide:

\sigma_{j}^{2}+\sum_{k\neq j}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}=\sigma_{j}^{2}+\sigma_{j}(2b-\sigma_{j})=2\sigma_{j}b.

∎

Theorem 2.2
.

Under the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
, the differentials
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
are Möbius invariant on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}.

Proof.

Due to the previous lemma, it remains to check the Möbius invariance of
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
on the Riemann sphere in the case that a charge
\sigma_{\infty}
at infinity is non-zero.
Let

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\sigma_{\infty}\cdot\infty+\sum\sigma_{j}\cdot z_{j}.

We may assume that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}(0)=0
by translation.
For the inversion
\tau(z)=-1/z,
denote
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}:=\boldsymbol{\sigma}\circ\tau^{-1}
and
\widetilde{C}(\boldsymbol{z}):=C_{\mathbb{C}}[\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}].

Then

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\sigma_{\infty}\cdot 0+\sum\sigma_{j}\cdot(-1/z_{j})

and

(2.4)

\widetilde{C}(\boldsymbol{z})=C(\boldsymbol{z})\prod_{j<k}(z_{j}z_{k})^{-\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}}\prod_{j}z_{j}^{-\sigma_{\infty}\sigma_{j}}.

It follows from

C(\boldsymbol{z})=\{C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]\,\|\,\mathrm{id}\}
and
\widetilde{C}(\boldsymbol{z})=\{C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]\,\|\,\tau\}

that the conformal invariance of
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
under
\tau
means

(2.5)

C(\boldsymbol{z})=\widetilde{C}(\boldsymbol{z})\big(h^{\prime}(0)\big)^{\lambda_{\infty}}\prod_{j}\big(\tau^{\prime}(z_{j})\big)^{\lambda_{j}}=\widetilde{C}(\boldsymbol{z})\prod_{j}z_{j}^{-2\lambda_{j}},

where
h=\mathrm{id}
is the transition map between the two charts at infinity.
The equations (
2.4
) – (
2.5
) reduce to the identity

\prod_{j<k}(z_{j}z_{k})^{\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}}\prod_{j}z_{j}^{\sigma_{\infty}\sigma_{j}+2\lambda_{j}}=1.

This holds by the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
because the exponent of
z_{j}
on the left-hand side is

\sigma_{j}^{2}-2\sigma_{j}b+\sigma_{j}\Big(\sigma_{\infty}+\sum_{k\neq j}\sigma_{k}\Big)=0.

Finally, for a dilation-rotation
\tau(z)=az\,(a\neq 0),
we have

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}:=\boldsymbol{\sigma}\circ\tau^{-1}=\sigma_{\infty}\cdot\infty+\sum\sigma_{j}\cdot(az_{j})

and

\widetilde{C}(\boldsymbol{z})=C(\boldsymbol{z})a^{\sum_{j<k}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}}.

We need to check that

C(\boldsymbol{z})=\widetilde{C}(\boldsymbol{z})\big(h^{\prime}(0)\big)^{\lambda_{\infty}}\prod_{j}\big(\tau^{\prime}(z_{j})\big)^{\lambda_{j}}=\widetilde{C}(z)a^{-\lambda_{\infty}+\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}},

where
h(w)=w/a
is the transition map between the two charts at infinity.
(Indeed, the first chart is
z\mapsto-1/z
and the second one is
z\mapsto-1/\tau(z).
)
Thus the condition for conformal invariance is

\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}+\sum_{j<k}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}=\lambda_{\infty}.

The left-hand side simplifies to
\lambda_{b}(\sum_{j}\sigma_{j})
.
It follows from the neutrality condition (
\mathrm{NC}_{b}
) that

\lambda_{b}(\sum_{j}\sigma_{j})=\lambda_{b}(2b-\sigma_{\infty})=\lambda_{b}(\sigma_{\infty})=\lambda_{\infty}.

∎

2.5.
Schottky double construction

In this subsection we introduce Coulomb gas correlations for simply-connected domain
\subsetneq\mathbb{C}.

They are constructed from those of the Schottky double.
We compute them in the
\mathbb{H}
- and
\mathbb{D}
-uniformizations.

Suppose
D
is a simply-connected domain (
D\subsetneq\mathbb{C}
). Let
\partial D
be its Carathéodory “boundary” (prime ends).
Consider the Schottky double

S=D^{\mathrm{double}},

which equips with the canonical involution

\iota\equiv\iota_{D}:S\to S,\,z\mapsto z^{*}.

For example, we identify
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
with the Schottky double of
\mathbb{H}
or that of
\mathbb{D}.

Then the corresponding involution
\iota
is

\iota:z\mapsto z^{*}=\bar{z}
for
D=\mathbb{H}
and
\iota:z\mapsto z^{*}=1/\bar{z}
for
D=\mathbb{D}.

For two divisors
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}=\sum\sigma_{j}^{+}\cdot z_{j},
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}=\sum\sigma_{j}^{-}\cdot z_{j}

(\sigma_{j}^{+},\sigma_{j}^{-}\in\mathbb{C})
in
D\cup\partial D,
we define

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}^{-},\qquad\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}^{-}:=\sum\sigma_{j}^{-}\cdot z_{j}^{*}.

Then
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is a divisor in
S.

(We may assume that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}
is a divisor in
D
, i.e.,
\sigma_{j}^{-}=0
if
z_{j}\in\partial D.
)
We call
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-})
a
double divisor
in
D\cup\partial D.

By definition,

C_{D}^{(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-})}(\boldsymbol{z})(\equiv C_{D}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]):=C_{S}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}].

(We often omit the subscripts
D,S
when there is no danger of confusion.)
More precisely, the above definition means

(2.6)

\{C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]\,\|\,\phi\}=\{C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]\,\|\,(\phi,i\circ\phi\circ\iota)\},

where
i
is the complex conjugation.

Let
\lambda_{j}^{\pm}=\lambda_{b}(\sigma_{j}^{\pm}).

The following theorem is immediate from Theorem
2.2
.

Theorem 2.3
.

If
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}_{*}
satisfies the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}),
then
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}_{*}]
is a well-defined differential with conformal dimensions
[\lambda_{j}^{+},\lambda_{j}^{-}]
at
z_{j}.

\mathbb{H}
-uniformization

Let us consider
D=\mathbb{H}.
Then its prime end is
\partial D=\widehat{\mathbb{R}}.

We may assume that
\sigma_{j}^{-}=0
if
z_{j}\in\widehat{\mathbb{R}}.

We now define

C_{\mathbb{H}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]=\prod_{j<k}(z_{j}-z_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{+}\sigma_{k}^{+}}(\bar{z}_{j}-\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{-}\sigma_{k}^{-}}\prod_{j,k}(z_{j}-\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{+}\sigma_{k}^{-}},

where the product is taken over finite
z_{j}
’s and
z_{k}
’s, and as always we use the convention
0^{0}:=1.

Theorem 2.4
.

Under the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}),

C_{\mathbb{H}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
is the value of the differential
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
in the identity chart of
\mathbb{H}
(and the chart
z\mapsto-1/z
at infinity).

Remark
.

The expression
C_{\mathbb{H}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
of course makes sense without any neutrality condition but we should always reconstruct neutrality by adjusting the charge at infinity.
(Recall that there is a 1-to-1 correspondence between divisors on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying (
\mathrm{NC}_{b}
) and arbitrary divisors in
\mathbb{C}:

\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mapsto\boldsymbol{\sigma}|_{\mathbb{C}}.
)

We leave the proof of the above theorem as a trivial exercise. In the next subsection we state the version of this theorem in the
\mathbb{D}
-uniformization and provide its proof.
For a divisor
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}=\sum\sigma_{j}^{+}\cdot z_{j}
in
D\cup\partial D,
we define
\overline{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}}=\sum\overline{\sigma_{j}^{+}}\cdot z_{j}.

Examples
.

We have

(a) if
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}=\boldsymbol{0},
then

C_{\mathbb{H}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{0}]=\prod_{j<k}(z_{j}-z_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{+}\sigma_{k}^{+}};

(b) if
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}=\overline{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}},
then (up to a phase)

C_{\mathbb{H}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\overline{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}}]=\prod_{j<k}\Big|(z_{j}-z_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{+}\sigma_{k}^{+}}(z_{j}-\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{+}\overline{\sigma_{k}^{+}}}\Big|^{2}\prod_{\mathrm{Im}\,z_{j}>0}(2\,\mathrm{Im}\,z_{j})^{|\sigma_{j}^{+}|^{2}};

(c) if
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}=-\,\overline{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}},
then (up to a phase)

C_{\mathbb{H}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},-\,\overline{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}}]=\prod_{j<k}\Big|(z_{j}-z_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{+}\sigma_{k}^{+}}(z_{j}-\bar{z}_{k})^{-\sigma_{j}^{+}\overline{\sigma_{k}^{+}}}\Big|^{2}\prod_{\mathrm{Im}\,z_{j}>0}(2\,\mathrm{Im}\,z_{j})^{-|\sigma_{j}^{+}|^{2}}.

(The products are taken over finite
z_{j}
’s and
z_{k}
’s.)

\mathbb{D}
-uniformization

In the unit disc
\mathbb{D},
we define

C_{\mathbb{D}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]=\prod_{j<k}(z_{j}-z_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{+}\sigma_{k}^{+}}(\bar{z}_{j}-\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{-}\sigma_{k}^{-}}\prod_{j,k}(1-z_{j}\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{+}\sigma_{k}^{-}}.

Theorem 2.5
.

Under the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}),

C_{\mathbb{D}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
is the value of the differential
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
in the identity chart of
\mathbb{D}.

Proof.

We identify
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
with the Schottky double of
\mathbb{D}.

Then the corresponding involution
\iota
is

\iota:z\mapsto z^{*}=\frac{1}{\bar{z}}.

A double divisor
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-})
corresponds to a divisor
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}};

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}_{*}=\sum(\sigma_{j}^{+}\cdot z_{j}+\sigma_{j}^{-}\cdot z_{j}^{*}).

If one of the
z_{j}
’s is 0, we call it
z_{0}
(so that
z_{0}=0
).
Denote

\widetilde{C}=\{C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{D}}\}.

We need to show that
\widetilde{C}=C_{\mathbb{D}}.

By definition (
2.6
),

\widetilde{C}=\{C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]\,\|\,(\mathrm{id},\bar{\iota})\},

where we use the chart
\bar{\iota}:z\mapsto 1/z
at the nodes
z_{j}^{*}
including
z_{0}^{*}=\infty.

It follows that

C:=C_{\mathbb{C}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]=\widetilde{C}\prod_{j>0}(-\bar{z}_{j})^{2\lambda_{j}^{-}}.

Indeed,
C
is the value of
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
in the identity chart of
\mathbb{C}
and the chart
\bar{\iota}
at infinity, so

C=\widetilde{C}\prod_{j>0}\big(h^{\prime}(z_{j}^{*})\big)^{\lambda_{j}^{-}},

where
h=\bar{\iota}
is the transition map.
Recall the expression for
C_{\mathbb{C}}:

C=\prod_{j<k}(z_{j}-z_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{+}\sigma_{k}^{+}}\prod_{0<j<k}(1/\bar{z}_{j}-1/\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{-}\sigma_{k}^{-}}\prod_{j,k;k\neq 0}(z_{j}-1/\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{+}\sigma_{k}^{-}}.

Let us rewrite this as a fraction
C=N/D
with

N=\prod_{j<k}(z_{j}-z_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{+}\sigma_{k}^{+}}\prod_{0<j<k}(\bar{z}_{j}-\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{-}\sigma_{k}^{-}}\prod_{j,k}(1-z_{j}\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{+}\sigma_{k}^{-}},

and

D=\prod_{0<j<k}(-\bar{z}_{j}\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{-}\sigma_{k}^{-}}\prod_{j,k;k\neq 0}(-\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{+}\sigma_{k}^{-}}.

Comparing
C_{\mathbb{D}}
to
N,
we have

\displaystyle C_{\mathbb{D}}

\displaystyle=N\prod_{j=0,k>0}(\bar{z}_{j}-\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{-}\sigma_{k}^{-}}=N\prod_{k>0}(-\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{0}^{-}\sigma_{k}^{-}}

\displaystyle=CD\prod_{k>0}(-\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{0}^{-}\sigma_{k}^{-}}=\widetilde{C}D\prod_{j>0}(-\bar{z}_{j})^{2\lambda_{j}^{-}}\prod_{k>0}(-\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{0}^{-}\sigma_{k}^{-}}.

To verify
C_{\mathbb{D}}=\widetilde{C},
it remains to show that

\prod_{j>0}(-\bar{z}_{j})^{2\lambda_{j}^{-}}\prod_{j>0}(-\bar{z}_{j})^{\sigma_{0}^{-}\sigma_{j}^{-}}\prod_{0<j<k}(-\bar{z}_{j}\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{-}\sigma_{k}^{-}}\prod_{j,k;k\neq 0}(-\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{+}\sigma_{k}^{-}}=1.

The exponent of
\bar{z}_{j}\,(j\neq 0)
on the left-hand side is

\displaystyle 2\lambda_{j}^{-}+\sigma_{0}^{-}\sigma_{j}^{-}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k\neq j,0}\sigma_{j}^{-}\sigma_{k}^{-}+\sum_{k}\sigma_{k}^{+}\sigma_{j}^{-}=2\lambda_{j}^{-}+\sigma_{j}^{-}\Big(\sum_{k\neq j}\sigma_{k}^{-}+\sum_{k}\sigma_{k}^{+}\Big)

\displaystyle=2\lambda_{j}^{-}+\sigma_{j}^{-}(2b-\sigma_{j}^{-})=2\lambda_{j}^{-}-2\lambda_{b}(\sigma_{j}^{-})=0.

∎

For the exterior
\Delta
of the unit disc, we leave it to the readers to check that the Coulomb gas correlation functions in the
\Delta
-uniformization have the same expression as in the
\mathbb{D}
-uniformization but one must disregard the node at infinity (as in the
\mathbb{H}
-uniformization).

3.
Conformal field theory of Gaussian free field

In this section we review a version of conformal field theory with central charge
c=1
(i.e.,
b=0
) both in a simply-connected domain and on the Riemann sphere implemented in
[
18
,
17
]
and present their connection in the context of Schottky double construction.
Coulomb gas correlations in the case
b=0
are represented as correlations of (formal) multi-vertex fields constructed from the Gaussian free field through Wick’s calculus.
In Sections
4
–
5
, we extend multi-vertex fields to the case
b\neq 0
and interpret them as the OPE exponentials of background charge modifications of bosonic fields.

3.1.
Bosonic field and its Wick’s exponentials

The chiral bosonic fields are described as the holomorphic part or anti-holomorphic part of the Gaussian free field in the physics literature.

•
Gaussian free field.

The Gaussian free field
\Phi
in a planar domain
D
with Dirichlet boundary condition is an isometry

\Phi:\mathcal{E}(D)\to L^{2}(\Omega,\mathbf{P})

from the Dirichlet energy space
\mathcal{E}(D)
such that the image consists of centered Gaussian random variables.
Here
(\Omega,\mathbf{P})
is a probability space and
\mathcal{E}(D)
is the completion of smooth functions with compact supports in
D
with respect to the norm

\|f\|^{2}_{\mathcal{E}}=\iint 2G(\zeta,z)\,f(\zeta)\,\overline{f(z)}~\mathrm{d}A(\zeta)\,\mathrm{d}A(z),

where
A
is the (normalized) area measure and
G\equiv G_{D}
is the Dirichlet Green’s function for
D.

In the upper half-plane, we have

G_{\mathbb{H}}(\zeta,z)=\log\Big|\frac{\zeta-\bar{z}}{\zeta-z}\Big|,

where
G(\zeta,\infty)=0.

In the unit disc, we have

G_{\mathbb{D}}(\zeta,z)=\log\Big|\frac{1-\zeta\bar{z}}{\zeta-z}\Big|.

The same formula holds in the exterior
\Delta
of the unit disc, but
G_{\Delta}(\zeta,\infty)=\log|\zeta|.

The Gaussian free field can be viewed as a Fock space field with the
n
-point correlation function

\mathbf{E}[\Phi(z_{1})\cdots\Phi(z_{n})]=\sum\prod_{k}2G(z_{i_{k}},z_{j_{k}}),

where the sum is over all partitions of the set
\{1,\cdots,n\}
into disjoint pairs
\{i_{k},j_{k}\}.

This correlation function is a unique continuous function on
D^{n}_{\mathrm{distinct}}
such that

\mathbf{E}[\Phi(f_{1})\cdots\Phi(f_{n})]=\int f_{1}(z_{1})\cdots f_{n}(z_{n})~\mathbf{E}[\Phi(z_{1})\cdots\Phi(z_{n})]\,\mathrm{d}A(z_{1})\cdots\mathrm{d}A(z_{n})

for all test functions
f_{j}
with disjoint supports.

•
Chiral bosonic fields.

We write
J:=\partial\Phi
for the current field.
The chiral boson

\Phi^{+}(z,z_{0})=\int_{z_{0}}^{z}J(\zeta)\,\mathrm{d}\zeta

is a well-defined, “multivalued”, path dependent, “generalized” centered Gaussian.
More precisely,

\Phi^{+}(z,z_{0})=\Big\{\Phi^{+}(\gamma)=\int_{\gamma}J(\zeta)\,\mathrm{d}\zeta\Big\},

where
\gamma
is a curve from
z_{0}
to
z.

Then the values of
\Phi^{+}
are multivalued correlation functionals in the complement of the curve.
For example, we have

(3.1)

\mathbf{E}[\Phi^{+}(z,z_{0})\Phi(z_{1})]=2(G^{+}(z,z_{1})-G^{+}(z_{0},z_{1})),

where
G^{+}
is the
complex
Dirichlet Green’s function,

2G^{+}(z,z_{1})=G(z,z_{1})+i\widetilde{G}(z,z_{1}).

Here
\widetilde{G}
is the harmonic conjugate of
G.

The multivalued function
z\mapsto G^{+}(z,z_{1})
is defined up to constants.
Sometimes we work with a uniformization
w:(D,q)\to(\mathbb{D},0).

In such a case, it is convenient to choose the constant so that

G^{+}(z,z_{1})=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{1-w(z)\overline{w(z_{1})}}{w(z)-w(z_{1})}.

We write

\Phi^{-}(z,z_{0})=\overline{\Phi^{+}(z,z_{0})}.

Then we have

\Phi(z)-\Phi(z_{0})=\Phi^{+}(z,z_{0})+\Phi^{-}(z,z_{0}).

•
Wick’s calculus.

For centered jointly Gaussian random variables
\xi_{jk},

(1\leq j\leq l,1\leq k\leq m_{j}),
let
X_{j}=\xi_{j1}\odot\cdots\odot\xi_{jm_{j}}
be Wick’s product of
\xi_{jk},(1\leq k\leq m_{j}).

Then by Wick’s formula we have

(3.2)

X_{1}\cdots X_{l}=\sum_{\gamma}\prod_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}\mathbf{E}[\xi_{v}\xi_{v^{\prime}}]~\underset{v^{\prime\prime}}{\textstyle\bigodot}\xi_{v^{\prime\prime}},

where the sum is taken over all Feynman diagrams labeled by
\xi_{jk}
’s without edges joining any
\xi_{jk_{1}}
and
\xi_{jk_{2}}.

Recall that the Feynman diagram labeled by
\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{n}
is a graph with vertices
1,\cdots,n
such that edges
\{v,v^{\prime}\}
have no common endpoints.
(Such edges
\{v,v^{\prime}\}
are called “Wick’s contractions.”)
We denote the unpaired vertices by
v^{\prime\prime}.

For generalized Gaussians or Fock space fields like
\xi_{jk}=\Phi(z_{jk})
or
\Phi^{+}(\zeta_{jk},z_{jk}),
we set the tensor product
X_{1}\cdots X_{l}
with disjoint sets
S(X_{j})
to be (
3.2
).
For example,

\Phi^{\odot 2}(\zeta)\Phi^{\odot 2}(z)=\Phi^{\odot 2}(\zeta)\odot\Phi^{\odot 2}(z)+4\,\mathbf{E}[\Phi(\zeta)\Phi(z)]\Phi(\zeta)\odot\Phi(z)+2(\mathbf{E}[\Phi(\zeta)\Phi(z)])^{2},

where each of terms on the right hand-side comes from
0,1,
or 2 contractions, respectively.
Wick’s exponentials of (generalized) Gaussian
\xi
is defined by

\mathrm{e}^{\odot\xi}:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\xi^{\odot n}}{n!}.

For centered jointly (generalized) Gaussians
\xi_{j}
’s we have

\mathrm{e}^{\odot\alpha_{1}\xi_{1}}\cdots\mathrm{e}^{\odot\alpha_{n}\xi_{n}}=\mathrm{e}^{\sum_{j<k}\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k}\mathbf{E}[\xi_{j}\xi_{k}]}\mathrm{e}^{\odot\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}\xi_{j}}.

3.2.
Formal bosonic fields

To make the computation easy, it is convenient to consider a representation

\Phi^{+}(z,z_{0})=\Phi^{+}(z)-\Phi^{+}(z_{0})

such that

\Phi=\Phi^{+}+\Phi^{-},\qquad\Phi^{-}=\overline{\Phi^{+}}.

It is not possible to define such
\Phi^{\pm}
in a conformally invariant way, but linear combinations

\Phi[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]:=\sum\sigma_{j}^{+}\Phi^{+}(z_{j})-\sigma_{j}^{-}\Phi^{-}(z_{j})

satisfying the neutrality condition are well-defined as Fock space fields.

Lemma 3.1
.

If a double divisor
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-})
satisfies
(\mathrm{NC}_{0})
, i.e.,

\int\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}=0,

then the formal bosonic field
\Phi[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
can be represented as a linear combination of well-defined Fock space fields.

Proof.

Let us choose any point
z_{0}\in D
. (It can be one of
z_{j}
’s.)
Then

\Phi[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]=\Phi^{+}(z_{0})\int\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}-\Phi^{-}(z_{0})\int\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}+\sum\sigma_{j}^{+}\Phi^{+}(z_{j},z_{0})-\sigma_{j}^{-}\Phi^{-}(z_{j},z_{0}).

Under the neutrality condition, the first two terms on the right-hand side become the Fock space correlation functional:

\Phi^{+}(z_{0})\int\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}-\Phi^{-}(z_{0})\int\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}=\Phi(z_{0})\int\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}.

∎

Remark
.

The representation in the lemma is not unique, of course, but it is “unique” in the sense of “multivalued” fields.
For example,

\Phi[1\cdot z_{1}-1\cdot z_{2},\boldsymbol{0}]=\Phi^{+}(z_{1},z_{0})-\Phi^{+}(z_{2},z_{0})

is “independent” of
z_{0}.

If we specify the curves in the definition of bi-vertex fields with two different choices of
z_{0},
then the difference is an integral over a loop.
If we choose
z_{0}=z_{2},
then
\Phi[1\cdot z_{1}-1\cdot z_{2},\boldsymbol{0}]=\Phi^{+}(z_{1},z_{2})-\Phi^{+}(z_{2},z_{2}),
where
z\mapsto\Phi^{+}(z,z)
is a “monodromy field:”

\Phi^{+}(z,z)=\Big\{\int_{\gamma}J(\zeta)\,\mathrm{d}\zeta\,:\,\gamma\textrm{ is a loop rooted at }z\Big\}.

3.3.
Correlations of formal fields in the unit disc and the upper half-plane

We define
\Phi^{\pm}\equiv\Phi^{\pm}_{\mathbb{D}}

(\Phi^{-}=\overline{\Phi^{+}})
in
\mathbb{D}
as centered Gaussian (formal) fields with “formal” correlations

(3.3)

\mathbf{E}[\Phi^{+}(z_{1})\Phi^{+}(z_{2})]=\log\frac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}},\qquad\mathbf{E}[\Phi^{+}(z_{1})\Phi^{-}(z_{2})]=\log(1-z_{1}\bar{z}_{2}).

Note that these formal correlations have no
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D})
-invariance and depend on the order of particles.
It is easy to verify that

\mathbf{E}[\Phi(z_{1})\Phi(z_{2})]=\mathbf{E}[\Phi^{+}(z_{1})+\Phi^{-}(z_{1})][\Phi^{+}(z_{2})+\Phi^{-}(z_{2})],

where
\mathbf{E}
on the left-hand side has the usual meaning, but we use “formal” correlations on the right-hand side.
The same interpretation is applied for
\mathbf{E}[\Phi^{+}(z_{1},z_{2})\Phi^{+}(z_{3},z_{4})]
, etc.
If a double divisor
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-})
in
D
satisfies the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}),
then we can compute correlations of
\Phi[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
(or other functionals involving
\Phi[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}],
e.g., Wick’s exponentials of
\Phi[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
) with various Fock space fields by applying Wick’s calculus to our formal fields in
\mathbb{D}.

In the case of an arbitrary simply-connected domain
D,
we fix a conformal map
w:D\to\mathbb{D}
and define
\Phi^{\pm}(z)=\Phi^{\pm}_{\mathbb{D}}(w(z))
so the correlations are

\mathbf{E}[\Phi^{+}(z_{1})\Phi^{+}(z_{2})]=\log\frac{1}{w(z_{1})-w(z_{2})},\quad\mathbf{E}[\Phi^{+}(z_{1})\Phi^{-}(z_{2})]=\log(1-w(z_{1})\overline{w(z_{2})}).

The “formal” correlations depend on the choice of the conformal map but this dependence disappears under the neutrality condition.
In particular, we can use this method to introduce formal bosonic fields in
\mathbb{H}
but it is more convenient to define
\Phi^{\pm}\equiv\Phi^{\pm}_{\mathbb{H}}
in
\mathbb{H}
as follows:

\mathbf{E}[\Phi^{+}(z_{1})\Phi^{+}(z_{2})]=\log\frac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}},\qquad\mathbf{E}[\Phi^{+}(z_{1})\Phi^{-}(z_{2})]=\log(z_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})

for finite
z_{j}
’s and set
\Phi^{+}(\infty)=0.

3.4.
OPE exponentials and multi-vertex fields

The OPE product
X*Y
of two fields
X
and
Y
is a generic notation for a coefficient in the operator product expansion
X(\zeta)Y(z)
as
\zeta\to z,
i.e., expansion with respect to a chart independent asymptotic scale.
Typically (but not always) we use
*
for the coefficient of the first non-diverging term.
So, the OPE product
X*Y
of non-chiral fields is obtained by subtracting all divergent terms in the operator product expansion
X(\zeta)Y(z)
and then taking the limit as
\zeta\to z.

This is the case in notation
\Phi^{*2}:=\Phi*\Phi,\Phi^{*n}=\Phi*\Phi^{*n-1}
in the definition of OPE powers
of
\Phi.

Let
c(z),z\in D
denote the logarithm of conformal radius of
D,
i.e.,

c(z)=u(z,z),\quad u(\zeta,z):=G_{D}(\zeta,z)+\log|\zeta-z|.

Then by Wick’s calculus,

\Phi(\zeta)\Phi(z)=\log\frac{1}{|\zeta-z|^{2}}+\Phi^{*2}(z)+o(1),\quad\Phi^{*2}=2c+\Phi^{\odot 2}

as
\zeta\to z,\zeta\neq z.

In general, unlike Wick’s multiplication, the OPE multiplication is neither commutative nor associative.
For example, if
f
is a non-random holomorphic function and if
X,Y
are holomorphic fields, then

f*X=X*f=fX,
and
(fX)*Y\neq X*(fY).

We define OPE exponentials of the Gaussian free field by

\mathcal{V}^{(\sigma)}:=\mathrm{e}^{*i\sigma\Phi}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(i\sigma)^{n}}{n!}\,\Phi^{*n}.

Then we have

\mathcal{V}^{(\sigma)}(z)=C(z)^{-\sigma^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\sigma\Phi(z)},\qquad C(z)=\mathrm{e}^{c(z)},

see
[
18
, Proposition 3.3]
.
More generally, OPE exponentials (non-chiral multi-vertex fields)
\mathcal{V}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
of

i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\sigma},-\boldsymbol{\sigma}]=\sum i\sigma_{j}\Phi(z_{j})
can be defined in a similar way

\mathcal{V}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]:=\mathrm{e}^{*i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\sigma},-\boldsymbol{\sigma}]}

and be computed as

\mathcal{V}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]=\mathcal{C}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\sigma},-\boldsymbol{\sigma}]},

where
\mathcal{C}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]=C[\boldsymbol{\sigma},-\boldsymbol{\sigma}].

See
[
17
, Section 5]
for more details.

•
Multi-vertex fields.

We now extend the OPE exponentials or multi-vertex fields to chiral fields by stating the definition of the chiral multi-vertex field in terms of Coulomb gas correlation function and Wick’s exponential.
Suppose a double divisor
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-})
in
D
satisfies the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}).

Then the Wick’s exponential

V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]:=\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]}\equiv\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\sum\sigma_{j}^{+}\Phi^{+}(z_{j})-\sigma_{j}^{-}\Phi^{-}(z_{j})}

is a well-defined Fock space functional;
V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
is a scalar, i.e.,
(0,0)
-differential and

\mathbf{E}\,V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]=1.

We define the multi-vertex field
V[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
by

V[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]=C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}].

Thus a multi-vertex field consists of two parts – its expectation given by
the correlation differential
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}],
and the Wick exponential
V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}].

The conformal dimensions of
V[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
at
z_{j}
’s are

\lambda_{j}^{+}=\frac{(\sigma_{j}^{+})^{2}}{2},\qquad\lambda_{j}^{-}=\frac{(\sigma_{j}^{-})^{2}}{2}.

(Recall that
b=0
in this section.)

•
Formal representation of multi-vertex fields.

For a given conformal map
w:D\to\mathbb{D},
we formally set

V^{\sigma}(z)=(w^{\prime}(z))^{\sigma^{2}/2}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\sigma\Phi^{+}(z)},\qquad\bar{V}^{\sigma}(z)=(\overline{w^{\prime}(z)})^{\sigma^{2}/2}\mathrm{e}^{\odot-i\sigma\Phi^{-}(z)}.

Then under the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}),

\mathbf{E}\,V[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]=\mathbf{E}\,V^{\sigma_{1}^{+}}(z_{1})\bar{V}^{\sigma_{1}^{-}}(z_{1})\cdots V^{\sigma_{n}^{+}}(z_{n})\bar{V}^{\sigma_{n}^{-}}(z_{n}),

where the left-hand side is
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
by the definition above and the right-hand side is computed by Wick’s calculus from formal correlations in
\mathbb{D}.

The result does not depend on the choice of the conformal map
w,
and is the same if we use formal correlations in
\mathbb{H}
or
\Delta.

3.5.
Conformal field theory on the Riemann sphere

In this subsection we define the Gaussian free field on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
both as a Gaussian field indexed by the energy space and as a bi-variant Fock space field.
After introducing formal bosonic fields, we define multi-vertex fields on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}.

•
Energy space.

For a compact Riemann surface
S
of genus zero, we denote

\mathcal{W}(S)\equiv\mathcal{E}^{(0)}(S)=W^{1,2}(S)/\mathbb{C}

with the scalar product

(f,g)_{\nabla}=\int_{S}\mathrm{d}f\wedge\overline{\mathrm{d}g}.

The
Dirichlet energy space
,
\mathcal{E}(S)=\mathcal{E}^{(1,1)}(S)
is the Hilbert space defined as the completion of the smooth
(1,1)
forms with respect to the scalar product such that the Laplacian operator

\partial{\bar{\partial}}:\,\mathcal{W}(S)\to\mathcal{E}(S)

is unitary.
Note that if
\mu\in\mathcal{E}(S)
, then by Green’s theorem
\mu
satisfies the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}):

(3.4)

\int\mu=0.

Furthermore,
\|\mu\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}
is represented as

\|\mu\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}=\iint_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}}\log\frac{1}{|z-w|^{2}}\,\mu(z)\overline{\mu(w)}

for
any

\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
-uniformization of
S.

The
\mathrm{Aut}(\widehat{\mathbb{C}})
-invariance of the right-hand side means that

\iint_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}}\log\frac{1}{|\tau z-\tau w|^{2}}\,\mu(z)\overline{\mu(w)}

is independent of
\tau\in\mathrm{Aut}(\widehat{\mathbb{C}}).

Since translation invariance is trivial, it suffices to show this for
\tau(z)=az\,(a\neq 0)
and
\tau(z)=1/z.

It follows from the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0})
on
\mu.

•
Gaussian free field.

The Gaussian free field
\Psi
on
S
is a (real, centered) Gaussian field indexed by
\mathcal{E}(S)
, i.e.,

\Psi:\,\mathcal{E}(S)\to L^{2}(\Omega,\mathbf{P})

is an isometry such that
\Psi(\mu)
is a (real, centered) Gaussian random variable for each
\mu\in\mathcal{E}(S).

We introduce the Fock space functionals (“generalized” Gaussians)

\Psi(z,z_{0})=\Psi(\delta_{z}-\delta_{z_{0}}).

(Note that
\mu=\delta_{z}-\delta_{z_{0}}
is not in
\mathcal{E}(S)
but as a signed/complex measure it can be approximated by
\mu_{n}
’s in
\mathcal{E}
because it satisfies
(\mathrm{NC}_{0});
here
\delta
’s are
\delta
-
functions
, i.e.,
(1,1)
forms.)
Then the Fock space functionals
\Psi(z,z_{0})
have the following properties:

(a)

\Psi(z,z_{0})=-\Psi(z_{0},z),
in particular
\Psi(z,z)=0;

(b)

\Psi(z_{1},z_{3})=\Psi(z_{1},z_{2})+\Psi(z_{2},z_{3}).

For
\mu\in\mathcal{E}(S),
somewhat symbolically we have

\Psi(\mu)=\int\Psi(z,z_{0})\,\mu(z).

The integral is independent of the reference point
z_{0}
by (b) and the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}).

Thus we can think of the Gaussian free field as a bi-variant field with

\mathbf{E}\,\Psi(z,z_{0})\,\Psi(z^{\prime},z_{0}^{\prime})=2\log\bigg|\frac{(z-z_{0}^{\prime})(z_{0}-z^{\prime})}{(z-z^{\prime})(z_{0}-z_{0}^{\prime})}\bigg|

on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}.

As a scalar, conformal invariance is manifest in the cross-ratio on the right-hand side.
As in a simply-connected domain, we define the chiral boson
\Psi^{+}
as a bi-variant multivalued field by

\Psi^{+}(z,z_{0})=\int_{z_{0}}^{z}\partial_{\zeta}\Psi(\zeta,z_{1})\,\mathrm{d}\zeta.

It is obvious that
\Psi^{+}(z,z_{0})
does not depend on
z_{1}.

On
\widehat{\mathbb{C}},
we have

\mathbf{E}\,\Psi^{+}(z,z_{0})\Psi^{+}(z^{\prime},z_{0}^{\prime})=\log\frac{(z-z_{0}^{\prime})(z_{0}-z^{\prime})}{(z-z^{\prime})(z_{0}-z_{0}^{\prime})}.

•
Formal 1-point field.

We introduce
\Psi
on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
as a centered Gaussian formal field with formal correlation
