\mathbf{E}\,\Psi(z_{1})\,\Psi(z_{2})=2\log\frac{1}{|z_{1}-z_{2}|}.

For a compact Riemann surface
S
of genus zero, we fix a uniformization
w:S\to\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
and define
\Psi(z)=\Psi_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}(w(z))
so that

\mathbf{E}\,\Psi(z_{1})\,\Psi(z_{2})=2\log\frac{1}{|w(z_{1})-w(z_{2})|}.

The dependence of the formal correlations on the choice of
w
disappears if we apply this formalism only to the linear combinations satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}):

\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}\Psi(z_{j}),\qquad\sum\sigma_{j}=0.

For example, we have a representation

\Psi(z,z_{0})=\Psi(z)-\Psi(z_{0}).

Next we introduce the formal bosonic fields
\Psi^{\pm}\equiv\Psi^{\pm}_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}
on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
as centered Gaussian formal fields satisfying
\Psi=\Psi^{+}+\Psi^{-},\Psi^{-}=\overline{\Psi^{+}}
and

\mathbf{E}\,\Psi^{+}(z_{1})\,\Psi^{+}(z_{2})=\log\frac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}},\qquad\mathbf{E}\,\Psi^{+}(z_{1})\,\Psi^{-}(z_{2})=0.

For a given uniformizing map
w:S\to\widehat{\mathbb{C}},
we define
\Psi^{\pm}(z)\equiv\Psi^{\pm}_{S}(z):=\Psi^{\pm}_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}(w(z)).

If both
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}=\sum\sigma^{+}_{j}\cdot z_{j}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}=\sum\sigma^{-}_{j}\cdot z_{j}
satisfy the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}),
then
the linear combinations

\Psi^{+}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}]:=\sum\sigma^{+}_{j}\Psi^{+}(z_{j}),\qquad\Psi^{-}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]:=\sum\sigma^{-}_{j}\Psi^{-}(z_{j}),

and

\Psi[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]:=\Psi^{+}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}]-\Psi^{-}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]=\sum\sigma^{+}_{j}\Psi^{+}(z_{j})-\sigma^{-}_{j}\Psi^{-}(z_{j})

are well-defined Fock space fields.
For a divisor
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}=\sum\sigma^{-}_{j}\cdot z_{j}
satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}),
we have

\Psi^{-}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]=\overline{\Psi^{+}[\overline{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}}]},

where
\overline{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\sum\overline{\sigma_{j}}\cdot z_{j}
for
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\sum\sigma_{j}\cdot z_{j}.

•
Vertex fields.

Suppose that both
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}
satisfy the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}).

We define the multi-vertex field
V[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
by

V[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]=C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}],\qquad V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]:=\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Psi[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]},

where the Coulomb gas correlation function
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]\equiv C_{S}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
is given by

C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]=C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}]\,\overline{C[\overline{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}}]}.

We remark that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}
does not interact with
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}
in
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
due to the independence of
\Psi^{+}
and
\Psi^{-}
:

\mathbf{E}\,\Psi^{+}(z_{1})\,\Psi^{-}(z_{2})=0.

In the identity chart of
\mathbb{C}
and the chart
z\mapsto-1/z
at infinity, the value of the differential
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
is

\prod_{j<k}(z_{j}-z_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{+}\sigma_{k}^{+}}(\bar{z}_{j}-\bar{z}_{k})^{\sigma_{j}^{-}\sigma_{k}^{-}},

where the product is taken over finite
z_{j}
’s and
z_{k}
’s, and as usual
0^{0}:=1.

Remark
.

Recall that we may assume that
\sigma_{j}^{-}=0
if
z_{j}\in\partial D.

There is a 1-1 correspondence between a divisor
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
on
S=D^{\mathrm{double}}
and a double divisor
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-})
with
\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}\subseteq D\cap\partial D,

\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}\subseteq D:

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}^{-}.

Sometimes it is convenient to write
\Phi[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
for
\Phi[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}],

C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
for
C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}],
and
V[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
for
V[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}],
etc.

4.
Modified multi-vertex fields

In this section we extend the concept of multi-vertex fields to the case
b\neq 0.

Their correlation functions are defined in terms of correlation functions with the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
and their Wick’s parts are Wick’s exponentials of bosonic fields with charges
\boldsymbol{\tau}
satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}).

To reconcile these two neutrality conditions, background charges
\boldsymbol{\beta}
with the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
are placed.
In the next section we view the modified multi-vertex fields
\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]
as the OPE exponentials of background charge modification of
i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\tau}].

4.1.
Definition

Let us fix the (background charge) parameter
b\in\mathbb{R}.

This parameter
b
is related to the central charge
c
in the following way:

c=1-12b^{2}.

As we mentioned in the last remark in the previous section, we often use the 1-1 correspondence between a divisor
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
on
S=D^{\mathrm{double}}
and a double divisor
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-})
with
\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}\subseteq D\cap\partial D,

\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}\subseteq D:

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}^{-}.

Recall that the Coulomb gas correlation functions

C[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]\equiv C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]

are well-defined
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{+},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{-}]
-differentials under the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}):

\int\boldsymbol{\sigma}=2b,

see Theorem
2.3
, and that Wick’s exponentials
V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]\equiv V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]=\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]}

(\boldsymbol{\tau}=\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{*}^{-})
are well-defined Fock space fields under the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}):

\int\boldsymbol{\tau}=0,

where
\Phi[\boldsymbol{\tau}]\equiv\Phi[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]=\sum\tau_{j}^{+}\Phi^{+}(z_{j})-\tau_{j}^{-}\Phi^{-}(z_{j}),
see Subsection
3.4
.

To reconcile these two neutrality conditions
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
on
C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
and
(\mathrm{NC}_{0})
on
V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}],

we need at least one marked point in
D\cup\partial D
to place the “background charge” there.
Let us consider the case of one marked point and denote by
q
this marked point.
It can be one of the nodes of
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}).

The case
q\in D
is called
radial
, and the case
q\in\partial D

chordal
.

•
Standard chordal case.
For a divisor
\boldsymbol{\sigma}(=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}^{-})
on
S
satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
we define
V[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]\equiv V[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
by

V[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]=C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]~V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}-2b\cdot q].

Sometimes we write
V_{q}
or
V_{(b,q)}
to indicate the location of the marked point and/or the background charge.
Let us emphasize that the expectation of the multi-vertex (the correlation
C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
) does not depend on
q
but the Wick part does.
Multi-vertex functionals/fields are differentials with the same conformal dimensions as in
C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}].

•
Standard radial case.

For a divisor
\boldsymbol{\sigma}(=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}^{-})
on
S
satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
we define
V[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]\equiv V[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
by

V[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]=C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]~V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}-b\cdot q-b\cdot q^{*}].

Again,
V[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
is
\mathrm{Aut}(D,q)
-invariant but its expectation
C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]
is
\mathrm{Aut}(D)
-invariant.
(This is not the only way to satisfy neutrality condition in Wick’s part of the functional.)

Both cases can be generalized to several marked points.
Additional marked points
q_{k}
are not necessarily on the boundary
\partial D.

Suppose we have a background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}(=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}^{-})
on
S
with the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}).

For a divisor
\boldsymbol{\sigma}(=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}^{-})
on
S
satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
we define
V_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]\equiv V_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]
by

V_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]=C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}]~V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{\beta}].

4.2.
Background charge operators

Let us introduce the background charge operators.
For a background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}
on
S,
we define the background charge operator
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
associated with
\boldsymbol{\beta}
by

\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:=V_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]=C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}].

Note that the Wick part of
V[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
is the constant field
1.

•
Standard chordal case.

We write
\mathcal{P}_{q}
for
\mathcal{P}_{2b\cdot q}=C_{(b)}[2b\cdot q].

Then we would have
\mathcal{P}_{q}=1
in all charts because

\lambda_{q}^{+}=\lambda_{b}(2b)=0

and clearly
\mathcal{P}_{q}=1
in the
(\mathbb{H},\infty)
-uniformization.

•
Standard radial case.

We write
\mathcal{P}_{q}
for
\mathcal{P}_{b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*}}=C_{(b)}[b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*}].

This is a non-random differential with dimensions

\lambda_{q}^{+}=\lambda_{q}^{-}=\lambda_{b}(b)=-\frac{b^{2}}{2}

at
q.

Note that
\mathcal{P}_{q}=1
in the
(\mathbb{D},0)
-uniformization.

4.3.
OPE exponentials

We use the background charge operators to modify the multi-vertex fields so that the OPE calculus of the modified multi-vertex fields has a very simple and natural form.
We call the modified multi-vertex fields the OPE exponentials; the reason for this terminology becomes apparent in the next section.

Suppose
\boldsymbol{\tau}(=\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{*}^{-})
is a divisor on
S
satisfying the neutrality
condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}).

For this divisor
\boldsymbol{\tau}
and a background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}(=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}^{-})
on
S
(satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
), we define the OPE exponentials (the modified multi-vertex fields)
\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]\equiv\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]
by

\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]:=\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}V_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}+\boldsymbol{\beta}]=\frac{C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\tau}+\boldsymbol{\beta}]}{C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]}~V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\tau}].

We remark that all charges in the correlation

\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=\frac{C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\tau}+\boldsymbol{\beta}]}{C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]}

interact except that background charges do not interact with each other.

•
Standard radial case.

If
\boldsymbol{\beta}=b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*},
then

\mathcal{O}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]\equiv\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=\frac{C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\tau}+b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*}]}{C_{(b)}[b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*}]}~V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\tau}].

We call
(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-})
the (double) divisor of Wick’s exponents and
(\tau_{q}^{+}+b,\tau_{q}^{-}+b)
the
effective
charges at
q.

Thus the sum of exponents is zero but the sum of (effective) charges is
2b,
i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\tau}
satisfies
(\mathrm{NC}_{0})
and
\boldsymbol{\tau}+\boldsymbol{\beta}
satisfies
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}).

Sometimes we omit the subscript
\boldsymbol{\beta}
in
\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]
when
\boldsymbol{\beta}=b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*}.

Proposition 4.1
.

The conformal dimensions of
\,\mathcal{O}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]
at
q
are

h_{q}^{\pm}=\frac{(\tau_{q}^{\pm})^{2}}{2}.

Proof.

The Coulomb gas correlations
C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+}+b\cdot q,\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}+b\cdot q],C_{(b)}[b\cdot q,b\cdot q]
have conformal dimensions
[\lambda_{b}(\tau_{q}^{+}+b),\lambda_{b}(\tau_{q}^{-}+b)],

[\lambda_{b}(b),\lambda_{b}(b)],
respectively at
q.

Since Wick’s exponentials
V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]
are scalars or
(0,0)
-differentials, we have

h_{q}^{\pm}=\lambda_{b}(\tau_{q}^{\pm}+b)-\lambda_{b}(b)=\frac{(\tau_{q}^{\pm})^{2}}{2}.

∎

We call the numbers
\lambda_{b}(\tau_{q}^{\pm}+b)

effective
conformal dimensions at
q
; they are dimensions of
\mathcal{P}_{q}\,\mathcal{O}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=V[\boldsymbol{\tau}+\boldsymbol{\beta}].

As we have just seen,

\lambda_{b}(\tau_{q}^{\pm}+b)=h_{q}^{\pm}-\frac{b^{2}}{2}.

Proposition 4.2
.

In the
(\mathbb{D},0)
-uniformization, we have

\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{O}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=\prod_{j}(z_{j})^{\nu_{j}^{+}}(\bar{z}_{j})^{\nu_{j}^{-}}\prod_{j<k}(z_{j}-z_{k})^{\tau_{j}^{+}\tau_{k}^{+}}(\bar{z}_{j}-\bar{z}_{k})^{\tau_{j}^{-}\tau_{k}^{-}}\prod_{j,k}(1-z_{j}\bar{z}_{k})^{\tau_{j}^{+}\tau_{k}^{-}},

where
\nu_{j}^{\pm}=\tau_{j}^{\pm}(\tau_{q}^{\pm}+b)
and
z_{j}
’s are non-zero nodes.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{P}_{q}=1
in the
(\mathbb{D},0)
-uniformization and
\mathbf{E}\,V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=1,

we have

\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{O}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\tau}+b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*}].

Proposition now follows from Theorem
2.5
.
We have
\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{O}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=C_{\mathbb{D}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+}+b\cdot q,\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}+b\cdot q]
in the identity chart of
\mathbb{D}.

∎

In other words,
\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{O}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]
in
(\mathbb{D},0)
is given by the usual Coulomb gas correlation function for effective charges.

•
Standard chordal case.

If
\boldsymbol{\beta}=2b\cdot q,
then

\mathcal{O}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]\equiv\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]:=V[\boldsymbol{\tau}+2b\cdot q]=C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\tau}+2b\cdot q]\,V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\tau}].

Recall that
\mathcal{P}_{q}=1
in all charts.
The (effective) charge at
q
is
\tau_{q}^{+}+2b,
so the sum of (effective) charges is
2b.

Sometimes we omit subscript
\boldsymbol{\beta}
in
\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]
when
\boldsymbol{\beta}=2b\cdot q.

The conformal dimension of
\mathcal{O}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]
at
q
is

h_{q}^{+}=\lambda_{b}(\tau_{q}^{+}+2b)=\lambda_{b}(-\tau_{q}^{+})=\frac{(\tau_{q}^{+})^{2}}{2}+\tau_{q}^{+}b.

Remark
.

In the standard chordal case,
there is a 1-to-1 correspondence between the double divisors
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-})
in
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\setminus\{q\}
and the double divisors
(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-})
in
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
satisfying
(\mathrm{NC}_{0})
with
\tau_{q}^{-}=0:

\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}=\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+}-\tau_{q}^{+}\cdot q,\quad\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}=\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-},\quad\tau_{q}^{+}=-\int(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}).

In
[
18
]
we use the notation

\Sigma=\int(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}),

so
\Sigma=-\tau_{q}^{+}
(by
(\mathrm{NC}_{0})
) and
h_{q}=\lambda_{b}(\Sigma).

Also, in
[
18
]
we use the notation

\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma_{1}^{+},\sigma_{1}^{-})}(z_{1})\star\cdots\star\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma_{n}^{+},\sigma_{n}^{-})}(z_{n})

(which can be shortened to
\mathcal{O}^{(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-})}
) instead of our present notation
\mathcal{O}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]
for the OPE exponentials.

4.4.
Example: one-leg operators

As a special case of Theorem
1.5
, under the insertion of Wick’s part
\Lambda_{p}/\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{p}
of the radial one-leg operator
\Lambda_{p}
(rooted at
q
) with
p\in\partial D
all correlation functions of the fields in the extended OPE family of
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
(
\boldsymbol{\beta}=b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*}
) are radial
\mathrm{SLE}(\kappa)
martingale-observables (see Subsection
5.4
for the modified Gaussian free
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
and Subsection
6.2
for its extended OPE family) if the parameters
a
(the charge of
\Lambda_{p}
at
p
) and
b
are related to the SLE parameter
\kappa
as

a=\pm\sqrt{2/\kappa},\qquad b=a(\kappa/4-1).

(a) We define the
radial one-leg operator

\Lambda
(rooted at
q
) by

\Lambda_{z}\equiv\Lambda(z):=\mathcal{O}\big[a\cdot z-\frac{a}{2}\cdot q-\frac{a}{2}\cdot q^{*}\big].

Its conformal dimensions
\lambda_{z}
at
z
and
h_{q}^{\pm}
at
q
are

(4.1)

\lambda_{z}=h\equiv h_{1,2}:=\frac{a^{2}}{2}-ab=\frac{6-\kappa}{2\kappa}

and

h_{q}^{+}=h_{q}^{-}=\frac{a^{2}}{8},\qquad H_{q}(:=h_{q}^{+}+h_{q}^{-})=\frac{a^{2}}{4}.

The effective charges at
q
are
(b-a/2,b-a/2)
and the effective dimensions at
q
are

(4.2)

H_{q}^{\mathrm{eff}}(\Lambda)=2h_{0,1/2},\quad h_{0,1/2}:=\frac{a^{2}}{8}-\frac{b^{2}}{2}=\frac{(\kappa-2)(6-\kappa)}{16\kappa}.

Indeed,
H_{q}^{\mathrm{eff}}(\Lambda)=H_{q}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}),
where
\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}
is the “effective” one-leg operator

\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}=\mathcal{P}_{q}\,\Lambda=V\big[a\cdot z+\big(b-\frac{a}{2}\big)\cdot q+\big(b-\frac{a}{2}\big)\cdot q^{*}\big].

In
(\mathbb{D},0)
we have

\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda(z)=\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}(z)=z^{a(b-a/2)}=z^{-h},

so in
(D,q)

\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda(z)=\Big(\frac{w^{\prime}(z)}{w(z)}\Big)^{h}|w_{q}^{\prime}|^{H_{q}},

where
w:(D,q)\to(\mathbb{D},0)
is a conformal map and
w_{q}^{\prime}=w^{\prime}(q).

(b) Let
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
be the parameter in the definition of radial
\mathrm{SLE}_{\eta}(\kappa,\boldsymbol{\rho}),
see (
1.8
).
We define the
radial one-leg operator

\Lambda_{(s)}
with spin
s=i\eta a^{2}/2
by

\Lambda_{(s)}(z):=\mathcal{O}\big[a\cdot z-\frac{a+i\delta}{2}\cdot q-\frac{a-i\delta}{2}\cdot q^{*}\big],

where
\delta=\eta a.

Its conformal dimensions
h_{q}^{\pm}
at
q
are

h_{q}^{\pm}=\frac{(a\pm i\delta)^{2}}{8},

so the spin
s:=h_{q}^{+}-h_{q}^{-}
and the conformal dimension
H_{q}:=h_{q}^{+}+h_{q}^{-}
at
q
are

s=\frac{ia\delta}{2},\qquad H_{q}=\frac{a^{2}+\delta^{2}}{4}.

In
(\mathbb{D},0)
we have

\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{(s)}(z)=\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{(s)}^{\mathrm{eff}}(z)=z^{a(b-a/2-i\delta/2)}=z^{-h-s},
and

\kappa\partial_{\theta}\log\frac{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{(s)}^{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})}{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})}=\eta.

See (
1.7
) and (
1.8
).
Later we define the partition function in terms of the correlation function of the effective one-leg operator.

(c) We define the
chordal one-leg operator

\Lambda
by

\Lambda(z):=\mathcal{O}\big[a\cdot z-a\cdot q].

Its conformal dimension
\lambda_{z}
at
z
and effective dimension
h_{q}^{\mathrm{eff}}
at
q
are

\lambda_{z}=h,\qquad h_{q}^{\mathrm{eff}}=\lambda_{b}(2b-a)=\lambda_{b}(a)=h.

In
(\mathbb{H},\infty)
we have

\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}(z)=1,

so

\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}(z)=(w^{\prime})^{h}(w_{q}^{\prime})^{h}
in
(D,q).

4.5.
Algebra of multi-vertex functionals and OPE exponentials

In the general case, given a background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}
we define the multiplication of OPE exponentials by

\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}]\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}]=\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}].

In the next section we relate this operation to OPE/tensor multiplication.
Multiplication of OPE exponentials is commutative and associative (if we ignore the order of particles).
Formally, we have the following representation

\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]=\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\tau_{1}^{+})}(z_{1})\overline{\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\bar{\tau}_{1}^{-})}(z_{1})}\cdots\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\tau_{n}^{+})}(z_{n})\overline{\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\bar{\tau}_{n}^{-})}(z_{n})},

where
\bar{\tau}_{j}^{-}=\overline{\tau_{j}^{-}}.

5.
Background charge modifications of Gaussian free field

In this section we discuss background charge modifications of the Gaussian free field in a simply-connected domain
D
with marked boundary/interior points.
A special type of such modifications with a marked boundary point appeared in
[
29
]
and
[
18
]
to present their connections to chordal SLE theory.
Similar constructions had been well known both in the physics literature and in the algebraic literature.
For example, see
[
11
, Chapter 9]
for Coulomb gas formalism, and
[
16
]
for Fairlie’s modifications of Virasoro generators.

The background charge modifications of the radial conformal field theory with a marked interior point
q
under our consideration equip the Gaussian free field with the additive monodromy around the puncture
q\in D.

We define the OPE exponentials of modified bosonic fields with nodes in
D^{*}=D\setminus\{q\}
as differentials in the OPE family.
The OPE exponentials extend to the puncture through operator product expansion with the constant field
1_{q}
or the rooting procedure.
We discuss these extensions in the next section.

5.1.
Modification of Gaussian free field on the Riemann sphere

In this subsection we borrow the concepts of the background charge modifications of Gaussian free field on a compact Riemann surface from
[
17
]
. Later we adapt them in a simply-connected domain employing Schottky double construction.

Recall that a non-random field
\psi
is called a
pre-pre-Schwarzian form
of order
(\mu,\nu)
(or
\mathrm{PPS}(\mu,\nu)
) if the transformation law is

\psi=\tilde{\psi}\circ h+\mu\log h^{\prime}+\nu\log\overline{h^{\prime}},

where
\psi=(\psi\,\|\,\phi)
in a chart
\phi,

\tilde{\psi}=(\psi\,\|\,\tilde{\phi})
in a chart
\tilde{\phi},
and
h
is the transition map between two overlapping charts
\phi,\tilde{\phi}.

We consider a holomorphic/harmonic PPS form
\psi
such that
\partial\psi
is meromorphic and
\boldsymbol{\beta}\equiv\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\psi}:=i/\pi{\bar{\partial}}\partial\psi
is a finite linear combination of
\delta
-measures,
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\sum_{k}\beta_{k}\delta_{q_{k}}.

Such
\psi
is called a
simple
PPS form.
We often write
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\psi}=\sum_{k}\beta_{k}\cdot q_{k}
as a divisor and call it the
background charge
of
\psi.

Proposition 5.1
.

On a compact Riemann surface
S
of genus zero, we have the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
for the background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\psi}
of a simple
\mathrm{PPS}(ib,0)
form
\psi:

(5.1)

\sum_{k}\beta_{k}=2b.

It is a consequence of a version of Gauss-Bonnet theorem, e.g., see
[
17
, Corollary 6.3]
.
For the reader’s convenience, we present its proof.

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Let us choose a conformal metric
\rho=|\omega_{q}|^{2}
(a positive
(1,1)
-differential) on
S,
where
\omega_{q}
is a meromorphic differential with a sole double pole at
q\in S.

In the identity chart of
\mathbb{C}
with
q=0,
one can take
\rho(z)=|z|^{-4},
so

{\bar{\partial}}\partial\log\rho=-2\pi\delta_{0}

and

\int_{S}{\bar{\partial}}\partial\log\rho=-2\pi=-\pi\chi(S),

where
\chi(S)
is the Euler characteristic of
S.

Let
\varphi=\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}/(ib).

Then
\varphi
is a simple
\mathrm{PPS}(1,0)
form.
We now consider the harmonic
\mathrm{PPS}(1,1)
form,
\varphi_{*}=\log\rho=\log|\omega_{0}|^{2}.

Proposition now follows from

(5.2)

\int_{S}{\bar{\partial}}\partial(\varphi-\varphi_{*})=0

since

\int\boldsymbol{\beta}=\frac{i}{\pi}\int{\bar{\partial}}\partial\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\frac{i}{\pi}ib\int{\bar{\partial}}\partial\varphi=\frac{i}{\pi}ib\int{\bar{\partial}}\partial\varphi_{*}=2b.

By means of Green’s theorem, the integral in (
5.2
) is the sum of all residues of the meromorphic differential
\partial(\varphi-\varphi_{*})/(2i).

It is well known that such a sum vanishes.
∎

Given a background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\sum_{k}\beta_{k}\cdot q_{k}
with the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
, there is a unique (up to an additive constant) simple
\mathrm{PPS}(ib,0)
form
\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}
with the background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta},

\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}=i\sum_{k}\beta_{k}\psi_{q_{k}}^{+},

where

\psi_{q}^{+}=\frac{1}{2}\log\omega_{q}
and
\omega_{q}
is a meromorphic differential with a sole double pole at
q\in S.

On the Riemann sphere
\widehat{\mathbb{C}},

\omega_{\infty}=1
and
\omega_{q}=1/(z-q)^{2}
in the identity chart of
\mathbb{C}.

In terms of a uniformization
w:S\to\widehat{\mathbb{C}},

\psi_{q_{k}}^{+}(z)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\log w^{\prime}(z)-\log(w(z)-w(q_{k})),\qquad&w(q_{k})\neq\infty\\
\frac{1}{2}\log w^{\prime}(z),&w(q_{k})=\infty.\end{cases}

We now define the background charge modifications of the formal bosonic fields.
Given two background charges

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\pm}=\sum_{k}\beta_{k}^{\pm}\cdot q_{k}
with the neutrality conditions

\sum\beta_{k}^{\pm}=2b^{\pm},
we define

\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\pm}}^{\pm}=\Psi^{\pm}+\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\pm}}^{\pm},\qquad\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{-}=\overline{\psi_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{+}},\qquad\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}=\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}+\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{-},\qquad\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}=\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}+\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{-}

so that
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}=\Psi+\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}.

Then
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}
is a
\mathrm{PPS}(ib^{+},-ib^{-})
form.
In the
\mathbb{C}
-uniformization, we have

\displaystyle\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}(z)

\displaystyle=i\sum_{k}\beta_{k}^{+}\log\frac{1}{z-q_{k}}-i\sum_{k}\beta_{k}^{-}\log\frac{1}{\bar{z}-\bar{q}_{k}}

\displaystyle=i\sum_{k}(\beta_{k}^{+}-\beta_{k}^{-})\log\frac{1}{|z-q_{k}|}+\sum_{k}(\beta_{k}^{+}+\beta_{k}^{-})\arg(z-q_{k}).

Here the summation is taken over all
k
’s but
q_{k}=\infty.

In particular, if
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}=\overline{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}\equiv\boldsymbol{\beta},
then

(5.3)

\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta},\overline{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}(z)=-2\sum_{k}\mathrm{Im}\,\beta_{k}\log\frac{1}{|z-q_{k}|}+2\sum_{k}\mathrm{Re}\,\beta_{k}\arg(z-q_{k})

in the
\mathbb{C}
-uniformization.

For two divisors
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\pm}=\sum\tau_{j}^{\pm}\cdot z_{j}
satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}),
we define

\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]:=\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+}]-\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{-}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}],

where

\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+}]:=\sum_{j}\tau_{j}^{+}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}(z_{j}),\qquad\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{-}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]:=\sum_{j}\tau_{j}^{-}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{-}(z_{j}).

Then we have

\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]=\Psi[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]+i\sum_{j,k}\tau_{j}^{+}\beta_{k}^{+}\log\frac{1}{z_{j}-q_{k}}+i\sum_{j,k}\tau_{j}^{-}\beta_{k}^{-}\log\frac{1}{\bar{z}_{j}-\bar{q}_{k}}

in the
\mathbb{C}
-uniformization.

Sometimes it is convenient to write
\boldsymbol{\beta}=[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}],

\boldsymbol{\tau}=[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}],
and

\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}].

5.2.
Stress tensors and Virasoro fields

For the reader’s convenience, we briefly review the definitions of a stress tensor and the Virasoro field. See
[
18
, Lectures 4 and 5]
for more details. Suppose
A^{+}
(
A^{-},
respectively) is a Fock space
holomorphic
(
anti-holomorphic
, respectively) quadratic differential.
Recall that a Fock space field
X
is (anti-)holomorphic if the correlation
z\mapsto\mathbf{E}\,X(z)\mathcal{X}
is (anti-)holomorphic in the complement of
S_{\mathcal{X}}
for any tensor product
\mathcal{X}
of the Gaussian free fields.
Let
v
be a non-random holomorphic vector field defined in some neighborhood of
\zeta.

We define the residue operator
A_{v}^{+}
(
A_{v}^{-}
, respectively) as an operator on Fock space fields:

(A_{v}^{+}X)(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{(z)}vA^{+}\,X(z),\qquad(A_{v}^{-}X)(z)=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{(z)}\bar{v}A^{-}\,X(z)

in a given chart
\phi,\;\phi(\zeta)=z.

A pair
W=(A^{+},A^{-})

is called a
stress tensor
for a Fock space field
X
if for all non-random local vector fields
v,
the so-called “residue form of Ward’s identity”

(5.4)

\mathcal{L}_{v}X=A^{+}_{v}X+A^{-}_{v}X

holds in the maximal open set
D_{\mathrm{hol}}(v)
where
v
is holomorphic.
We recall the definition of Lie derivatives (see
[
18
, Section 3.4]
):

(\mathcal{L}_{v}X\,\|\,\phi)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}(X\,\|\,\phi\circ\psi_{-t}),

where
\psi_{t}
is a local flow of
v,
and
\phi
is an arbitrary chart.
The Lie derivative operator
\mathcal{L}_{v}
depends
\mathbb{R}
-linearly on vector fields
v
and it is convenient to consider its
\mathbb{C}
-linear part
\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}
and anti-linear part
\mathcal{L}_{v}^{-}:

2\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}=\mathcal{L}_{v}-i\mathcal{L}_{iv},\qquad 2\mathcal{L}_{v}^{-}=\mathcal{L}_{v}+i\mathcal{L}_{iv}.

The
\mathbb{C}
-linear part and the anti-linear part are related as
\mathcal{L}_{v}^{-}=\overline{\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}}
and the conjugation means that

\overline{\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}}X=\overline{\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}\bar{X}}.

If
X
is a
(\lambda,\lambda_{*})
-differential, then

(5.5)

\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}X=(v\partial+\lambda v^{\prime})X,\qquad\mathcal{L}_{v}^{-}X=(\bar{v}{\bar{\partial}}+\lambda_{*}\overline{v^{\prime}})X;

if
X
is a
\mathrm{PPS}(\mu^{+},\mu^{-})
form, then

(5.6)

\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}X=v\partial X+\mu^{+}v^{\prime},\qquad\mathcal{L}_{v}^{-}X=\bar{v}{\bar{\partial}}X+\mu^{-}\overline{v^{\prime}};

if
X
is a pre-Schwarzian form of order
\mu,
then

(5.7)

\mathcal{L}_{v}X=\left(v\partial+v^{\prime}\right)X+\mu v^{\prime\prime};

if
X
is a Schwarzian form of order
\mu,
then

(5.8)

\mathcal{L}_{v}X=\left(v\partial+2v^{\prime}\right)X+\mu v^{\prime\prime\prime}.

We denote by
\mathcal{F}(W)\equiv\mathcal{F}(A^{+},A^{-})

Ward’s family
of
W,
the linear space of all Fock space fields
X
with a stress tensor
W
in common.
If
\mathcal{F}(A^{+},A^{-})
is closed under complex conjugation, we can choose
A^{+}=A,A^{-}=\bar{A}.

In this symmetric case, the Lie derivatives operators
\mathcal{L}^{\pm}_{v}
acts on
\mathcal{F}(A,\bar{A})
as the residue operators
A^{\pm}_{v}.

In this case
X\in\mathcal{F}(A,\bar{A})
if and only if the following Ward’s OPEs hold in every local chart
\phi:

(5.9)

\Sing_{\zeta\to z}[A(\zeta)X(z)]=(\mathcal{L}_{k_{\zeta}}^{+}X)(z),\quad\Sing_{\zeta\to z}[A(\zeta)\bar{X}(z)]=(\mathcal{L}_{k_{\zeta}}^{+}\bar{X})(z),

where
\Sing_{\zeta\to z}
means the singular part of the operator product expansion in chart
\phi
as
\zeta\to z
and
k_{\zeta}
is the local vector field given by

(k_{\zeta}\|\phi)(\eta)=\frac{1}{\zeta-\eta}.

See
[
18
, Proposition 5.3]
.
We often use the notation
\sim
for the singular part of the operator product expansion.

For example, in a simply-connected domain, the Gaussian free field
\Phi
(with Dirichlet boundary condition) has a stress tensor

(A,\bar{A}),\qquad A=-\frac{1}{2}J\odot J,\qquad J=\partial\Phi.

It follows from Ward’s OPE

A(\zeta)\Phi(z)\sim\frac{\partial\Phi(z)}{\zeta-z},\qquad\zeta\to z.

It is well known that Ward’s family is closed under the OPE product
*
and differentiations, see
[
18
, Proposition 5.8]
.
This fact implies that
(A,\bar{A})
is also a stress tensor for
J
and
T:=-\frac{1}{2}J*J.

On the Riemann sphere, the current fields
J,\bar{J}
are defined as well-defined single-variable Fock space fields,

J(z)=\partial_{z}\Psi(z,z_{0}),\qquad\bar{J}(z)={\bar{\partial}}_{z}\Psi(z,z_{0}).

It is easy to see that this definition does not depend on the choice of
z_{0}.

Furthermore,
J
is holomorphic and
\bar{J}
is anti-holomorphic.
As in a simply-connected domain, the Gaussian free field
\Psi
has a stress tensor

(A,\bar{A}),\qquad A=-\frac{1}{2}J\odot J.

In this case, Ward’s OPE reads as

A(\zeta)\Psi(z,z_{0})\sim\frac{\partial_{z}\Psi(z,z_{0})}{\zeta-z},\qquad\zeta\to z.

A pair
(T^{+},T^{-})
of Fock space fields is called the Virasoro pair for the family
\mathcal{F}(A^{+},A^{-})
if

T^{\pm}\in\mathcal{F}(A^{+},A^{-})
and if
T^{+}-A^{+},\overline{T^{-}-A^{-}}
are non-random holomorphic (or meromorphic with poles where background charges are placed, see Section
5
) Schwarzian forms.
In the symmetric case,
T
is called the
Virasoro field
for Ward’s family
\mathcal{F}(A,\bar{A}).

Both in a simply-connected domain and on the Riemann sphere, the Virasoro field
T
is given by

T=-\frac{1}{2}J*J

or by the operator product expansion

J(\zeta)J(z)=-\frac{1}{(\zeta-z)^{2}}-2T(z)+o(1),\qquad\zeta\to z.

It is easy to see that
T
is a Schwarzian form of order
1/12.

See
[
18
, Proposition 3.4]
in a simply-connected domain or
[
17
, Proposition 4.1]
on a compact Riemann surface.

Theorem 5.2
.

The bosonic field
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\equiv\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}
has a stress tensor
W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\equiv(A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+},A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{-}),

A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}=A+ib^{+}\partial J-\partial\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}J,\qquad A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{-}=\overline{A_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{+}}(=\bar{A}-ib^{-}{\bar{\partial}}\bar{J}-\overline{\partial\psi_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{+}}\bar{J}).

The Virasoro pair
(T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+},T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{-})
for the family
\mathcal{F}(W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}})
is given by

\displaystyle T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(\partial\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}*\partial\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}})+ib^{+}\partial^{2}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}},

\displaystyle T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{-}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}({\bar{\partial}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}*{\bar{\partial}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}})-ib^{-}{\bar{\partial}}^{2}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}.

Proof.

We first observe that
A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}
is a holomorphic quadratic differential.
Indeed,
ib^{+}\partial J
and
\partial\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}J
satisfy

\displaystyle ib^{+}\partial J

\displaystyle=ib^{+}h^{\prime\prime}\tilde{J}\circ h+ib(h^{\prime})^{2}\partial\tilde{J}\circ h,

\displaystyle\partial\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}J

\displaystyle=(h^{\prime}\partial\tilde{\psi}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}\circ h+ib^{+}\frac{h^{\prime\prime}}{h^{\prime}})h^{\prime}\tilde{J}\circ h.

Similarly,
A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{-}
is an anti-holomorphic quadratic differential.

Next we check Ward’s OPE on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}(\zeta)\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}(z)

\displaystyle=\big(A(\zeta)+ib^{+}\partial J(\zeta)-\partial\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}(\zeta)J(\zeta)\big)\big(\Psi(z)+\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}(z)+\overline{\psi_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}}^{+}(z)}\big)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{J(z)}{\zeta-z}+ib^{+}\frac{1}{(\zeta-z)^{2}}+\frac{\partial\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}(z)}{\zeta-z}=\frac{\partial\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}(z)}{\zeta-z}+ib^{+}\frac{1}{(\zeta-z)^{2}}.

Similar operator product expansion holds for
A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{-}(\zeta)\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}(z)
as
\zeta\to z.

Finally we want to show that
T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}
is a Schwarzian form of order
c^{+}/12

(c^{+}=1-12(b^{+})^{2}).

We find

T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}=T+(A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}-A)-\frac{1}{2}(\partial\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}})^{2}+ib^{+}\partial^{2}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}

from the expressions of
A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}
and
T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}.

All we need to check is that
-\frac{1}{2}(\partial\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}})^{2}+ib\partial^{2}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}
is a Schwarzian form of order
-(b^{+})^{2}.

It follows from the transformation laws for
-\frac{1}{2}(\partial\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}})^{2}
and
ib^{+}\partial^{2}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}:

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}(\partial\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}})^{2}

\displaystyle=-ib^{+}h^{\prime\prime}\partial\tilde{\psi}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}\circ h-\frac{1}{2}(h^{\prime})^{2}(\partial\tilde{\psi}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}\circ h)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(b^{+})^{2}\Big(\frac{h^{\prime\prime}}{h^{\prime}}\Big)^{2},

\displaystyle ib^{+}\partial^{2}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}

\displaystyle=\phantom{-}ib^{+}h^{\prime\prime}\partial\tilde{\psi}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}\circ h+ib^{+}(h^{\prime})^{2}(\partial^{2}\tilde{\psi}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}\,)\circ h-(b^{+})^{2}\Big(\frac{h^{\prime\prime}}{h^{\prime}}\Big)^{\prime}.

Similarly,
\overline{T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{-}}
is a Schwarzian form of order
\overline{c^{-}}/12

(c^{-}=1-12(b^{-})^{2}).

∎

Example
.

In the
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
-uniformization, we have

\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}}^{+}(z)=\sum_{k}\frac{\lambda_{b^{+}}(\beta_{k}^{+})}{(z-q_{k})^{2}}+\sum_{j<k}\frac{\beta_{j}^{+}\beta_{k}^{+}}{(z-q_{j})(z-q_{k})}.

Denote by
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(S^{\prime})
the
OPE family
of
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
on
S^{\prime}\subseteq S,
the algebra (over
\mathbb{C}
) spanned by
1
and the derivatives of
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},

\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
with nodes in
S^{\prime}
under the OPE multiplication
*.

Let
S^{*}:=S\setminus(\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}^{\pm}\cup\,\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}^{\pm}).

Theorem 5.3
.

Given background charges
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\pm}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\pm}_{2}
with the neutrality conditions
(\mathrm{NC}_{b^{\pm}}),

the image of
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}(S^{*})

under the insertion of

\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Psi[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}]}
is

\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}}(S^{*})
within correlations.

Proof.

We first show that the image of
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}
under the insertion of

\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Psi[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}]}
is
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}}
within correlations:

\mathbf{E}\,\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}(\zeta,z)\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Psi[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}]}=\mathbf{E}\,\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}}(\zeta,z).

By Wick’s calculus, we have

(5.10)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\,\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}(\zeta,z)\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Psi[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}]}

\displaystyle=\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}(\zeta)-\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}(z)+\mathbf{E}\,\Psi(\zeta,z)\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Psi[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}]}

\displaystyle=\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}(\zeta)-\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}(z)+i\,\mathbf{E}\,\Psi(\zeta,z)\Psi[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}]=\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}}(\zeta)-\psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}}(z).

We define the correspondence
\mathcal{X}\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{X}}
by the formula
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}\mapsto\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}}

and the rules

\partial\mathcal{X}\mapsto\partial\widehat{\mathcal{X}},\quad{\bar{\partial}}\mathcal{X}\mapsto{\bar{\partial}}\widehat{\mathcal{X}},\quad\alpha\mathcal{X}+\beta\mathcal{Y}\mapsto\alpha\widehat{\mathcal{X}}+\beta\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\quad\mathcal{X}\odot\mathcal{Y}\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{X}}\odot\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}.

Denote

\widehat{\mathbf{E}}\,\mathcal{X}=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Psi[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}]}.

It suffices to show

\widehat{\mathbf{E}}\,\mathcal{X}=\mathbf{E}\,\widehat{\mathcal{X}}

for
\mathcal{X}=X_{1}(\zeta_{1},z_{1})\odot\cdots\odot X_{n}(\zeta_{n},z_{n}),

X_{j}=\partial_{\zeta_{j}}^{m_{j}}{\bar{\partial}}_{\zeta_{j}}^{\widetilde{m}_{j}}\partial_{z_{j}}^{n_{j}}{\bar{\partial}}_{z_{j}}^{\widetilde{n}_{j}}\Psi(\zeta_{j},z_{j}).

Differentiating (
5.10
),
it follows from Wick’s calculus that

\mathbf{E}\,X_{1}(\zeta_{1},z_{1})\odot\cdots\odot X_{n}(\zeta_{n},z_{n})\Psi^{\odot k}[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}]=\begin{cases}k!\prod_{j=1}^{n}\,\mathbf{E}\,X_{j}(\zeta_{j},z_{j})\Psi[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}]&\textrm{if }n=k,\\
0&\textrm{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We now compute

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{E}}\,\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{i^{k}}{k!}\,\mathbf{E}\,X_{1}(\zeta_{1},z_{1})\odot\cdots\odot X_{n}(\zeta_{n},z_{n})\Psi^{\odot k}[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}]

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{n}i\mathbf{E}\,X_{j}(\zeta_{j},z_{j})\Psi[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}]=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{E}\,\widehat{X}_{j}(\zeta_{j},z_{j})=\mathbf{E}\,\widehat{\mathcal{X}},

which completes the proof.
∎

5.3.
Embeddings

In this subsection we construct the Gaussian free field in a simply-connected domain
D
with Dirichlet boundary condition from the Gaussian free field on its Schottky double
S=D^{\mathrm{double}}.

Recall that the Gaussian free field can be defined as a Gaussian field indexed by the energy space.
See Subsection
3.5
for this definition on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}.

The energy space
\mathcal{E}(D)
can be embedded isometrically into
\mathcal{E}(S)
in a natural way.
For example, for
\mu
in the energy space
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{H}),

\displaystyle\|\mu\|_{\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{H})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}}\log\frac{1}{|z-w|^{2}}\,\mu(z)\overline{\mu(w)}-\int_{\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}}\log\frac{1}{|z-\bar{w}|^{2}}\,\mu(z)\overline{\mu(w)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}}\log\frac{1}{|z-w|^{2}}\,\nu(z)\overline{\nu(w)}=\frac{1}{2}\|\nu\|_{\mathcal{E}(\widehat{\mathbb{C}})}^{2},

where

\nu:=\begin{cases}\phantom{-}\mu\phantom{{}^{*}}\textrm{ in }\mathbb{H},\\
-\mu^{*}\textrm{ in }\mathbb{H}^{*},\end{cases}

and
E^{*}:=\{\bar{z}\,|\,z\in E\},\mu^{*}(E):=\mu(E^{*}).

As a Fock space field, the Gaussian free field
\Phi\equiv\Phi_{D}
in
D
with Dirichlet boundary condition can be constructed from the Gaussian free field
\Psi\equiv\Psi_{S}
on
S:

\Phi_{D}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Psi_{S}(z,z^{*}),

where
\iota:S\to S,z\mapsto z^{*}
is the canonical involution in the Schottky double
S
of
D.

For example,

\Phi_{\mathbb{H}}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Psi_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}(z,\bar{z}),

and

\Phi_{\mathbb{D}}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Psi_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}(z,z^{*}),\qquad z^{*}=1/\bar{z}.

In the chordal case, the current fields and the stress energy tensors are related as

J_{\mathbb{H}}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(J_{\mathbb{C}}(z)-{\bar{J}_{\mathbb{C}}(\bar{z})}\big)

and

A_{\mathbb{H}}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\big(A_{\mathbb{C}}(z)+{\bar{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\bar{z})}+J_{\mathbb{C}}(z)\odot{\bar{J}_{\mathbb{C}}(\bar{z})}\big).

Similar statements hold in the radial case.
Furthermore, the formal bosonic fields are related as

\Phi^{\pm}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(\Psi^{\pm}(z)-\Psi^{\mp}(z^{*})\big).

More generally, for a double divisor
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-})
satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}),
we have

(5.11)

\Phi[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Psi[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}^{-},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{-}].

In Subsection
9.1
we construct the Gaussian free field in
D
with Neumann boundary condition from
\Psi_{S}.

5.4.
Modification of Gaussian free field in a simply-connected domain

In Subsection
5.1
we introduce background charge modifications
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}
of the Gaussian free field on the Riemann sphere.
Here the background charges
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\pm}
satisfy the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b^{\pm}})(b^{\pm}\in\mathbb{C}).

In this subsection, from
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}},
we construct background charge modifications of the Gaussian free field with Dirichlet boundary condition in a simply-connected domain.

Let us fix a background charge parameter
b\in\mathbb{R}.

This parameter
b
is related to the central charge
c
as
c=1-12b^{2}.

For a double background charge
(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})
on
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\times D
satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}):

\int(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})=2b,

we set
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}_{*}
and define

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)\equiv\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Psi_{\sqrt{2}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\sqrt{2}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}^{-}}(z,z^{*}).

Then
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is a
\mathrm{PPS}(ib,-ib)
form and

\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z):=\mathbf{E}\,\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)=\sum(\beta_{k}^{+}+\beta_{k}^{-})\big(\arg(z-q_{k})+\arg(z-\bar{q}_{k})\big)+i\sum(\beta_{k}^{+}-\beta_{k}^{-})\log\Big|\frac{z-\bar{q}_{k}}{z-q_{k}}\Big|

in the
\mathbb{H}
-uniformization.

We also define background charge modifications of formal bosonic fields as

\Phi^{\pm}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(\Psi^{\pm}_{\sqrt{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\sqrt{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}^{-}}(z)-\Psi^{\mp}_{\sqrt{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\sqrt{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}^{-}}(z^{*})\big).

For a double divisor
(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-})
satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}),
we set
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{*}^{-}.

Then we have

(5.12)

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Psi_{\sqrt{2}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\sqrt{2}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}^{-}}[\boldsymbol{\tau},\boldsymbol{\tau}_{*}].

•
Chordal case without spins.

For a simply-connected domain
D
with marked boundary points
q,q_{k}\in\partial D,
a background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}=(2b-\sum\beta_{k})\cdot q+\sum\beta_{k}\cdot q_{k},(\beta_{k}\in\mathbb{R}),
and a conformal map
w\equiv w_{D,q}:~(D,q)\to(\mathbb{H},\infty)

we have

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\Phi+\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\qquad\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=-2b\arg w^{\prime}+2\sum\beta_{k}\arg(w-w(q_{k})).

We also define
J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:=\partial\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

Then it is a
\mathrm{PS}(ib)
form and

J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=J+j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\qquad j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=ib\frac{w^{\prime\prime}}{w^{\prime}}-i\sum\beta_{k}\frac{w^{\prime}}{w-w(q_{k})}.

In particular, if
\boldsymbol{\beta}=2b\cdot q,
then we have

(5.13)

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\Phi-2b\arg w^{\prime},\qquad J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\partial\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=J+ib\frac{w^{\prime\prime}}{w^{\prime}},

and the 1-point function
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\mathbf{E}\,\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=-2b\arg w^{\prime}
does not depend on the choice of the conformal map.
Due to this property, these modifications (
5.13
) are well-defined.
As a
\mathrm{PS}(ib)
form, the current field
J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is conformally invariant with respect to
\mathrm{Aut}(D,q).

•
Radial case with a spin only at a marked interior point.

For a simply-connected domain
D
with a marked interior point
q\in D,
we consider a conformal map

w\equiv w_{D,q}:~(D,q)\to(\mathbb{D},0),

from
D
onto the unit disc
\mathbb{D}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|<1\}.

Let
q_{k}\in\partial D
be marked boundary points.
Given a background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\beta_{q}\cdot q+\overline{\beta_{q}}\cdot q^{*}+\sum\beta_{k}\cdot q_{k}(\beta_{k}\in\mathbb{R})
with the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}):

2\,\mathrm{Re}\,\beta_{q}+\sum\beta_{k}=2b,

the 1-point function
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\mathbf{E}\,\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is given by

\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)=2\,\mathrm{Re}\,\beta_{q}\arg z+2\,\mathrm{Im}\,\beta_{q}\log|z|+2\sum\beta_{k}\arg(z-q_{k}).

in the
\mathbb{D}
-uniformization.
Also we define
J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:=\partial\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

Then it is a
\mathrm{PS}(ib)
form and

J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=J+ib\frac{w^{\prime\prime}}{w^{\prime}}-i{\beta_{q}}\frac{w^{\prime}}{w}-i\sum\beta_{k}\frac{w^{\prime}}{w-w(q_{k})}.

In particular, if
\boldsymbol{\beta}=b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*},
then

(5.14)

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\Phi+\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\qquad\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=-2b\arg\frac{w^{\prime}}{w}.

Since the multivalued function
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
does not depend on the choice of the conformal map, the “bosonic” field,
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
in
D^{*}:=D\setminus\{q\}
is invariant with respect to
\mathrm{Aut}(D,q).

Also we have

(5.15)

J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=J+j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\qquad j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\partial\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=ib\Big(\frac{w^{\prime\prime}}{w^{\prime}}-\frac{w^{\prime}}{w}\Big).

Remarks
.

(a) For
b\neq 0,
the 1-point function
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
has monodromy
4\pi b
around
q.

(b) For
b\neq 0,
the 1-point function
j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
has a simple pole at
q,
and
J
is holomorphic in
D^{*}.

(c) As a
\mathrm{PS}(ib)
form, the current
J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is conformally invariant with respect to
\mathrm{Aut}(D,q).

For a symmetric background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\sum\beta_{k}\cdot q_{k}+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\beta_{k}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\cdot q_{k}^{*}

on
S,

we have

\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z):=\mathbf{E}\,\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)=2\sum\mathrm{Re}\,\beta_{k}\big(\arg(z-q_{k})+\arg(z-\bar{q}_{k})\big)-2\sum\mathrm{Im}\,\beta_{k}\log\Big|\frac{z-\bar{q}_{k}}{z-q_{k}}\Big|

and

(5.16)

j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z):=\mathbf{E}\,J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)=-i\sum\Big(\frac{\beta_{k}}{z-q_{k}}+\frac{\bar{\beta}_{k}}{z-\bar{q}_{k}}\Big)

in the
\mathbb{H}
-uniformization if all
q_{k}
’s are in
D.

Later, we present the connection between boundary conformal field theory with symmetric background charges and the chordal/radial SLE theory with forces and spins.
The following theorem is parallel with Theorem
5.2
.

Theorem 5.4
.

For a symmetric background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}
on
S,
the bosonic field
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
in
D
has a stress tensor
(A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\bar{A}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}):

A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=A+ib\partial J-j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}J

and its Virasoro field is

T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=-\frac{1}{2}J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}*J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+ib\partial J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

Example
.

For a background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\beta_{q}\cdot q+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\beta_{q}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\cdot q^{*}+\sum\beta_{k}\cdot q_{k}(q\in D,q_{k}\in\partial D,\beta_{k}\in\mathbb{R})
satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
,
we have

\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)=\sum_{k}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(z-q_{k})^{2}}+\sum_{j<k}\frac{\beta_{j}\beta_{k}}{(z-q_{j})(z-q_{k})}+\frac{\lambda_{q}}{z^{2}}+\sum_{k}\frac{\beta_{q}\beta_{k}}{z(z-q_{k})}

in the
(\mathbb{D},0)
-uniformization,
where
\lambda_{k}=\lambda_{b}(\beta_{k})
and
\lambda_{q}=\lambda_{b}(\beta_{q}).

5.5.
OPE exponentials in a punctured domain

For simplicity, we consider the standard radial case
\boldsymbol{\beta}=b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*}.

Our goal in this subsection is to explain the statement that under the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0})
on
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}_{*}
the modified multi-vertex field
\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]\equiv\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]
(with
\tau_{q}^{+}=\tau_{q}^{-}=0
) in
D^{*}
can be viewed as the OPE exponential of
i\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}].

Non-chiral vertex fields
\mathcal{V}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\sigma)}
are defined by

\mathcal{V}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\sigma)}:=\mathrm{e}^{*i\sigma\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(i\sigma)^{n}}{n!}\,\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*n}.

We have

\mathcal{V}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\sigma)}=\mathrm{e}^{i\sigma\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\mathcal{V}^{(\sigma)}=\mathrm{e}^{i\sigma\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}C^{-\sigma^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\sigma\Phi},\qquad(\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:=\mathbf{E}\,\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}).

The non-random field
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is a
\mathrm{PPS}(ib,-ib)
form and
C
is the conformal radius.
In terms of a conformal map
w:(D,q)\to(\mathbb{D},0),
we have

(C\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{D})(z)=\frac{1-|w(z)|^{2}}{|w^{\prime}(z)|},

where
\mathrm{id}_{D}
is the identity chart of
D.

Thus the conformal radius is a
[-1/2,-1/2]
-differential and
\mathcal{V}^{(\sigma)}
is a
[\lambda,\lambda_{*}]
-differential with
\lambda=\lambda_{b}(\sigma),\lambda_{*}=\lambda_{b}(-\sigma).

Its 1-point function in the
(\mathbb{D},0)
-uniformization is

\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{V}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\sigma)}(z)=(1-|z|^{2})^{-\sigma^{2}}z^{\sigma b}(\bar{z})^{-\sigma b}.

Since
\mathcal{V}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\sigma)}(z)
and
\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\sigma\cdot z,-\sigma\cdot z]
contain the same Wick’s exponential and they are differentials with the same conformal dimensions and the same correlation functions in the
(\mathbb{D},0)
-uniformization,
we conclude that

\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\sigma\cdot z,-\sigma\cdot z]=\mathrm{e}^{*i\sigma\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)}.

Next, we explain the OPE product of OPE exponentials and why it corresponds to the addition of divisors.
Here is the typical example:

(5.17)

\mathcal{V}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\sigma_{1})}*\mathcal{V}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\sigma_{2})}=\mathcal{V}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})}.

Here
*
means the coefficients of the leading term in OPE expansion.
An alternative notation for such OPE multiplication is
\mathcal{V}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\sigma_{1})}\star\mathcal{V}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\sigma_{2})},
see
[
18
, Section 15.2]
.
To see (
5.17
), we need to compute asymptotic behavior of
\exp(-\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\mathbf{E}[\Phi(\zeta)\Phi(z)]):

\bigg|\frac{w(\zeta)-w(z)}{1-w(\zeta)\overline{w(z)}}\bigg|^{2\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}\sim\bigg|\frac{w^{\prime}(z)(\zeta-z)}{1-|w(z)|^{2}}\bigg|^{2\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}},\qquad\zeta\to z.

This implies
\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\sigma_{1}\Phi}*\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\sigma_{2}\Phi}=C^{-2\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})\Phi}.

The same argument shows that if
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are non-random fields, then

(f_{1}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\sigma_{1}\Phi})*(f_{2}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\sigma_{2}\Phi})=f_{1}f_{2}C^{-2\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})\Phi}.

Taking
f_{j}=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{V}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\sigma_{j})}=\mathrm{e}^{i\sigma_{j}\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}C^{-\sigma_{j}^{2}},
the above identity shows (
5.17
).

The computation is more transparent if we use formal OPE exponentials.
Let us formally define

\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}(``\equiv"\mathrm{e}^{*i\sigma\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}})=(w^{\prime})^{\lambda}w^{\sigma b}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\sigma\Phi^{+}}

(which depends on the choice of conformal map) so that

\mathcal{O}[\sigma\cdot z-\sigma\cdot z_{0}]=\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}(z)\mathcal{O}^{(-\sigma)}(z_{0})

holds (formally) within formal correlations.
In addition to the product of 1-point functions (and Wick’s exponential), we have the interaction term

\mathrm{e}^{\sigma^{2}\mathbf{E}\,[\Phi^{+}(z)\Phi^{+}(z_{0})]}=(w(z)-w(z_{0}))^{-\sigma^{2}}

in
\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}(z)\mathcal{O}^{(-\sigma)}(z_{0})
.
As in the OPE product of non-chiral vertex fields, we have (again formally)

(5.18)

\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma_{1})}*\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma_{2})}=\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})},

where the OPE product
*
could be further specified as the
\star
product (leading
coefficient) or as
*_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}
(coefficient of
(\zeta-z)^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}
in the operator product expansion of
\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma_{1})}(\zeta)\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma_{2})}(z)
).
Let us compute (
5.18
):

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma_{1})}(\zeta)\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma_{2})}(z)

\displaystyle=(w(\zeta)-w(z))^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}w^{\prime}(\zeta)^{\lambda_{b}(\sigma_{1})}w(\zeta)^{\sigma_{1}b}w^{\prime}(z)^{\lambda_{b}(\sigma_{2})}w(z)^{\sigma_{2}b}

\displaystyle\sim(\zeta-z)^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}w^{\prime}(z)^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}+\lambda_{b}(\sigma_{1})+\lambda_{b}(\sigma_{2})}w(z)^{(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})b}.

It is trivial to compute Wick’s exponential part in the operator product expansion.
The identity
\lambda_{b}(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})=\lambda_{b}(\sigma_{1})+\lambda_{b}(\sigma_{2})+\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}
now shows (
5.18
).

Note that the last OPE has the form

(5.19)

X(\zeta)Y(z)=(\zeta-z)^{\alpha}\sum{C_{n}(z)}{(\zeta-z)^{n}},\qquad C_{n}:=X*_{\alpha+n}Y.

5.6.
Insertions and changes of background charges

Denote by
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D^{\prime})
the
OPE family
of
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
on
D^{\prime}\subseteq D,
the algebra (over
\mathbb{C}
) spanned by
1
and the derivatives of
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},

\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
with nodes in
D^{\prime}
under the OPE multiplication
*.

Let
D^{*}:=D\setminus(\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}^{\pm}\cup\,\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}^{\pm}).

Theorem 5.5
.

Given background charges
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}
on
S
with the neutrality conditions
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}),

the image of
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}(D^{*})

under the insertion of

e^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}]}
is

\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}}(D^{*})
within correlations.

Theorem
5.5
follows immediately from Theorem
5.3
and the Schottky double construction (
5.12
) of
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
from
\Psi_{\sqrt{2}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\sqrt{2}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}^{-}}.

We now present a typical example of the insertion operators which create the chordal/radial SLE curves.
In the radial case with
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}=b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*},(q\in D),
the insertion of one-leg operator

\Lambda_{p}\equiv\Lambda(p):=\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}],\quad\boldsymbol{\tau}=a\cdot p-\frac{a}{2}\cdot q-\frac{a}{2}\cdot q^{*},\quad(p\in\partial D)

produces an operator

\mathcal{X}\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{X}}

acting on Fock space functionals/fields by the rules

(5.20)

\partial\mathcal{X}\mapsto\partial\widehat{\mathcal{X}},\quad{\bar{\partial}}\mathcal{X}\mapsto{\bar{\partial}}\widehat{\mathcal{X}},\quad\alpha\mathcal{X}+\beta\mathcal{Y}\mapsto\alpha\widehat{\mathcal{X}}+\beta\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\quad\mathcal{X}\odot\mathcal{Y}\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{X}}\odot\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}

for Fock space functionals
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
in
D^{*}
and the formula

(5.21)

\widehat{\Phi}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(z)=\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)=\Phi_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(z)+a\arg\frac{(1-w(z))^{2}}{w(z)},

where
w:(D,p,q)\to(\mathbb{D},1,0)
is a conformal map and

(5.22)

\boldsymbol{\beta}=\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}+\boldsymbol{\tau}=a\cdot p+\Big(b-\frac{a}{2}\Big)\cdot q+\Big(b-\frac{a}{2}\Big)\cdot q^{*}.

In the chordal case with
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}=2b\cdot q,(q\in\partial D),
recall that the insertion of one-leg operator

\Lambda_{p}\equiv\Lambda(p):=\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}],\quad\boldsymbol{\tau}=a\cdot p-a\cdot q,\quad(p\in\partial D)

produces an operator
\mathcal{X}\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{X}}
by the rules (
5.20
) and the formula

\widehat{\Phi}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(z)=\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)=\Phi_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(z)+2a\arg w(z),

where
w:(D,p,q)\to(\mathbb{H},0,\infty)
is a conformal map and

\boldsymbol{\beta}=\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}+\boldsymbol{\tau}=a\cdot p+(2b-a)\cdot q.

See
[
18
, Section 14.2]
.

Let

\widehat{\mathbf{E}}[\mathcal{X}]:=\frac{\mathbf{E}[\Lambda_{p}\mathcal{X}]}{\mathbf{E}[\Lambda_{p}]}=\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\tau}]}\mathcal{X}],\quad\boldsymbol{\tau}=a\cdot p-\frac{a}{2}\cdot q-\frac{a}{2}\cdot q^{*}.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D^{*})
correspond to the string
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{F}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\,}(D^{*})
under the map given by (
5.20
) – (
5.21
).
Then by Theorem
5.5
we have

(5.23)

\widehat{\mathbf{E}}[\mathcal{X}]=\mathbf{E}[\widehat{\mathcal{X}}].

Examples
.

Let
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:=\mathbf{E}\,\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
in the radial case with
\boldsymbol{\beta}
in (
5.22
).

(a) The current
J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is a pre-Schwarzian form of order
ib,

(5.24)

J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=J+ia\frac{w^{\prime}}{1-w}+\Big(\frac{ia}{2}-ib\Big)\frac{w^{\prime}}{w}+ib\frac{w^{\prime\prime}}{w^{\prime}}.

In the
(\mathbb{D},1,0)
-uniformization,

j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)=\mathbf{E}\,J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)=ia\dfrac{1}{1-z}+\Big(\dfrac{ia}{2}-ib\Big)\dfrac{1}{z};

(b) The Virasoro field
T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is a Schwarzian form of order
\frac{1}{12}c,

(5.25)

\displaystyle T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{1}{2}J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}*J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+ib\partial J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

\displaystyle=A-j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}J+ib\partial J+\frac{c}{12}S_{w}+h_{1,2}\frac{w^{\prime 2}}{w(1-w)^{2}}+h_{0,1/2}\frac{w^{\prime 2}}{w^{2}}\qquad(A=-\frac{1}{2}J\odot J),

where
h_{1,2}=\frac{1}{2}a^{2}-ab,

h_{0,1/2}=\frac{1}{8}{a^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}b^{2},

(see (
4.1
) and (
4.2
))
and
S_{w}=N_{w}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}{N_{w}^{2}},\,(N_{w}=(\log w^{\prime})^{\prime})
is the Schwarzian derivative of
w.

In the
(\mathbb{D},1,0)
-uniformization,

\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)=\dfrac{h_{1,2}}{z(1-z)^{2}}+\dfrac{h_{0,1/2}}{z^{2}};

(c) The operator produced by the insertion of one-leg operator can be extended to the formal fields.
For example, we have

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}=\Phi_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}^{+}+\dfrac{ia}{2}\log w-ia\log(1-w);

and

\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\sigma)}=\Big(\frac{1-w}{\sqrt{w}}\Big)^{a\sigma}\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}^{(\sigma)}.

6.
Extended OPE family

In this section we extend the OPE family
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D^{*})
(
D^{*}:=D\setminus(\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}\cup\,\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})
,
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}_{*}
) of
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
by adding the generators obtained from the operator product expansion of fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D^{*})
at the punctures
q_{k}\in\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}\cup\,\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}
or the rooting procedure.
Examples include the OPE exponentials of
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
with nodes at
q_{k}\in\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}\cup\,\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}.

6.1.
OPE at the puncture

Let us consider the radial case first.
For simplicity we only consider the “formal” holomorphic puncture differential in the case that
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}=b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*}:

\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau)}=(w_{q}^{\prime})^{\tau^{2}/2}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\tau\Phi^{+}(q)}.

This is a (formal) Fock space correlation functional with conformal dimension
h_{q}=\tau_{q}^{2}/2
at
q.

Proposition 6.1
.

We have

\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}(z)\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau)}\sim(z-q)^{\sigma(\tau+b)}\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma+\tau)}.

Proof.

As
z\to q,
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}(z)\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau)}

\displaystyle=w^{\sigma b}(w^{\prime})^{\lambda_{b}(\sigma)}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\sigma\Phi^{+}(z)}(w_{q}^{\prime})^{\tau^{2}/2}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\tau\Phi^{+}(q)}

\displaystyle=w^{\sigma(\tau+b)}(w^{\prime})^{\lambda_{b}(\sigma)}(w_{q}^{\prime})^{\tau^{2}/2}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\sigma\Phi^{+}(z)+i\tau\Phi^{+}(q)}

\displaystyle\sim(z-q)^{\sigma(\tau+b)}(w_{q}^{\prime})^{\sigma(\tau+b)+\lambda_{b}(\sigma)+\tau^{2}/2}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i(\sigma+\tau)\Phi^{+}(q)}

\displaystyle=(z-q)^{\sigma(\tau+b)}\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma+\tau)}.

Here we use
\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\sigma\Phi^{+}(z)}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\tau\Phi^{+}(q)}=w^{\sigma\tau}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i(\sigma\Phi^{+}(z)+\tau\Phi^{+}(q))},

w(z)\sim(z-q)w_{q}^{\prime}
as
z\to q,
and the identity
\lambda_{b}(\sigma)+\tau^{2}/2+\sigma(\tau+b)=(\sigma+\tau)^{2}/2.

The notation
\sim
means the first leading term of the operator product expansion.
∎

We can express the above proposition in any of the following formulas:

\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}*\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau)}\equiv\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}*_{\sigma(\tau+b)}\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau)}=\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma+\tau)},\qquad\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}\star\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau)}=\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma+\tau)}.

The point is that the arithmetic of divisors has the OPE nature, both in
D^{*}:=D\setminus\{q\}
and at the puncture
q.

Let us mention a special case (
\tau=0
) of the proposition.

Corollary 6.2
.

We have

\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma)}=\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}*1_{q}.

This OPE
\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}(z)\sim(z-q)^{\sigma b}\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma)}\,(z\to q)
is related to the “rooting” procedure (see
[
18
, Section 12.3]
for this procedure in the chordal case):

\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma)}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}z_{\varepsilon}^{-\sigma b}\,\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}(z_{\varepsilon}),

where the point
z_{\varepsilon}
is at distance
\varepsilon
from
q=0
in the chart.
Equivalently, this definition can be reached by applying the following rooting rule to the formal field
\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}(z)=w(z)^{\sigma b}w^{\prime}(z)^{\lambda}\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\sigma\Phi(z)}:

(a)

the point
z
in the terms
w^{\prime}(z)^{\lambda}
and
\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\sigma\Phi(z)}
is replaced by the puncture
q;

(b)

the term
w(z)^{\sigma b}
is replaced by
(w_{q}^{\prime})^{\sigma b}.

In the chordal case with
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}=2b\cdot q,
we consider the “formal” boundary puncture differential

\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau)}=(w_{q}^{\prime})^{\lambda_{b}(-\tau)}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\tau\Phi^{+}(q).}

In terms of a uniformizing map
w:(D,q)\to(\mathbb{H},0)

we have
\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}=(w^{\prime})^{\lambda_{b}(\sigma)}w^{2\sigma b}
and

\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}(z)\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau)}\sim(z-q)^{\sigma(\tau+2b)}\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma+\tau)}

as
z\to q.

We use the identity
\lambda_{b}(\sigma)+\lambda_{b}(-\tau)+\sigma(\tau+2b)=\lambda_{b}(-\sigma-\tau).

This OPE can be expressed as

\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}*\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau)}\equiv\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}*_{\sigma(\tau+2b)}\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau)}=\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma+\tau)},\qquad\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}\star\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau)}=\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma+\tau)}.

We refer to (
5.19
) to remind the readers what this type of OPE is.
In the special case
\tau=0,
we have

\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma)}=\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}*_{2\sigma b}1_{q}.

This is related to the “rooting” procedure:

\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma)}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}z_{\varepsilon}^{-2\sigma b}\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}(z_{\varepsilon}),

where the point
z_{\varepsilon}
is at distance
\varepsilon
from
q=0
in the chart.
In terms of a uniformizing map
w:(D,q)\to(\mathbb{H},\infty)
satisfying
w(\zeta)\sim-1/(\zeta-q)
as
\zeta\to q
in a fixed boundary chart at
q,
then the rooting procedure becomes

\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma)}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}w^{\prime}(z_{\varepsilon})^{\sigma b}\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}(z_{\varepsilon}),

where the point
z_{\varepsilon}
is at (spherical) distance
\varepsilon
from
q
in the chart
\phi,
see
[
18
, Section 12.3]
.

6.2.
Definition of the extended OPE family

For simplicity, we consider the standard radial case with
\boldsymbol{\beta}=b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*}.

If
X\in\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D^{*}),

(D^{*}:=D\setminus\{q\}),
then for all points
z\in D
and all holomorphic (local) vector fields
v
we have

(6.1)

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{(z)}vT_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\,X(z)=\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}X(z),\qquad\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{(z)}vT_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\,\bar{X}(z)=\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}\bar{X}(z).

This is not a characterization of fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D^{*}),
e.g.,
X\Phi(q)
would satisfy (
6.1
).
Note that the fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D^{*})
are typically not defined at
q,
e.g.,
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},
and
T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

A Fock space functional at
q
is the value of a Fock space field at
q,

X_{q}=X(q).

Here Fock space means the Fock space of the Gaussian free field
\Phi
and the functionals that depend on the chart at the puncture
q.

A Fock space functional at
q
correlates with Fock space strings
\mathcal{X}
in
D^{*}.

In fact we can consider the (tensor) products
\mathcal{X}X_{q}.

Such products are well-defined Fock space functionals.
Examples of Fock space functional at
q
include
1_{q},\Phi(q),
and even formal functionals
\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}(q)
(with our usual proviso of neutrality).

Definition
.

We say
X_{q}\in\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(q)
if
X_{q}=X_{1}*(\cdots*(X_{n}*1_{q})\cdots)
for some fields
X_{1},\cdots X_{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D^{*}).

The (extended) OPE family
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D,q)
is the collection of Fock space strings

\{\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{X}X_{q}\,|\,\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D^{*}),X_{q}\in\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(q)\}.

For example, if
X=Y=J_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}
(
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}=b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*}
), then
both
Y*(X*1_{q})
and
(Y*X)*1_{q}
are in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(q),
but they are different, see the last example in the next subsection.

6.3.
Examples of the OPE family

It is clear that
1_{q}\in\mathcal{F}(q),
so every
X\in\mathcal{F}(D^{*})
belongs to the extended family.
We now present some less obvious examples in the standard radial case,
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}=b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*}.

Example
.

As mentioned before,

\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma)}=\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}*1_{q}\in\mathcal{F}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(q),

and more generally

\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}*\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau)}=\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma+\tau)}_{q}.

We define the formal functional
\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau,\tau_{*})}
by

\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau,\tau_{*})}:=\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau)}\overline{\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\overline{\tau_{*}})}}=(w_{q}^{\prime})^{\tau^{2}/2}(\overline{w_{q}^{\prime}})^{\tau_{*}^{2}/2}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\tau\Phi^{+}(q)-i\tau_{*}\Phi^{-}(q)}.

Let us discuss the vertex algebra at the puncture.
We define the multiplication of OPE exponentials at the puncture by

\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma,\sigma_{*})}\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau,\tau_{*})}=\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma,\sigma_{*})}*\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau,\tau_{*})}

so that

\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma,\sigma_{*})}\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\tau,\tau_{*})}=\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma+\tau,\sigma_{*}+\tau_{*})}.

Examples
.

(a) We have
(\Phi_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}})_{q}=\Phi(q).
It is because
(z-q)w^{\prime}(z)/w(z)=1+o(z-q).

(b) We have

(J_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}})_{q}=J(q)+\frac{ib}{2}N_{w}(q).

This follows from
J_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}=J+j_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}
with
j_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}=ib({w^{\prime\prime}}/{w^{\prime}}-{w^{\prime}}/{w}),
and

\Big(\frac{w^{\prime}}{w}\Big)*1_{q}=\lim_{z\to q}\Big(\frac{w^{\prime}(z)}{w(z)}-\frac{1}{z-q}\Big)=\lim_{z\to q}\frac{w^{\prime\prime}(z)}{w^{\prime}(z)+w(z)/(z-q)}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{w^{\prime\prime}(q)}{w^{\prime}(q)}.

(c) We have

\displaystyle(T_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}})_{q}

\displaystyle=A(q)+2ib\partial J(q)-\frac{ib}{2}N_{w}(q)J(q)+\Big(\frac{c}{12}-\frac{1}{3}\,b^{2}\Big)S_{w}(q)-\frac{3}{8}\,b^{2}N_{w}(q)^{2}.

Example
.

For
X_{q}=X*1_{q},

Y*X_{q}\neq(Y*X)*1_{q}
in general.
For example, with the choice of
X=Y=J_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}},
we have
Y*(X*1_{q})\neq(Y*X)*1_{q}.

It is because

\Big(\frac{w^{\prime\prime}}{w^{\prime}}-\frac{w^{\prime}}{w}\Big)^{2}*1_{q}=-\frac{4}{3}\,S_{w}(q)-\frac{1}{4}\,N_{w}(q)^{2}\neq\bigg(\Big(\frac{w^{\prime\prime}}{w^{\prime}}-\frac{w^{\prime}}{w}\Big)*1_{q}\bigg)^{2}=\frac{1}{4}\,N_{w}(q)^{2}.

6.4.
Insertions

In this subsection we extend the insertion of the one-leg operator to the strings containing puncture functionals in particular to the fields in the extended OPE family.
For simplicity, we consider the standard radial case with
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}=b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*}.

Let
\Lambda_{\zeta}
denote the one-leg operator,

\Lambda_{\zeta}=\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[a\cdot\zeta-\frac{a}{2}\cdot q-\frac{a}{2}\cdot q^{*}]=\mathcal{O}^{(a)}(\zeta)\mathcal{O}^{(-a/2)}(q)\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(-a/2)}(q)}.

If
q
is a node of
\mathcal{X},
e.g.,

\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}X_{q},\qquad X_{q}=X*1_{q}\in\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(q),

then the accurate definition of the product
\Lambda_{\zeta}\mathcal{X}
is

\Lambda_{\zeta}\mathcal{X}:=\mathcal{O}^{(a)}(\zeta)\mathcal{Y}(X*\mathcal{O}_{q}),\qquad\mathcal{O}_{q}:=\mathcal{O}^{(-a/2)}(q)\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(-a/2)}(q)}.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}X_{q},

X_{q}=X*1_{q}
and
\widehat{X}_{q}=\widehat{X}*1_{q}.
Then we have

\widehat{\mathbf{E}}\,\mathcal{Y}X_{q}=\mathbf{E}\,\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}\widehat{X}_{q}.

Proof.

By definition,

\widehat{\mathbf{E}}\,\mathcal{Y}X_{q}=\frac{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{p}\mathcal{Y}X_{q}}{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{p}}=\frac{\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{O}^{(a)}(p)\mathcal{Y}(X*\mathcal{O}_{q})}{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{p}}.

In follows from (
5.23
) that

\frac{\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{Y}X(z)\Lambda_{p}}{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{p}}=\mathbf{E}\,\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}\widehat{X}(z)1_{q}.

Comparing the OPE coefficients at the puncture, we have

\frac{\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{O}^{(a)}(p)\mathcal{Y}(X*\mathcal{O}_{q})}{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{p}}=\mathbf{E}\,\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}\widehat{X}_{q}.

∎

Examples
.

Let
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}=b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}=a\cdot p+(b-a/2)\cdot q+(b-a/2)\cdot q^{*}.

(a) Recall that (see (
5.21
) and Subsection
6.3
, Example (a))

\widehat{\Phi}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}=\Phi_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}+a\arg\frac{(1-w)^{2}}{w}=\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},

and
(\Phi_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}})_{q}=\Phi(q).

Since
(\arg w)*1_{q}=\arg w_{q}^{\prime},
we have

(\widehat{\Phi}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}})_{q}=\Phi(q)-a\arg w_{q}^{\prime}.

(b) It follows from (
5.24
) and Example (b) in Subsection
6.3
that

(\widehat{J}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}})_{q}=J(q)+i\Big(\frac{a}{4}+\frac{b}{2}\Big)N_{w}(q)+iaw^{\prime}(q).

(c) Note
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{q}^{(\sigma)}:=\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{(\sigma)}*1_{q}=(w_{q}^{\prime})^{-a\sigma/2}\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma)},

cf.

\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{(\sigma)}=\Big(\frac{1-w}{\sqrt{w}}\Big)^{a\sigma}\mathcal{O}^{(\sigma)}.

We have
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}\,\mathcal{O}_{q}^{(\sigma)}=\mathbf{E}\,\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{q}^{(\sigma)}

because the differentials on both sides have the same dimension and are equal to 1 in
(\mathbb{D},0,1).

It is not difficult to see that Theorem
5.5
extends to Theorem
1.1
using the argument in the proof of Proposition
6.3
.
We remark that Proposition
6.3
is a special case (
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}=b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}=a\cdot p+(b-a/2)\cdot q+(b-a/2)\cdot q^{*}
) of Theorem
1.1
.

For two background charges
\boldsymbol{\beta},\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}
satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}),
let

\widehat{\mathbf{E}}\,\mathcal{X}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}:=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}]=\frac{\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}]}{\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}]}.

If the nodes of
\mathcal{X}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}
intersect with the those of
\boldsymbol{\beta}-\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}
, then the product of
\mathcal{X}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}]
should be understood as the sense of OPE’s.

The insertion of
V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}]
is an operator
\mathcal{X}\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{X}}
on Fock space functionals/fields given by the rules (
5.20
) and the formula

\widehat{\Phi}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}=\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

Example
.

Suppose that a divisor
\boldsymbol{\tau}
on
S
satisfies the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}).

Then we have

(6.2)

\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}].

Since both sides contain the same Wick’s exponential
V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\tau}]},
all we need is to check that both sides have the same correlation functions.
It follows from Theorem
1.1
and the algebra of OPE exponentials that

\mathbf{E}\,\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=\widehat{\mathbf{E}}\,\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=\frac{\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}]}{\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}]}=\frac{\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}+\boldsymbol{\beta}-\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}]}{\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}]}=\frac{C[\boldsymbol{\beta}+\boldsymbol{\tau}]}{C[\boldsymbol{\beta}]}=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}].

7.
Ward identities and BPZ equations

We represent the Ward functionals as the Lie derivative operators within correlations of fields in the extended OPE family.
Combining these representations (Ward identities) with the expression of the Virasoro fields in terms of the Ward functionals, we derive the Belavin-Polyakov-Zamolodchikov equations (BPZ equations) for correlations involving the Virasoro fields or the Virasoro generators.

7.1.
Ward’s identity

Let
D^{*}:=D\setminus(\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}\cup\,\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}_{*}
as before.

Lemma 7.1
.

For a non-random local vector field
v,
all fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D^{*})
satisfy local Ward’s identity (
5.4
) in
D_{\mathrm{hol}}(v)\cap D^{*}.

Proof.

For simplicity, let us consider the case of
holomorphic
fields
X
and
Y
only.
We need to show that if
X
and
Y
satisfy the residue form (
5.4
) of Ward’s identity at the nodes, then so does
X*_{\alpha+n}Y\in\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D^{*}).

(The case
\alpha=0
was covered in
[
18
, Proposition 5.8]
.)
In this case, we have

\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}X=(vT)*_{-1}X
and
\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}Y=(vT)*_{-1}Y.

We need to check that

\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}(X*_{\alpha+n}Y)=(vT)*_{-1}(X*_{\alpha+n}Y).

By Leibniz’s rule, the left-hand side is

(\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}X)*_{\alpha+n}Y+X*_{\alpha+n}(\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}Y).

Since
X
and
Y
