satisfy the residue form of Ward’s identity, it is equal to

[(vT)*_{-1}X]*_{\alpha+n}Y+X*_{\alpha+n}[(vT)*_{-1}Y].

Let us show
[(vT)*_{-1}X]*_{\alpha+n}Y+X*_{\alpha+n}[(vT)*_{-1}Y]=(vT)*_{-1}(X*_{\alpha+n}Y).

Let
C_{-},

C,
and
C_{+}
be three concentric circles centered at
z
with increasing radii,
\varepsilon_{-}<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{+}(\ll 1).

In the correlations with the string
\mathcal{X}
whose nodes are outside of the discs, we have

\displaystyle(vT)*_{-1}(X*_{\alpha+n}Y)(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\,\oint_{\zeta\in C_{+}}v(\zeta)T(\zeta)\,(X*_{\alpha+n}Y)(z)\,\mathrm{d}

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\,\oint_{\zeta\in C_{+}}\oint_{\eta\in C}v(\zeta)T(\zeta)\,(\eta-z)^{-\alpha-n-1}X(\eta)\,Y(z)\,\mathrm{d}\eta\,\mathrm{d}

\displaystyle\zeta.

For
\alpha\notin\mathbb{Z},
the multivalued factor
(\eta-z)^{-\alpha}
is canceled out by the
\mathrm{OPE}_{\alpha}
of
X(\eta)
and
Y(z).

In a similar way, we compute
X*_{\alpha+n}[(vT)*_{-1}Y](z)
as

\qquad\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\,\oint_{\zeta\in C_{-}}\oint_{\eta\in C}v(\zeta)T(\zeta)\,(\eta-z)^{-\alpha-n-1}X(\eta)\,Y(z)\,\mathrm{d}\eta\,\mathrm{d}\zeta.

Subtracting, we express
(vT)*_{-1}(X*_{\alpha+n}Y)(z)-X*_{\alpha+n}[(vT)*_{-1}Y](z)
as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\,\oint_{\eta\in C}\oint_{\zeta\in[C_{+}-C_{-}]}v(\zeta)T(\zeta)\,(\eta-z)^{-\alpha-n-1}X(\eta)\,Y(z)\,\mathrm{d}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\eta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\,\oint_{\eta\in C}(\eta-z)^{-\alpha-n-1}\oint_{(\eta)}v(\zeta)T(\zeta)X(\eta)\,\mathrm{d}\zeta\,Y(z)\,\mathrm{d}\eta.

The last integral simplifies to
[(vT)*_{-1}X]*_{\alpha+n}Y(z).

∎

The next lemma states that within correlations of fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D^{*})
the residue form of Ward’s identity holds at the marked points
q_{k}
’s where the background charges are placed.

Lemma 7.2
.

We have

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{(q_{k})}vA_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}(q_{k})=v(q_{k})\partial_{q_{k}}

within correlations of fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D^{*}).

Proof.

From the relation

A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=A+ib\partial J-j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}J,\qquad j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\zeta)=\mathbf{E}\,J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\zeta),

and the fact that the meromorphic function
j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
has a simple pole at
q_{k}
with
\Res_{q_{k}}j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=-i\beta_{k},

it follows that

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{(q_{k})}vA_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{(q_{k})}vj_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}J=i\beta_{k}v(q_{k})J(q_{k}).

We now claim that

(7.1)

i\beta_{k}\mathbf{E}\,J(q_{k})\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\partial_{q_{k}}\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

for any string
\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
of fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D^{*}).

Then lemma follows immediately.
Note that the second identity

\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}(q_{k})\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=v(q_{k})\partial_{q_{k}}\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

is obvious since
\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is a scalar with respect to
q_{k}
.

To prove the claim, let us consider a reference background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}
such that

\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\cap\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}=\emptyset.

Differentiating the relation (Theorem
5.5
)
\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]},

we have

(7.2)

\partial_{q_{k}}\mathbf{E}\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\partial_{q_{k}}\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}=i\beta_{k}\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}J(q_{k})\odot\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}.

It follows from Wick’s calculus that

(7.3)

J(q_{k}^{\prime})\odot\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}=J(q_{k}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}-i\mathbf{E}[J(q_{k}^{\prime})\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]]\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}.

(Indeed, if one applies Wick’s calculus to
J(q_{k}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]},
the term
J(q_{k}^{\prime})\odot\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}
comes from
0
contraction and the term
i\mathbf{E}[J(q_{k}^{\prime})\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]]\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}
comes from
1
contraction.)
Here we take
q_{k}^{\prime}\neq q_{k}
so that the term
J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(q_{k}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}
in the above makes sense but send
q_{k}^{\prime}
to
q_{k}
at the end.
By Wick’s calculus, we find the non-random factor in the last term as

(7.4)

i\mathbf{E}[J(q_{k}^{\prime})\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]]=\mathbf{E}[J(q_{k}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}]=j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(q_{k}^{\prime})-j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(q_{k}^{\prime}).

Alternatively, the last identity in the above follows from Theorem
5.5
:

\displaystyle j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(q_{k}^{\prime})=\mathbf{E}\,J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(q_{k}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}[J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(q_{k}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}[(J(q_{k}^{\prime})+j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(q_{k}^{\prime}))\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}]=\mathbf{E}[J(q_{k}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}]+j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(q_{k}^{\prime}).

Combining (
7.3
) and (
7.4
), we have

J(q_{k}^{\prime})\odot\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}=J(q_{k}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}+\big(j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(q_{k}^{\prime})-j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(q_{k}^{\prime})\big)\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}.

In application to
\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}
within correlations,

\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}J(q_{k}^{\prime})\odot\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(q_{k}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}-j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(q_{k}^{\prime})\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}.

Applying Theorem
5.5
again to each term on the right-hand side, we obtain

\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}J(q_{k}^{\prime})\odot\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(q_{k}^{\prime})-j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(q_{k}^{\prime})\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\mathbf{E}\,J(q_{k}^{\prime})\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

Sending
q_{k}^{\prime}
to
q_{k},
we have

\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}J(q_{k})\odot\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}]}=\mathbf{E}\,J(q_{k})\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

The claim (
7.1
) now follows from the above equation and (
7.2
).
We now finish the proof of the lemma.
∎

Combining the above two lemmas, we claim that all fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D^{*})
satisfy Ward’s identity (
5.4
) in
D_{\mathrm{hol}}(v)\cap D
within correlations.
Applying the same arguments as in the proof of Lemma
7.1
to this claim, we extend this claim to
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D).

Theorem 7.3
.

For a non-random local vector field
v,
all fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D)
satisfy local Ward’s identity (
5.4
) in
D_{\mathrm{hol}}(v)\cap D
within correlations.

7.2.
Ward equations on the Riemann sphere

In this subsection we express the Virasoro fields
T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
in terms of Lie derivative within correlations of fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(S).

Given a meromorphic vector field with poles
\xi_{l},
we define the Ward functional
W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}(v)
by

\mathbf{E}\,W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}(v)\mathcal{X}:=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\sum_{l}\oint_{(\xi_{l})}v\,\mathbf{E}\,A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{X}

for any tensor product
\mathcal{X}
of Fock space fields such that the set
S_{\mathcal{X}}
of all nodes of
\mathcal{X}
does not intersect the set of poles of
v.

Proposition 7.4
.

In correlations with any string of fields in the extended OPE family
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(S),
we have

W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}(v)=\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}.

Proof.

Since the sum of all residues of the meromorphic
1
-differential
\zeta\mapsto v(\zeta)\mathbf{E}\,A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\zeta)\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is zero, we have

\mathbf{E}\,W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}(v)\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\sum_{j}\oint_{(z_{j})}v\,\mathbf{E}\,A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\sum_{k}\oint_{(q_{k})}v\,\mathbf{E}\,A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

It follows from Ward’s identity (Theorem
7.3
) that

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\sum_{j}\oint_{(z_{j})}v\,\mathbf{E}\,A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\sum_{k}\oint_{(q_{k})}v\,\mathbf{E}\,A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\sum_{j}\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}(z_{j})\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+\sum_{k}\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}(q_{k})\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},

which completes the proof.
∎

Given
\xi\in\mathbb{C},
let us consider the vector fields
k_{\xi},v_{\xi}
given by

k_{\xi}(z)=\frac{1}{\xi-z},\qquad v_{\xi}(z)=z\frac{\xi+z}{\xi-z}

in the identity chart of
\mathbb{C}.

Ward’s equations on the Riemann sphere now follow from the previous theorem.

Corollary 7.5
.

In the
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
-uniformization, for any tensor product
\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
of fields in the extended OPE family
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},
we have

(7.5)

\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\xi)\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\xi)\,\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{L}_{k_{\xi}}^{+}\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

and

(7.6)

2\xi^{2}\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\xi)\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=2\xi^{2}\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\xi)\,\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{L}_{v_{\xi}}^{+}\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

7.3.
Ward’s equations in a simply-connected domain

To derive Ward’s equations in
\mathbb{H}
and
\mathbb{D}
, we modify Ward’s functional to be defined in a simply-connected domain.
Ward’s equations in
\mathbb{H}
(resp. in
\mathbb{D}
) represent the insertion of the Virasoro fields within correlations of fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
in terms of Lie derivative operators with respect to the chordal (resp. radial) Loewner vector fields.

We first consider the special case that no background charge is placed on the boundary
\partial D.

The general case can be treated through a suitable limit procedure.
In this special case,
A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is continuous on the boundary.
(The continuity on the boundary should be understood in terms of standard boundary charts.)
As we mentioned before, we mostly concern ourselves with a symmetric background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}.

In this case,
A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is real on the boundary.
(Again, we understand the real-valuedness on the boundary in terms of standard boundary charts.)

Given a meromorphic vector field
v
in
D
(continuous up to the boundary) with poles
\xi_{l}
’s (
\xi_{l}\in D
), we define the Ward functional
W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}(v)
by

W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}(v):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Big(\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial D_{\varepsilon}}vA_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{D_{\varepsilon}}({\bar{\partial}}v)A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\Big),

where
D_{\varepsilon}=D\setminus\bigcup B(\xi_{l},\varepsilon).

Both integrals are coordinate independent since
vA_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is a
[1,0]
-differential and
({\bar{\partial}}v)A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is a
[1,1]
-differential. Their correlations with Fock space functionals
\mathcal{X}
are well-defined provided that any node of
\mathcal{X}
is not a pole of
v.

Somewhat symbolically, the Ward functional
W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}(v)
can be represented as

W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}(v)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial D}vA_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\sum_{l}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{(\xi_{l})}vA_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},

or

W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}(v)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial D}vA_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{D}({\bar{\partial}}v)A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},

where
{\bar{\partial}}v
in the last integral should be understood in the sense of distributions.
If a background charge is placed on the boundary, then the first integral should be taken in the sense of the Cauchy principal value.

From now on, we only consider the case that
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is symmetric.
Then
W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=(A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\overline{A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}).

We now prove Theorem
1.2
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

We may consider the case that
\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\pm}\subseteq D.

It is enough to show that

W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}(v)=\mathcal{L}_{v}^{+}.

in correlations with any string of fields in the extended OPE family
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D).

Let
D_{j}\,(D^{\prime}_{k},D^{\prime\prime}_{l})
be a sufficiently small open disk centered at
z_{j}\,(q_{k},\xi_{l}),
respectively.
Applying Stokes’ theorem to

\mathrm{d}\big(v(z)\mathbf{E}\,A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}z\big)=-{\bar{\partial}}\big(v(z)\mathbf{E}\,A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\big)\,\mathrm{d}z\wedge\mathrm{d}\bar{z}=2i{\bar{\partial}}\big(v(z)\mathbf{E}\,A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\wedge\mathrm{d}y

over
D\setminus(\bigcup_{j}D_{j}\cup\bigcup_{k}D^{\prime}_{k}\cup\bigcup_{l}D^{\prime\prime}_{l}),
we have

\mathbf{E}\,W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}(v)\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\sum_{j}\oint_{(z_{j})}v\,\mathbf{E}\,A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\sum_{j}\oint_{(q_{k})}v\,\mathbf{E}\,A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

Theorem now follows from Ward’s identity (Theorem
7.3
) as in the proof of Proposition
7.4
.
∎

We recall the representation of a stress tensor
A
in terms of Ward’s functionals
W_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}(v)
with the meromorphic vector field
v=k_{\zeta}(\zeta\in\mathbb{C}).

Proposition 7.6

(Proposition 5.11 in
[
18
]
)
.

Let
A
be a holomorphic quadratic differential in
\mathbb{H},
and
W=(A,\bar{A}).

Suppose
A
is continuous and real on the boundary (including
\infty
).
Then

(7.7)

(A\,\|\,\mathrm{id})(\xi)=W^{+}(k_{\xi})+\overline{W^{+}(k_{\bar{\xi}})}.

Applying the above proposition to
A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
in the case that
\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\pm}\subseteq D,
we obtain (
7.7
) for
A=A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
and
W^{+}=W^{+}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

Combining the above proposition with Theorem
1.2
and using a limit procedure in the general case that
\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\pm}\subseteq\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,
we derive Ward’s equation in
\mathbb{H}.

Corollary 7.7

(Ward’s equations in
\mathbb{H}
)
.

In the
\mathbb{H}
-uniformization, for any tensor product
\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
of fields in the extended OPE family
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},
we have

(7.8)

\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\xi)\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\xi)\,\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+\mathbf{E}\,(\mathcal{L}_{k_{\xi}}^{+}+\mathcal{L}_{k_{\bar{\xi}}}^{-})\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

Example
.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=X_{1}(z_{1})\cdots X_{n}(z_{n})
be the tensor product of
[\lambda_{j}^{+},\lambda_{j}^{-}]
-differentials
X_{j}
in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

If
\xi\in\mathbb{R},
then we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\xi)\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\xi)\,\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+\sum_{k}\Big(\frac{\partial_{q_{k}}}{\xi-q_{k}}+\frac{{\bar{\partial}}_{q_{k}}}{\xi-\bar{q}_{k}}\Big)\,\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j}\Big(\frac{\partial_{j}}{\xi-z_{j}}+\frac{\lambda_{j}^{+}}{(\xi-z_{j})^{2}}+\frac{{\bar{\partial}}_{j}}{\xi-\bar{z}_{j}}+\frac{\lambda_{j}^{-}}{(\xi-\bar{z}_{j})^{2}}\Big)\,\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

Given a meromorphic vector field
v
with a local flow
z_{t}
on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}},

we define its reflected vector field
v^{*}
with respect to the unit circle
\partial\mathbb{D}
by the vector field of the reflected flow
z_{t}^{*}
of
z_{t}.

Indeed, if
z_{t}
is the flow of
v
, then
z_{t}^{*}=1/\bar{z}_{t}
is the reflected flow and
its vector field
v^{*}
is given by the equation
\dot{z}^{*}=-\overline{\dot{z}/z^{2}}=-\overline{v(z)}(z^{*})^{2}.

Thus we have

v^{*}(z)=-z^{2}\overline{v({1}/{\bar{z}})}.

Using a similar method as in the previous corollary, we obtain the following form of Ward’s equations in the radial case.

Corollary 7.8

(Ward’s equations in
\mathbb{D}
)
.

In the
\mathbb{D}
-uniformization, for any tensor product
\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
of fields in the extended OPE family
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},

(7.9)

2\zeta^{2}\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\zeta)\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=2\zeta^{2}\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\zeta)\,\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+\mathbf{E}\,(\mathcal{L}_{v_{\zeta}}^{+}+\mathcal{L}_{v_{\zeta^{*}}}^{-})\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

Let us emphasize that the above formulas apply to the strings without nodes at
\zeta.

The case where
\zeta
is a node is discussed in the following subsection.
We use OPE calculus and local operators.
Theorem
1.3
now follows from Corollaries
7.7
–
7.8
and the next lemma.

Lemma 7.9
.

We have

(7.10)

\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)=\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{+}_{k_{z}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{-}_{k_{\bar{z}}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

in the identity chart of
\mathbb{H}

and

(7.11)

2z^{2}\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)=\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{+}_{v_{z}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{-}_{v_{z^{*}}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

in the identity chart of
\mathbb{D}.

Proof.

Let
\lambda_{k}^{\pm}=\lambda(\beta_{k}^{\pm}),(\lambda(\sigma)=\sigma^{2}/2-\sigma b).

Since
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
is a
[\lambda_{j}^{+},\lambda_{j}^{-}]
-differential at
q_{j},

\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{+}_{k_{z}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\sum_{j}\frac{\lambda_{j}^{+}}{(z-q_{j})^{2}}+\frac{\partial_{j}\log\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}{z-q_{j}},\qquad\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{-}_{k_{\bar{z}}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\sum_{j}\frac{\lambda_{j}^{-}}{(z-\bar{q}_{j})^{2}}+\frac{{\bar{\partial}}_{j}\log\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}{z-\bar{q}_{j}}

in the identity chart of
\mathbb{H}.

Differentiating
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}],
we have

\partial_{j}\log\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\sum_{k\neq j}\frac{\beta_{j}^{+}\beta_{k}^{+}}{q_{j}-q_{k}}+\sum_{k}\frac{\beta_{j}^{+}\beta_{k}^{-}}{q_{j}-\bar{q}_{k}},\qquad{\bar{\partial}}_{j}\log\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\sum_{k\neq j}\frac{\beta_{j}^{-}\beta_{k}^{-}}{\bar{q}_{j}-\bar{q}_{k}}+\sum_{k}\frac{\beta_{j}^{-}\beta_{k}^{+}}{\bar{q}_{j}-q_{k}}.

On the other hand, it follows from (
5.16
) and
\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=-\frac{1}{2}j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{2}+ibj^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{j}^{+}}{(z-q_{j})^{2}}+\frac{\lambda_{j}^{-}}{(z-\bar{q}_{j})^{2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j<k}\bigg(\frac{\beta_{j}^{+}\beta_{k}^{+}}{(z-q_{j})(z-q_{k})}+\frac{\beta_{j}^{-}\beta_{j}^{-}}{(z-\bar{q}_{j})(z-\bar{q}_{k})}\bigg)+\sum_{j,k}\frac{\beta_{j}^{+}\beta_{k}^{-}}{(z-q_{j})(z-\bar{q}_{k})}.

As a rational function in
z,

\displaystyle\sum_{j}\frac{1}{z-q_{j}}

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{k\neq j}\frac{\beta_{j}^{+}\beta_{k}^{+}}{q_{j}-q_{k}}+\sum_{k}\frac{\beta_{j}^{+}\beta_{k}^{-}}{q_{j}-\bar{q}_{k}}\bigg)+\sum_{j}\frac{1}{z-\bar{q}_{j}}\bigg(\sum_{k\neq j}\frac{\beta_{j}^{-}\beta_{k}^{-}}{\bar{q}_{j}-\bar{q}_{k}}+\sum_{k}\frac{\beta_{j}^{-}\beta_{k}^{+}}{\bar{q}_{j}-q_{k}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j<k}\bigg(\frac{\beta_{j}^{+}\beta_{k}^{+}}{(z-q_{j})(z-q_{k})}+\frac{\beta_{j}^{-}\beta_{j}^{-}}{(z-\bar{q}_{j})(z-\bar{q}_{k})}\bigg)+\sum_{j,k}\frac{\beta_{j}^{+}\beta_{k}^{-}}{(z-q_{j})(z-\bar{q}_{k})},

which shows (
7.10
).
The formula (
7.11
) can be shown similarly.
∎

7.4.
BPZ equations

Recall the definition of Virasoro generators
L_{n}\equiv L_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
: they are operators
X\mapsto L_{n}X
acting on fields,
L_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}X=T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}*_{(-n-2)}X,
i.e.,

(7.12)

T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\zeta)X(z)=\cdots+\frac{(L_{0}X)(z)}{(\zeta-z)^{2}}+\frac{(L_{-1}X)(z)}{\zeta-z}+(L_{-2}X)(z)+\cdots\qquad(\zeta\to z).

We write
L(z)=L_{-2}(z)
in
(\mathbb{H},\infty)
and

(7.13)

L(z)=2z^{2}L_{-2}(z)+3zL_{-1}(z)+L_{0}(z)

in
(\mathbb{D},0).

Recall the Belavin-Polyakov-Zamolodchikov equations (BPZ equations
[
7
]
) in the chordal case with
\boldsymbol{\beta}=2b\cdot q,
see
[
18
, Proposition 5.13]
.

Proposition 7.10
.

Let
Y,X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
and let
X
be the tensor product of
X_{j}
’s.
Then

\mathbf{E}\,(T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}*Y)(z)\,X=\mathbf{E}\,Y(z)\mathcal{L}^{+}_{k_{z}}X+\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{L}^{-}_{k_{\bar{z}}}[Y(z)X],\qquad\boldsymbol{\beta}=2b\cdot q,

where all fields are evaluated in the identity chart of
(\mathbb{H},\infty).

Somewhat symbolically, we have

L(z)=\check{\mathcal{L}}^{+}_{k_{z}}+\mathcal{L}^{-}_{k_{\bar{z}}},

where
\check{\mathcal{L}}^{+}_{k_{z}}
means
\mathcal{L}^{+}_{k_{z}}(\mathbb{H}\setminus\{z\}),
e.g.,
\check{\mathcal{L}}^{+}_{k_{z}}Y(z)X:=Y(z)\mathcal{L}^{+}_{k_{z}}X.

We can neglect the check mark if we come to terms that we never differentiate at the poles of
v.

The previous proposition can be extended to the general background charge, see the next two theorems.
We only present the proof of BPZ equations in the radial case.
The chordal case can be proved in a similar way.

Theorem 7.11

(BPZ equations in
\mathbb{H}
)
.

Let
Y,X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
and let
X
be the tensor product of
X_{j}
’s.
Then

\mathbf{E}\,(T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}*Y)(z)\,X=\mathbf{E}\,Y(z)\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{+}_{k_{z}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}X+\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{-}_{k_{\bar{z}}}[\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}Y(z)X],

where all fields are evaluated in the identity chart of
\mathbb{H}.

Theorem 7.12

(BPZ equations in
\mathbb{D}
)
.

Let
Y,X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
and let
X
be the tensor product of
X_{j}
’s.
Then

\mathbf{E}\,L(z)Y(z)\,X=\mathbf{E}\,Y(z)\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{+}_{v_{z}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}X+\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{-}_{v_{z^{*}}}[\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}Y(z)X],

where all fields are evaluated in the identity chart of
\mathbb{D}
and
L(z)
is given by (
7.13
).

Proof.

Recall Ward’s OPE,
\Sing_{\zeta\to z}T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\zeta)Y(z)=\mathcal{L}^{+}_{k_{\zeta}}Y(z)
(see
[
18
, Proposition 5.3]
).
It follows immediately that

(7.14)

2\zeta^{2}\Sing_{\zeta\to z}T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\zeta)Y(z)=\mathcal{L}^{+}_{2\zeta^{2}k_{\zeta}}Y(z).

Denote
\delta_{\zeta}(\eta)=2\zeta^{2}k_{\zeta}(\eta)-v_{\zeta}(\eta)=2\zeta+\eta.

By (
7.12
), we observe that

\mathcal{L}_{\delta_{\zeta}}^{+}Y(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{(z)}\delta_{\zeta}(\eta)T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\eta)Y(z)\,\mathrm{d}\eta=L_{0}Y(z)+(2\zeta+z)L_{-1}Y(z).

It follows from (
7.14
) that

(7.15)

2\zeta^{2}\Sing_{\zeta\to z}T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\zeta)Y(z)-\mathcal{L}^{+}_{v_{\zeta}}Y(z)=L_{0}Y(z)+(2\zeta+z)L_{-1}Y(z).

On the other hand, by Theorem
1.3
,

2\zeta^{2}\,\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\zeta)Y(z)X=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{+}_{v_{\zeta}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}Y(z)X+\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{-}_{v_{\zeta^{*}}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}Y(z)X,

where the first term on the right-hand side is

\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{+}_{v_{\zeta}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}Y(z)X=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{L}^{+}_{v_{\zeta}}Y(z)X+\mathbf{E}\,Y(z)\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{+}_{v_{\zeta}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}X.

Combining the last three equations and subtracting all singular terms, we get

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\,\big(2\zeta^{2}\Reg_{\zeta\to z}(T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\zeta)Y(z))X

\displaystyle+L_{0}Y(z)X+(2\zeta+z)L_{-1}Y(z)X\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\,Y(z)\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{+}_{v_{\zeta}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}X+\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{-}_{v_{\zeta^{*}}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}Y(z)X,

where
\Reg_{\zeta\to z}T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\zeta)Y(z)
is the regular part of the operator product expansion.
Theorem now follows by taking the limit
\zeta\to z
in both sides.
For instance, the left-hand side converges to
\mathbf{E}\,L(z)Y(z)\,X.

∎

7.5.
Null vectors

After we briefly review the level two degeneracy equations for current primary fields, we apply these equations and the BPZ equations to the (tensor/OPE) products of the one-leg operators and the fields in the extended OPE family.

We denote by
\{J_{n}\}
and
\{L_{n}\}
the current generators and the Virasoro generators, the modes of
J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
and
T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
theory, respectively:

J_{n}(z):=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{(z)}(\zeta-z)^{n}J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\zeta)\,\mathrm{d}\zeta,\qquad L_{n}(z):=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{(z)}(\zeta-z)^{n+1}T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\zeta)\,\mathrm{d}\zeta.

As operators acting on fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},
we have the following equations:

[J_{m},J_{n}]=n\delta_{m+n,0},\quad[L_{m},J_{n}]=-nJ_{m+n}+ibm(m+1)\delta_{m+n,0},

and

[L_{m},L_{n}]=(m-n)L_{m+n}+\frac{c}{12}m(m^{2}-1)\delta_{m+n,0},

where
c
is the central charge,
c=1-12b^{2}.

By definition,
X\in\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is (Virasoro) primary if
X
is a
[\lambda,\lambda_{*}]
-differential; equivalently

(7.16)

L_{\geq 1}X=0,\qquad L_{0}X=\lambda X,\qquad L_{-1}X=\partial X,

and similar equations hold for
\bar{X}.

Here
L_{\geq k}X=0
means that
L_{n}X=0
for all
n\geq k.

A (Virasoro) primary field
X
is called
current primary
with charges
\sigma
and
\sigma_{*}
if

J_{\geq 1}X=J_{\geq 1}\bar{X}=0,

and

J_{0}X=-i\sigma X,\quad J_{0}\bar{X}=i\bar{\sigma}_{*}\bar{X}.

Let us recall the characterization of level two degenerate current primary fields.

Proposition 7.13

(Proposition 11.2 in
[
18
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a current primary field in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},
and let
\sigma,\sigma_{*}
be charges of
\mathcal{O}.

If
2\sigma(b+\sigma)=1,\,\eta=-1/(2\sigma^{2}),
then

(L_{-2}+\eta L_{-1}^{2})\mathcal{O}=0.

If the parameters
a
and
b
are related to the SLE parameter
\kappa
as

a=\pm\sqrt{2/\kappa},\qquad b=a(\kappa/4-1),

then
2a(a+b)=1
and the formal fields
\mathcal{O}^{(a)},\mathcal{O}^{(-a-b)}
satisfy

(7.17)

L_{-2}\,\mathcal{O}^{(a)}=\frac{\kappa}{4}\,\partial_{z}^{2}\mathcal{O}^{(a)},\qquad L_{-2}\,\mathcal{O}^{(-a-b)}=\frac{4}{\kappa}\,\partial_{z}^{2}\mathcal{O}^{(-a-b)}

(in all charts).
Since
\mathcal{O}^{(a)},\mathcal{O}^{(-a-b)}
are formal fields, we need to assume the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0})
on
\mathcal{O}^{(a)}X,\mathcal{O}^{(-a-b)}X
for some product
X
of formal fields.
For example, the one-leg operator
\Lambda_{p}
(both in the chordal case and in the radial one) satisfy the level two degeneracy equation.
As mentioned before, we will use this level two degeneracy equation for
\Lambda_{p}
to establish the connection between the chordal/radial SLE theory and conformal field theory.

•
Standard chordal case with
\boldsymbol{\beta}=2b\cdot q
.

Recall the BPZ equations in
(\mathbb{H},\infty):

L(z)=\check{\mathcal{L}}^{+}_{k_{z}}+\mathcal{L}^{-}_{k_{\bar{z}}},\qquad k_{\zeta}(\eta)=\frac{1}{\zeta-\eta},

which we apply to the field
Y(z)X
in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},
where
X
has no node at
z.

In all applications
Y
is holomorphic, so we have

(7.18)

\big(L(z)Y(z)\big)X=Y(z)(\check{\mathcal{L}}^{+}_{k_{z}}+\mathcal{L}^{-}_{k_{\bar{z}}})X=Y(z)(\mathcal{L}^{+}_{k_{z}}+\mathcal{L}^{-}_{k_{\bar{z}}})X.

If
X
is also holomorphic (in particular, in the “boundary” situation), then (
7.18
) becomes

\big(L(z)Y(z)\big)X=Y(z)\mathcal{L}^{+}_{k_{z}}X.

Let us discuss both sides in the equation (
7.18
).

We study the cases that
Y
is the null vector

Y(z)=\mathcal{O}^{(a)}(z),\qquad Y(z)=\mathcal{O}^{(-a-b)}(z).

Of course,
Y
is a formal field, so we need to assume the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0})
in
Y(z)X.

Recall that the conformal dimensions
h,h^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{O}^{(a)},\mathcal{O}^{(-a-b)},
respectively at
z
are

(7.19)

h:=\frac{3a^{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}=\frac{6-\kappa}{2\kappa},\qquad h^{\prime}:=\frac{3}{8a^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}=\frac{3\kappa-8}{16}.

Recall the level two degeneracy equation (
7.17
) for the formal field
\mathcal{O}^{(a)}
.
In the boundary case
z=x\in\mathbb{R}
we have

(7.20)

L_{-2}\,\mathcal{O}^{(a)}(x)=\frac{\kappa}{4}\,\partial_{x}^{2}\mathcal{O}^{(a)}(x)

in the
(\mathbb{H},\infty)
-uniformization because
\partial_{x}=\partial+{\bar{\partial}}
and
\mathcal{O}^{(a)}
is holomorphic.

In the case that
X=\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]
with
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\sum\tau_{j}\cdot z_{j},
we have

Y(z)\mathcal{L}^{+}_{k_{z}}X=\big(\sum k_{z}(z_{j})\partial_{z_{j}}+\lambda_{j}\partial_{z_{j}}k_{z}(z_{j})\big)Y(z)X,

where
\lambda_{j}=\lambda_{b}(\tau_{j})=\frac{1}{2}\tau_{j}^{2}-b\tau_{j}.

•
Standard radial case with
\boldsymbol{\beta}=b\cdot q+b\cdot q^{*}
.

We apply the BPZ equation to the tensor product
Y(z)X
of fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D,q)
with holomorphic
Y:

(7.21)

\big(L(z)Y(z)\big)X=Y(z)\mathcal{P}_{q}^{-1}(\mathcal{L}^{+}_{v_{z}}+\mathcal{L}^{-}_{v_{z^{*}}})\mathcal{P}_{q}X,\qquad v_{\zeta}(\eta)=\eta\frac{\zeta+\eta}{\zeta-\eta}.

Let us discuss both sides in the equation (
7.21
).

We consider the
(\mathbb{D},0)
-uniformization.
For
Y=\mathcal{O}^{(a)},
we have

(7.22)

L(z)\mathcal{O}^{(a)}=\Big(\frac{\kappa}{2}\,z^{2}\partial_{z}^{2}+3z\partial_{z}+h\Big)\mathcal{O}^{(a)},

where
h
is given by (
7.19
).
In particular, for
z=\mathrm{e}^{i\theta}\in\partial\mathbb{D},

L(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})\mathcal{O}^{(a)}=\Big(\!-\kappa\big(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+ih\partial_{\theta}\big)+h\Big)\mathcal{O}^{(a)}.

In the case of
O^{(-a-b)}
the corresponding differential operator is

L(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})=-\kappa^{\prime}\big(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+ih^{\prime}\partial_{\theta}\big)+h^{\prime},

where
\kappa^{\prime}=16/\kappa
and
h^{\prime}
is given by (
7.19
).

By Lemma
7.9
, the right hand-side becomes

Y(z)(\mathcal{L}^{+}_{v_{z}}+\mathcal{L}^{-}_{v_{z^{*}}})X+2z^{2}Y(z)X\,\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z).

8.
Connection to the theory of forward chordal/radial SLE with forces and spins

In Subsections
8.1
–
8.2
we define the one-leg operators for chordal/radial SLEs with forces and spins as the OPE exponentials with specific background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}
at the marked points.
The insertion of a one-leg operator changes the boundary values of Fock space fields.
As the PDEs for correlation functions of the fields in the extended OPE family
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(D),
the BPZ-Cardy equations for such insertion operations are used to prove the connection (Theorem
1.5
) between the theory
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
and the chordal/radial
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
theory.
A dipolar theory studied in
[
19
]
is the special of this theory since it is well known (
[
33
,
37
]
) that dipolar
\mathrm{SLE}(\kappa)
with two force points
q_{\pm}
is equivalent to chordal
\mathrm{SLE}(\kappa,\boldsymbol{\rho})
with
\boldsymbol{\rho}=\frac{1}{2}(\kappa-6)\cdot q_{+}+\frac{1}{2}(\kappa-6)\cdot q_{-}.

In Subsection
8.6
we present field theoretic approach to chordal
\mathrm{SLE}(\kappa,\boldsymbol{\rho})
restriction martingale-observable introduced in
[
13
, Lemma 4]
.

8.1.
One-leg operators for chordal SLEs with forces and spins

Let
(D,p,q)
be a simply-connected domain with two distinct marked boundary points
p,q\in\partial D,

and
\boldsymbol{\beta}
be a symmetric background charge on
S=D^{\mathrm{double}}
with

\boldsymbol{\beta}=a\cdot p+\boldsymbol{\eta},\quad p\notin\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\eta}

satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}):

\int\boldsymbol{\beta}=2b.

Here the parameters
a
and
b
are related to the SLE parameter
\kappa
as

a=\pm\sqrt{2/\kappa},\qquad b=a(\kappa/4-1).

Let
\boldsymbol{\tau}=a\cdot p-a\cdot q.

For any divisor
\boldsymbol{\sigma},
we denote
\check{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\,}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{\tau}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\,}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\boldsymbol{\tau}.

•
Insertions.

The insertion of the one-leg operator

\Lambda_{p}\equiv\Lambda(p):=\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]

produces an operator
\mathcal{X}\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{X}}
acting on Fock space functionals/fields by the rules (
5.20
) and the formula

\widehat{\Phi}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(z)=\Phi_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(z)+2a\arg w(z)=\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z),

where
w:(D,p,q)\to(\mathbb{H},0,\infty)
is a conformal map.

We denote

\widehat{\mathbf{E}}\,\mathcal{X}:=\frac{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{p}\mathcal{X}}{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{p}}=\frac{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{p}^{\mathrm{eff}}\mathcal{X}}{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{p}^{\mathrm{eff}}}=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}\mathrm{e}^{\odot ia\Phi^{+}(p,q)},

where
\Lambda_{p}^{\mathrm{eff}}
is the effective one-leg operator

\Lambda_{p}^{\mathrm{eff}}=\mathcal{P}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}\Lambda_{p}=C_{(b)}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}]\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]V^{\odot}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=V_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\beta}].

Theorem
1.1
says that

\widehat{\mathbf{E}}\,\mathcal{X}=\mathbf{E}\,\widehat{\mathcal{X}}

for any string
\mathcal{X}
of fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}.

We define the partition function associated with a symmetric background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}
by

Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:=\big|C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]\big|.

In the
\mathbb{H}
-uniformization,
C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
is non-negative (up to a phase), see Example (b) in Subsection
2.5
and thus we have
Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}].

•
BPZ equations.

Let
\xi\in\partial D
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}=\boldsymbol{\beta}-a\cdot p+a\cdot\xi.

Let
X
be the tensor product
X=X_{1}(z_{1})\cdots X_{n}(z_{n})
of fields
X_{j}
in
\mathcal{F}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}

(z_{j}\in D).

It follows from Theorem
7.11
that

(8.1)

\frac{1}{2a^{2}}\partial_{\xi}^{2}\mathbf{E}\Lambda_{\xi}^{\mathrm{eff}}X=\mathbf{E}\,\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}\Lambda_{\xi}^{\mathrm{eff}}X,\qquad k_{\xi}(z):=\frac{1}{\xi-z},

in the
\mathbb{H}
-uniformization.
Here
\partial_{\xi}=\partial+\bar{\partial}
is the operator of differentiation with respect to the real variable
\xi
and
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}
is taken over the finite notes of
X
and
\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}\setminus\{\xi\}.

In particular, the partition function
Z_{\xi}\equiv Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}}
satisfies the null vector equation

(8.2)

\frac{1}{2a^{2}}\partial_{\xi}^{2}Z_{\xi}=\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}Z_{\xi},

in the
\mathbb{H}
-uniformization since
Z_{\xi}=\mathbf{E}\Lambda_{\xi}^{\mathrm{eff}}
up to a phase in the
\mathbb{H}
-uniformization.
Here
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}
is taken over
\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}\setminus\{\xi\}.

•
BPZ-Cardy equations.

For
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and a tensor product
X=X_{1}(z_{1})\cdots X_{n}(z_{n})
of fields
X_{j}
in
\mathcal{F}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}

(z_{j}\in\mathbb{H})
, we denote

R_{\xi}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{q})\equiv\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}X:=\frac{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{\xi}X}{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{\xi}}=\frac{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{\xi}^{\mathrm{eff}}X}{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{\xi}^{\mathrm{eff}}}=\mathbf{E}\,X\mathrm{e}^{\odot ia\Phi^{+}(\xi,q)}.

The following is the first half of Theorem
1.4
.

Proposition 8.1

(BPZ-Cardy equations in
\mathbb{H}
)
.

In the identity chart of
\mathbb{H},
we have

(8.3)

\frac{1}{2a^{2}}\big(\partial_{\xi}^{2}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X+2(\partial_{\xi}\log Z_{\xi})\partial_{\xi}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X\big)=\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X,\qquad k_{\xi}(z):=\frac{1}{\xi-z},

where
\partial_{\xi}=\partial+{\bar{\partial}}
and
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}
is taken over the finite notes of
X
and
\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}\setminus\{\xi\}.

Proof.

By differentiation,

\frac{\partial_{\xi}^{2}\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{\xi}^{\mathrm{eff}}X}{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{\xi}^{\mathrm{eff}}}=\partial_{\xi}^{2}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X+2\frac{\partial_{\xi}Z_{\xi}}{Z_{\xi}}\partial_{\xi}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X+\frac{\partial_{\xi}^{2}Z_{\xi}}{Z_{\xi}}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X.

On the other hand, it follows from the BPZ equations (
8.1
) that the left-hand side of the above becomes
2a^{2}{\mathbf{E}\,\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}\Lambda_{\xi}^{\mathrm{eff}}X}/{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{\xi}^{\mathrm{eff}}}.

By Leibniz’s rule for Lie derivatives, we have

\frac{\mathbf{E}\,\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}\Lambda_{\xi}^{\mathrm{eff}}X}{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{\xi}^{\mathrm{eff}}}=\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X+\frac{\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}Z_{\xi}}{Z_{\xi}}\,\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X.

Combining all of the above, we find

\frac{1}{2a^{2}}\big(\partial_{\xi}^{2}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X+2\frac{\partial_{\xi}Z_{\xi}}{Z_{\xi}}\partial_{\xi}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X+\frac{\partial_{\xi}^{2}Z_{\xi}}{Z_{\xi}}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X\big)=\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X+\frac{\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}Z_{\xi}}{Z_{\xi}}\,\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X.

We remark that the coefficient of
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X
vanishes since the partition function
Z_{\xi}
satisfies the null vector equation (
8.2
).
∎

8.2.
One-leg operators for radial SLEs with forces and spins

Let
(D,p,q)
be a simply-connected domain
D
with a marked boundary point
p\in\partial D,
and a marked interior point
q\in D.

As in the previous subsection, we consider a symmetric background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}
on
S=D^{\mathrm{double}}
with

\boldsymbol{\beta}=a\cdot p+\boldsymbol{\eta},\quad p\notin\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\eta}

satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}).

Let
\eta\in\mathbb{R}.
We introduce the one-leg operator
\Lambda_{p}^{s}
with spin
s=i\eta{a^{2}}/2
as

\Lambda_{p}^{s}\equiv\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}],\qquad\boldsymbol{\tau}=a\cdot p-\frac{a+i\delta}{2}\cdot q-\frac{a-i\delta}{2}\cdot q^{*},\qquad\delta=\eta a,

where
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}=\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\tau}
is a symmetric background charge.

•
Insertions.

As in the chordal theory, the insertion of the one-leg operator
\Lambda_{p}^{s}

produces an operator
\mathcal{X}\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{X}}
acting on Fock space functionals/fields by the rules (
5.20
) and the formula

\widehat{\Phi}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(z)=\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z).

We denote

\widehat{\mathbf{E}}[\mathcal{X}]:=\frac{\mathbf{E}[\Lambda_{p}^{s}\mathcal{X}]}{\mathbf{E}[\Lambda_{p}^{s}]}=\frac{\mathbf{E}[(\Lambda_{p}^{s})^{\mathrm{eff}}\mathcal{X}]}{\mathbf{E}[(\Lambda_{p}^{s})^{\mathrm{eff}}]}=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{X}\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\tau}]},

where
(\Lambda_{p}^{s})^{\mathrm{eff}}
is the effective one-leg operator with spin
s
:

(\Lambda_{p}^{s})^{\mathrm{eff}}=\mathcal{P}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}\Lambda_{p}^{s}.

Then we have

\widehat{\mathbf{E}}[\mathcal{X}]=\mathbf{E}[\widehat{\mathcal{X}}].

Examples
.

We consider the case

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{s}=a\cdot p+\Big(b-\frac{a+i\delta}{2}\Big)\cdot q+\Big(b-\frac{a-i\delta}{2}\Big)\cdot q^{*},\qquad\delta=\eta a.

Let
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}.

(a) The bosonic
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{s}}
is a pre-pre-Schwarzian form of order
(ib,-ib),

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{s}}=\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\delta\log|w|,

where
w:(D,p,q)\to(\mathbb{D},1,0)
is a conformal map;

(b) The current
J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{s}}
is a pre-Schwarzian form of order
ib,

J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{s}}=J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\frac{\delta}{2}\frac{w^{\prime}}{w};

(c) The Virasoro field
T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{s}}
is a Schwarzian form of order
\frac{1}{12}c,

\displaystyle T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{s}}

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{1}{2}J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{s}}*J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{s}}+ib\partial J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{s}}

\displaystyle=A-j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{s}}J+ib\partial J+\frac{c}{12}S_{w}+h_{1,2}\frac{w^{\prime 2}}{w(1-w)^{2}}+h_{0,1/2}^{s}\frac{w^{\prime 2}}{w^{2}}+s\frac{w^{\prime 2}}{w(1-w)},

where
j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{s}}=\mathbf{E}\,J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{s}},

h_{1,2}=\frac{1}{2}a^{2}-ab
and

h_{0,1/2}^{s}=\frac{1}{8}(a+i\delta)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}b^{2}.

Let
X
be a tensor product of fields in the extended family
\mathcal{F}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(D).

We assume that
\zeta
is not a node of
X.

Denote

R_{\zeta}\equiv\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}X=\frac{\mathbf{E}\,(\Lambda^{s}_{\zeta})^{\mathrm{eff}}X}{\mathbf{E}\,(\Lambda^{s}_{\zeta})^{\mathrm{eff}}},\quad C_{\zeta}=\mathbf{E}\,(\Lambda^{s}_{\zeta})^{\mathrm{eff}}=C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\zeta}],\quad Z_{\zeta}=|C_{\zeta}|,

where
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\zeta}=\boldsymbol{\beta}-a\cdot p+a\cdot\zeta.

The following is the second half of Theorem
1.4
.

Proposition 8.2

(BPZ-Cardy equations in
\mathbb{D}
)
.

In the
(\mathbb{D},0)
-uniformization we have

-\frac{2}{a^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\big(\partial_{\theta}\log Z_{\zeta}\big)\partial_{\theta}\Big)\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}X=\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta}}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}X,\qquad(\zeta=\mathrm{e}^{i\theta},\theta\in\mathbb{R}),

where the Lie derivative
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta}}
is taken over the finite notes of
X
and
\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\zeta}\setminus\{\zeta\}.

Proof.

Let
\zeta=\mathrm{e}^{i\theta}\in\partial D.

Applying the BPZ equation in
(\mathbb{D},0)
to
(\Lambda^{s}_{\zeta})^{\mathrm{eff}}X,
we have

\mathbf{E}\,L(\zeta)(\Lambda^{s}_{\zeta})^{\mathrm{eff}}X=\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta}}\mathbf{E}\,(\Lambda^{s}_{\zeta})^{\mathrm{eff}}X,

where
L
is the differential operator in Subsection
7.5
:

L(z)=\frac{\kappa}{2}z^{2}\partial_{z}^{2}+3z\partial_{z}+h,\textrm{ or }L(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})=-\kappa\big(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+ih\partial_{\theta}\big)+h.

In particular,
C_{\zeta}=\mathbf{E}\,(\Lambda^{s}_{\zeta})^{\mathrm{eff}}
satisfies the null vector equation

(8.4)

L(\zeta)C_{\zeta}=\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta}}C_{\zeta}.

By differentiation,

\frac{\mathbf{E}\,L(\zeta)(\Lambda^{s}_{\zeta})^{\mathrm{eff}}X}{\mathbf{E}\,(\Lambda^{s}_{\zeta})^{\mathrm{eff}}}=(L(\zeta)-h)\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}X+\kappa\,\zeta^{2}\frac{\partial_{\zeta}C_{\zeta}}{C_{\zeta}}\partial_{\zeta}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}X+\frac{L(\zeta)C_{\zeta}}{C_{\zeta}}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}X.

It follows from Leibniz rule for Lie derivative that

\frac{\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta}}\mathbf{E}\,(\Lambda^{s}_{\zeta})^{\mathrm{eff}}X}{\mathbf{E}\,(\Lambda^{s}_{\zeta})^{\mathrm{eff}}}=\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta}}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}X+\frac{\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta}}C_{\zeta}}{C_{\zeta}}\,\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}X.

Combining all of the above, we have

(8.5)

-\kappa\Big(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+i\big(h+\zeta\frac{\partial_{\zeta}C_{\zeta}}{C_{\zeta}}\big)\partial_{\theta}\Big)\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}X=\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta}}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}X

in the
(\mathbb{D},0)
-uniformization.
Note that the coefficient of
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}X
vanishes since
C_{\zeta}
satisfies the null vector equation (
8.4
).

Let

\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\zeta}^{0}=a\cdot\zeta+\Big(b-\frac{a}{2}\Big)\cdot q+\Big(b-\frac{a}{2}\Big)\cdot q^{*},\qquad C_{\zeta}^{0}=C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\zeta}^{0}].

Then we have

\zeta\frac{\partial_{\zeta}C_{\zeta}^{0}}{C_{\zeta}^{0}}=-h,\quad h=h_{1,2}:=\frac{1}{2}a^{2}-ab.

and rewrite (
8.5
) as

-\kappa\Big(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\big(\partial_{\theta}\log\frac{C_{\zeta}}{C^{0}_{\zeta}}\big)\partial_{\theta}\Big)\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}X=\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta}}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}X.

It is easy to see that

\partial_{\theta}\log Z_{\zeta}=\partial_{\theta}\log\frac{C_{\zeta}}{C^{0}_{\zeta}}

in the
\mathbb{D}
-uniformization.
∎

8.3.
Martingale-observables for chordal/radial SLE with forces and spins

We now prove Theorem
1.5
.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

We first consider the chordal case.
Let
g_{t}
be the chordal
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
map driven by the real process
\xi_{t}:

\mathrm{d}\xi_{t}=\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\lambda(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,\quad\lambda(t)=(\lambda\,\|\,g_{t}^{-1}),\quad\lambda=\kappa\,\partial_{\xi}\log Z_{\xi},

Then we have

M_{t}=m(\xi_{t},t),\quad m(\xi,t)=(R_{\xi}\,\|\,g_{t}^{-1}).

It follows from Itô’s formula that

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}M_{t}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\kappa}\,\partial_{\xi}\big|_{\xi=\xi_{t}}m(\xi,t)\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\kappa\,\partial_{\xi}\big|_{\xi=\xi_{t}}m(\xi,t)\,\partial_{\xi}\big|_{\xi=\xi_{t}}(\log Z_{\xi}\,\|\,g_{t}^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\frac{\kappa}{2}\,\partial_{\xi}^{2}\big|_{\xi=\xi_{t}}m(\xi,t)\,\mathrm{d}t+L_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t,

where

L_{t}:=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}\big(R_{\xi_{t}}\,\|\,g_{t+s}^{-1}\big).

At each time
t,

f_{s,t}:=g_{t+s}\circ g_{t}^{-1}
satisfy the differential equations

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}f_{s,t}(\zeta)=\frac{2}{f_{s,t}(\zeta)-\xi_{t+s}}.

As
s\to 0,

f_{s,t}=\mathrm{id}-2sk_{\xi_{t}}+o(s),\qquad k_{\xi}(z)=\frac{1}{\xi-z}.

In terms of the time-dependent flows
f_{s,t},

L_{t}
is rewritten as

L_{t}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}\big(R_{\xi_{t}}\,\|\,g_{t}^{-1}\circ f_{s,t}^{-1}\big)=-2\big(\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi_{t}}}R_{\xi_{t}}\,\|\,g_{t}^{-1}\big).

The last equality follows from the fact any fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
depend smoothly on local charts.
Thus the drift term of
\mathrm{d}M_{t}
simplifies to

\Big(\frac{\kappa}{2}\,\partial_{\xi}^{2}\big|_{\xi=\xi_{t}}m(\xi,t)+\kappa\,\partial_{\xi}\big|_{\xi=\xi_{t}}m(\xi,t)\frac{\partial_{\xi}|_{\xi=\xi_{t}}(Z_{\xi}\,\|\,g_{t}^{-1})}{(Z_{\xi}\,\|\,g_{t}^{-1})}-2\big(\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi_{t}}}R_{\xi_{t}}\,\|\,g_{t}^{-1}\big)\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t.

It vanishes by the BPZ-Cardy equations (Proposition
8.1
).

Next, we consider the radial case.
For
\mathcal{X}=X_{1}(z_{1})\cdots X_{n}(z_{n}),X_{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(D),
denote

R_{\zeta}(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})\equiv\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}[X_{1}(z_{1})\cdots X_{n}(z_{n})].

Then the process
M_{t}(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})

is represented by

M_{t}=m(\zeta_{t},t),\qquad m(\zeta,t)=\big(R_{\zeta}\,\|\,g_{t}^{-1}\big),

where
g_{t}:(D_{t},\gamma_{t},q)\to(\mathbb{D},\zeta_{t},0)
is the radial
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
map driven by the real process
\theta_{t}:

\mathrm{d}\theta_{t}=\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\lambda(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,\quad\lambda(t)=(\lambda\,\|\,g_{t}^{-1}),\quad\lambda=\kappa\,\partial_{\theta}\log Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\zeta}},\quad\zeta_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{t}}.

Using a similar argument in the chordal case, we find the drift term of
\mathrm{d}M_{t}
as

\Big(\frac{\kappa}{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\kappa\frac{\partial_{\theta}(Z_{\zeta}\,\|\,g_{t}^{-1})}{(Z_{\zeta}\,\|\,g_{t}^{-1})}\big)\partial_{\theta}\Big)\Big|_{\zeta=\zeta_{t}}~m(\zeta,t)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
+\ \big(\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta_{t}}}R_{\zeta_{t}}\,\|\,g_{t}^{-1}\big)\mathrm{d}t.

By the BPZ-Cardy equations (Proposition
8.2
) in the radial case,
M_{t}
is driftless.
∎

Example
.

Non-chiral vertex fields

\mathcal{V}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\sigma)}:=\mathrm{e}^{*i\sigma\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\sigma\cdot z,-\sigma\cdot z]

have real conformal dimensions
[\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-}]
at
z:

\lambda^{+}=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}-\sigma b,\qquad\lambda^{-}=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}+\sigma b.

If the conformal spin
s:=\lambda^{+}-\lambda^{-}
is 1, then
\mathcal{V}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(\sigma)}
transforms as a (formal) vector field and its flow lines can be identified with
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}],
see
[
26
]
for a chordal case and
[
27
]
for a radial case with spin at
q.

8.4.
Examples of radial SLE martingale-observables

In this subsection we present examples of radial SLE martingale-observables including Schramm-Sheffield’s observables, Friedrich-Werner’s formula, and the restriction formula in the standard radial case with the following background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}
:

(8.6)

\boldsymbol{\beta}=a\cdot p+\Big(b-\frac{a}{2}\Big)\cdot q+\Big(b-\frac{a}{2}\Big)\cdot q^{*},

Example

(Schramm-Sheffield’s observables)
.

In the standard chordal case with

\boldsymbol{\beta}=a\cdot p+(2b-a)\cdot q,

the 1-point functions of the bosonic fields

\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)=\mathbf{E}[\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)]=2a\arg w(z)-2b\arg w^{\prime}(z),\quad w:(D,p,q)\to(\mathbb{H},0,\infty)

were introduced as SLE martingale-observables by Schramm and Sheffield, see
[
32
]
.
Similarly, the 1-point functions
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\mathbf{E}\,\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
(with standard radial background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}
in (
8.6
)) of the bosonic fields are martingale-observables of radial SLEs:

\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)=\mathbf{E}[\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)]=2a\arg(1-w(z))-a\arg w(z)-2b\arg\frac{w^{\prime}(z)}{w(z)},

where
w:(D,p,q)\to(\mathbb{D},1,0)
is a conformal map.
By Itô’s calculus, we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}[\Phi_{D_{t},\gamma_{t},q}(z)]=2a\arg(1-w_{t}(z))-a\arg w_{t}(z)-2b\arg\frac{w_{t}^{\prime}(z)}{w_{t}(z)}

\displaystyle=2a\arg(1-w(z))-a\arg w(z)-2b\arg\frac{w^{\prime}(z)}{w(z)}+\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{Re}\,\frac{1+w_{s}(z)}{1-w_{s}(z)}\,\mathrm{d}B_{s}.

One can use the
2
-point martingale-observables

\mathbf{E}[\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z_{1})\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z_{2})]=2G(z_{1},z_{2})+\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z_{1})\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z_{2})

or Hadamard’s variation formula

(8.7)

\mathrm{d}G_{D_{t}}(z_{1},z_{2})=-\,\mathrm{Re}\,\frac{1+w_{t}(z_{1})}{1-w_{t}(z_{1})}\,\mathrm{Re}\,\frac{1+w_{t}(z_{2})}{1-w_{t}(z_{2})}\,\mathrm{d}t=-\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{d}\langle\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z_{1}),\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z_{2})\rangle_{t}

to construct a coupling of radial SLE and the Gaussian free field such that

\mathbf{E}[\,\Phi_{D,p,q}\,|\,\gamma[0,t]\,]=\Phi_{D_{t},\gamma_{t},q},

see
[
14
]
.

Let us recall the
restriction property
of radial
\mathrm{SLE}(8/3)
(see
[
21
, Section 6.5]
):

•

the law of
\mathrm{SLE}(8/3)
in
\mathbb{D}
conditioned to avoid a fixed hull
K
is identical to the law of
\mathrm{SLE}(8/3)
in
\mathbb{D}\setminus K;

•

equivalently, there exist
\lambda
and
\mu
such that for all
K,

\mathbf{P}(\mathrm{SLE}(8/3)\textrm{ avoids }K)=|\Psi_{K}^{\prime}(1)|^{\lambda}(\Psi_{K}^{\prime}(0))^{\mu},

where
\Psi_{K}
is the conformal map
(\mathbb{D}\setminus K,0)\to(\mathbb{D},0)
satisfying
\Psi_{K}^{\prime}(0)>0.

(The restriction exponents
\lambda
and
\mu
of radial
\mathrm{SLE}(8/3)
are equal to
5/8
and
5/48,
respectively.)

Let
\kappa\leq 4.

On the event
\gamma[0,\infty)\cap K=\emptyset,
a conformal map
h_{t}:\Omega_{t}=g_{t}(D_{t}\setminus K)\to\mathbb{D}
is defined by

h_{t}=\widetilde{g}_{t}\circ\Psi_{K}\circ g_{t}^{-1},

where
\widetilde{g}_{t}
is a Loewner map from
\widetilde{D}_{t}=D\setminus\widetilde{\gamma}[0,t]
onto
\mathbb{D},
and

\widetilde{\gamma}(t)=\Psi_{K}\circ\gamma(t).

Let
\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}
be the effective one-leg operator, i.e.,
\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}=\mathcal{P}_{q}\Lambda
and

M_{t}:=\frac{(Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}}\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\Omega_{t}})}{(Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}}\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{H}})},\quad\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}=a\cdot\zeta_{t}+(b-\frac{a+\beta}{2})\cdot q+(b-\frac{a+\beta}{2})\cdot q^{*},\qquad(\zeta_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{i\sqrt{\kappa}B_{t}}).

Then

M_{t}=|h_{t}^{\prime}(\zeta_{t})|^{\lambda}h_{t}^{\prime}(0)^{\mu}=\Big(\zeta_{t}\frac{h_{t}^{\prime}(\zeta_{t})}{h_{t}(\zeta_{t})}\Big)^{\lambda}h_{t}^{\prime}(0)^{\mu},

where exponents are given by

\lambda=\lambda_{b}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}})\equiv\frac{a^{2}}{2}-ab=\frac{6-\kappa}{2\kappa},\qquad\mu=H_{q}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}})\equiv\frac{a^{2}}{4}-b^{2}=\frac{(\kappa-2)(6-\kappa)}{8\kappa}.

Restriction property of radial
\mathrm{SLE}(8/3)
follows from the local martingale property of
M_{t}
(by optional stopping theorem).
This is a special case of the following formula:

(8.8)

\textrm{the drift term of }\mathrm{d}M_{t}=-\frac{c}{6}\zeta_{t}^{2}S_{h_{t}}(\zeta_{t})M_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t.

In Subsection
8.6
we use the CFT argument to prove (
8.8
) for radial
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}].

We now prove Friedrich-Werner’s formula in the radial case.

Theorem 8.3
.

Let
\theta_{j}\in(0,2\pi)
all distinct. Then we have

\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{1}{t^{n}}\mathbf{P}(\mathrm{SLE}(8/3)\textrm{ hits all }[r_{t}\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{j}},\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{j}}])=(-2)^{n}\mathrm{e}^{2i\sum_{j=1}^{n}\theta_{j}}\mathbf{E}\,[\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{1}})\cdots T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{n}})\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{D}}\,],

where
\boldsymbol{\beta}=a\cdot p+(b-a/2)\cdot q+(b-a/2)\cdot q^{*},

a=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{3},

b=-\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{3},

p=1,q=0,
and
r_{t}=1-2\sqrt{t}.

Proof.

Let
z_{j}=\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{j}}.

We apply Ward’s equations to the function

\mathbf{E}\,[\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z_{1})\cdots T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z_{n})\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{D}}\,]=\mathbf{E}\,[\,T_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(z)\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}(1)T_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(z_{1})\cdots T_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(z_{n})\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{D}}\,],

by replacing
T_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(z)
on the right-hand side with the corresponding Ward’s functional.
Denote
\boldsymbol{z}=(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}),

\boldsymbol{z}_{j}=(z_{1},\cdots,\widehat{z}_{j}\cdots,z_{n}),
and

R(\zeta;\boldsymbol{z})=\mathbf{E}\,[\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}(\zeta)\,T_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(z_{1})\cdots T_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(z_{n})\,].

The non-random field
R(\zeta;\boldsymbol{z})
is a boundary differential of conformal dimension
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}{a^{2}}-ab=5/8
with respect to
\zeta
and of conformal dimension
2
with respect to
z_{j}.

It is also a differential of conformal dimension
\mu=\frac{1}{4}a^{2}-b^{2}=5/48
with respect to
q=0.

It follows from Ward’s equation for
\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}
that

(8.9)

R(\zeta;z,\boldsymbol{z})=\frac{1}{2z^{2}}\,\mathcal{L}({v_{z}},\mathbb{D})\,R(\zeta;\boldsymbol{z}),\quad(\textrm{in }\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{D}}),

where
v_{z}(\eta)=\eta(z+\eta)/(z-\eta).

Let

U(\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{n})=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2t)^{n}}\mathrm{e}^{-2i\sum_{j=1}^{n}\theta_{j}}\mathbf{P}(\mathrm{SLE}(8/3)\textrm{ hits all }[r_{t}\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{j}},\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{j}}])

(if the limit exists).
Define the non-random field
T(\zeta;z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})
as follows:

•

T
is a boundary differential of conformal dimension
\lambda=5/8
with respect to
\zeta,
and of conformal dimension
2
with respect to
z_{j};

•

T
is a differential of conformal dimension
\mu=\frac{1}{4}a^{2}-b^{2}=5/48
with respect to
q=0;

•

(T(\mathrm{e}^{i\varphi};\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{1}},\cdots,\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{n}})\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{D}})=U(\theta_{1}-\varphi,\cdots,\theta_{n}-\varphi).

We now claim that if the limit
U(\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{n})
exists then the limit
U(\theta,\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{n})
exists and

(8.10)

T(1;z,\boldsymbol{z})=\frac{1}{2z^{2}}\,\mathcal{L}({v_{z}},\mathbb{D})\,T(1;\boldsymbol{z})\qquad z,z_{j}\in\partial\mathbb{D}.

By (
8.9
) and (
8.10
),
T(1;\cdot)
and
R(1;\cdot)
satisfy the same recursive equation (see the remark at the end of this subsection) and are therefore equal since
T(1;\cdot)=R(1;\cdot)=1
for
n=0.

Thus

U(\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{n})=R(1;\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{1}},\cdots,\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{n}}).

To verify the induction argument for existence of the limit
U(\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{n})
and show (
8.10
), denote
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{n}).

We write
\mathbf{P}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
for the probability that radial
\mathrm{SLE}(8/3)
path hits all segments
[r_{t}\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{j}},\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{j}}]\,(1\leq j\leq n)
and
\mathbf{P}(\boldsymbol{\theta}\,|\,\neg\theta)
for the same probability conditioned on the event that the path avoids
[r_{t}\mathrm{e}^{i\theta},\mathrm{e}^{i\theta}].

By the induction hypothesis,

(8.11)

\mathbf{P}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\approx(-2t)^{n}z_{1}^{2}\cdots z_{n}^{2}\,T(1;\boldsymbol{z}),

where
z_{j}=\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{j}}.

On the other hand, by the restriction property of radial
\mathrm{SLE}(8/3),
we have

(8.12)

1-\mathbf{P}(\theta)=|\psi_{t}^{\prime}(1)|^{\lambda}\,\psi_{t}^{\prime}(0)^{\frac{1}{6}\lambda}

and

(8.13)

\mathbf{P}(\boldsymbol{\theta}\,|\,\neg\theta)\approx(-2t)^{n}\,T(\psi_{t}(1);\psi_{t}(z_{1}),\cdots,\psi_{t}(z_{n}))\prod_{j=1}^{n}z_{j}^{2}\psi_{t}^{\prime}(z_{j})^{2},

where
\psi_{t}
is a slit map from
(\mathbb{D}\setminus[r_{t}\mathrm{e}^{i\theta},\mathrm{e}^{i\theta}],0)
onto
(\mathbb{D},0)
with
\psi_{t}^{\prime}(0)>0
.
It follows from (
8.11
) – (
8.13
) and
\mathbf{P}(\theta,\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathbf{P}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mathbf{P}(\boldsymbol{\theta}\,|\,\neg\theta)(1-\mathbf{P}(\theta))

(z=\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})
that

\frac{\mathbf{P}(\theta,\boldsymbol{\theta})}{z^{2}z_{1}^{2}\cdots z_{n}^{2}(-2t)^{n+1}}=\frac{\psi_{t}^{\prime}(0)^{\lambda/6}|\psi_{t}^{\prime}(1)|^{\lambda}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\psi_{t}^{\prime}(z_{j})^{2}T(\psi_{t}(1);\psi_{t}(z_{1}),\cdots,\psi_{t}(z_{n}))-T(1;\boldsymbol{z})}{2tz^{2}}

up to
o(t)
terms.
Thus the limit
U(\theta,\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{n})
exists.
Since
\psi_{t}^{\prime}(0)=\mathrm{e}^{t},
we have (
8.10
):

T(1;z,\boldsymbol{z})=\frac{1}{2z^{2}}\Big(\mathcal{L}(v_{z},\mathbb{D}\setminus\{0\})+\frac{\lambda}{6}\Big)T(1;\boldsymbol{z})=\frac{1}{2z^{2}}\,\mathcal{L}({v_{z}},\mathbb{D})\,T(1;\boldsymbol{z}).

∎

Remark
.

The formula (
8.9
) holds for all
\kappa.

Setting
R(z,\boldsymbol{z})\equiv R(1;z,\boldsymbol{z}),

the formula (
8.9
) at
\zeta=1
gives the following recursive formula for
R:

\displaystyle R(z,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2z^{2}}\Big(2n\frac{1+z}{1-z}+2\lambda\frac{z}{(1-z)^{2}}+(\frac{a^{2}}{4}-b^{2})+\frac{1+z}{1-z}\sum_{j=1}^{n}z_{j}\partial_{z_{j}}\Big)R(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2z^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big(z_{j}\frac{z+z_{j}}{z-z_{j}}\partial_{z_{j}}+2\frac{z^{2}+2zz_{j}-z_{j}^{2}}{(z-z_{j})^{2}}\Big)R(\boldsymbol{z})+\frac{c}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(z-z_{j})^{4}}R(\boldsymbol{z}_{j}).

8.5.
1-point vertex observables

In this subsection we discuss some basic examples of 1-point vertex observables, including Lawler-Schramm-Werner’s derivative exponents of radial SLEs on the boundary.
Let

M^{(\tau^{+},\tau^{-};\tau_{q}^{+},\tau_{q}^{-})}(z)=\widehat{\mathbf{E}}\,\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}],\qquad\boldsymbol{\tau}=\tau^{+}\cdot z+\tau^{-}\cdot z^{*}+\tau_{q}^{+}\cdot q+\tau_{q}^{-}\cdot q^{*}.

Then by (
6.2
) we have

M^{(\tau^{+},\tau^{-};\tau_{q}^{+},\tau_{q}^{-})}=(w_{q}^{\prime})^{h_{q}^{+}}(\overline{w_{q}^{\prime}})^{h_{q}^{-}}(w^{\prime})^{h^{+}}(\overline{w^{\prime}})^{h^{-}}w^{\nu^{+}}(\bar{w})^{\nu^{-}}(1-w)^{a\tau^{+}}(1-\bar{w})^{a\tau^{-}}(1-|w|^{2})^{\tau^{+}\tau^{-}},

where the exponents are
\nu^{\pm}=\tau^{\pm}(\tau_{q}^{\pm}+b-a/2)
and the dimensions are

h^{\pm}=\lambda_{b}(\tau^{\pm}),\qquad h_{q}^{\pm}=\frac{\tau_{q}^{\pm}(\tau_{q}^{\pm}-a)}{2}.

The last formulas come from
h_{q}^{\pm}=\lambda_{b}(\tau_{q}^{\pm}+b-a/2)-\lambda_{b}(\tau_{q}^{\pm}).

•
Constant fields.

The simplest examples of 1-point vertex fields are constant fields, i.e., vertex fields with
\tau^{\pm}=0.

By the neutrality condition,
\tau_{q}^{-}=-\tau_{q}^{+}.

Since
w_{t}^{\prime}(0)=\mathrm{e}^{t-i\sqrt{\kappa}B_{t}},

M_{t}^{(0,0;\tau_{q},-\tau_{q})}=\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{q}^{2}t+ia\sqrt{\kappa}\tau_{q}B_{t}}=\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{q}^{2}t+i\sqrt{2}\tau_{q}B_{t}}.

This is a martingale.

•
Real fields.
The 1-point vertex fields are real if and only if
\tau^{+}=\tau^{-}
and
\tau_{q}^{+}=\tau_{q}^{-}.

By the neutrality condition,
\tau_{q}^{+}=-\tau^{+}.

Thus the only real fields are

M^{(\tau,\tau;-\tau,-\tau)}=|w_{q}^{\prime}|^{\tau^{2}+a\tau}|w^{\prime}|^{\tau^{2}-2b\tau}|w|^{\tau(2b-a-2\tau)}|1-w|^{2a\tau}(1-|w|^{2})^{\tau^{2}}.

When
\tau=-a,
there is no covariance at
q.

In this special case,

M^{(-a,-a;a,a)}=\bigg|\frac{w^{\prime}}{w}\bigg|^{1-2/\kappa}\bigg(\frac{1-|w|^{2}}{|1-w|^{2}}\bigg)^{2/\kappa}.

Example
.

If
\kappa=2,
then
M^{(-a,-a;a,a)}
coincides with the Lawler-Schramm-Werner observable

M=\frac{1-|w|^{2}}{|1-w|^{2}}=\frac{P_{\mathbb{D}}(1,w)}{P_{\mathbb{D}}(1,0)}=\frac{P_{D}(p,z)}{P_{D}(p,q)},

where
P_{D}
is the Poisson kernel of a domain
D.

As mentioned in Subsection
1.2
, this 1-point field is an important observable in the theory of LERW.

Example
.

If
\kappa=4,
then

M=\bigg|\frac{w^{\prime}}{w}\bigg|^{1/2}\bigg(\frac{1-|w|^{2}}{|1-w|^{2}}\bigg)^{1/2}

is Beffara’s type observable for radial
\mathrm{SLE}(4),
see
[
3
]
.
In the chordal case, Beffara’s observables are real martingale-observables of conformal dimensions

h^{+}=h^{-}=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\kappa}{16},\qquad h_{q}=0

with the estimate

\mathbf{P}(z,\varepsilon)\asymp\varepsilon^{1-\frac{1}{8}\kappa}~M(z),

where

\mathbf{P}(z,\varepsilon)
is the probability that the
\mathrm{SLE}(\kappa)
curve (
\kappa<8
) hits the disc at
z
of size
\varepsilon(\ll 1)
measured in a local chart
\phi.

See
[
6
]
.
In
[
3
]
, Feynman-Kac formula is used to construct radial SLE martingale-observables with the desired dimensions.

•
1-point vertex fields without covariance at
q
.

A 1-point vertex field
M
has no covariance at
q
if and only if

(\tau_{q}^{+},\tau_{q}^{-})=(0,0),\quad(a,0),\quad(0,a),\quad\textrm{or}\quad(a,a).

The first case is just the non-chiral vertex field

M=M^{(\tau,-\tau;0,0)}.

When
\tau^{-}=0,
the 1-point vertex observable

M^{(\tau,0;\tau_{q}^{+},\tau_{q}^{-})}=(w^{\prime}_{q})^{h_{q}^{+}}(\overline{w^{\prime}_{q}})^{h_{q}^{-}}(w^{\prime})^{h}w^{\nu}(1-w)^{a\tau}

is holomorphic.
Recall the expression for the exponents and the dimensions

h=\frac{1}{2}\tau^{2}-b\tau,\quad\nu=\tau(b-\frac{1}{2}a+\tau_{q}^{+}),\quad h_{q}^{\pm}=\frac{\tau_{q}^{\pm}(\tau_{q}^{\pm}-a)}{2}.

•
Holomorphic 1-point fields without spin at
q
.

A holomorphic 1-point field
M
has no spin at
q
if and only if

h_{q}^{+}=h_{q}^{-}.

Equivalently,

\tau_{q}^{+}=\tau_{q}^{-}\quad\textrm{or}\quad\tau_{q}^{+}+\tau_{q}^{-}=a.

Case 1.

\tau_{q}^{+}=\tau_{q}^{-}.

By the neutrality condition, we have

\boldsymbol{\tau}=\tau\cdot z-\frac{\tau}{2}\cdot q-\frac{\tau}{2}\cdot q^{*}.

The holomorphic 1-point field
\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]
is a generalization of the one-leg operator
\Lambda_{z}.

In this case, its conformal dimensions are

h=\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}-b\tau,\quad h_{q}^{\pm}=h_{q}=\frac{\tau^{2}}{8}+\frac{a\tau}{4}.

Thus

M_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{2h_{q}t}\Big(\frac{w_{t}^{\prime}}{w_{t}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}\tau^{2}-b\tau}\Big(\frac{w_{t}}{(1-w_{t})^{2}}\Big)^{-\frac{1}{2}{a\tau}}.

Example

(Derivative exponents on the boundary
[
24
]
)
.

On the unit circle, (up to constant)

M_{t}(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})=\mathrm{e}^{2h_{q}t}|w_{t}^{\prime}(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})|^{h}\Big(\sin^{2}\frac{\theta_{t}}{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}a\tau},

where
w_{t}(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})=\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{t}}.

Given
h,
the equation
h=\frac{1}{2}\tau^{2}-b\tau
is solved by

\tau_{\pm}=\frac{a}{4}\big(\kappa-4\pm\sqrt{(\kappa-4)^{2}+16\kappa h}\big).

With the choice of
\tau=\tau_{+},
Lawler, Schramm, and Werner proved that

M_{t}(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})
is a martingale.
They applied the optional stopping theorem to
M_{t}(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})
and used the estimate

\mathbf{E}[M_{t}(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})]\asymp\mathrm{e}^{2h_{q}t}\,\mathbf{E}[|w^{\prime}_{t}(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})|^{h}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{\mathrm{e}^{i\theta}}>t\}}]

to derive the derivative exponents:

\mathbf{E}[|w^{\prime}_{t}(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})|^{h}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{\mathrm{e}^{i\theta}}>t\}}]\asymp\mathrm{e}^{-2h_{q}t}\Big(\sin^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}a\tau}.

(Recall that
\tau_{z}
is the first time when a point
z
is swallowed by the hull of SLE, see Subsection
1.2
.)
From the derivative exponent for
\kappa=6,
they obtained the annulus crossing exponent for
\mathrm{SLE}_{6}
and combined it with other exponents to prove Mandelbrot’s conjecture that the Hausdorff dimension of the planar Brownian frontier is
4/3
.
See
[
23
]
and references therein.

Example
.

The field
M
is a scalar if
\tau=2b.

In this case,

M_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{t(\kappa-4)/{8}}\Big(w_{t}+\frac{1}{w_{t}}-2\Big)^{(\kappa-4)/(2\kappa)}.

Its derivative
\widetilde{M}=\partial M
has the conformal dimensions
[1,0;h_{q},h_{q}].

It is not a vertex observable.
If
\widetilde{M}=M^{(\tilde{\tau}^{+},\tilde{\tau}^{-};\tilde{\tau}_{q}^{+},\tilde{\tau}_{q}^{-})}
with
\tilde{h}^{+}=\lambda_{b}(\tilde{\tau}^{+})=1,
then

\tilde{\tau}^{+}=-2a
or
\tilde{\tau}^{+}=2a+2b.

As we will see below, the holomorphic 1-differentials without spin at
q
are not forms of
\partial M^{(2b,0,-b,-b)}.

Unlike the chordal case (see
[
18
, Proposition 15.2]
), the holomorphic differential observables are not necessarily vertex observables.

Example
.

If we take
\tau=2b-a
so that
h=h_{1,2}:=\frac{1}{2}a^{2}-ab
(there are only two values of
\tau
such that
h=h_{1,2},
namely
\tau=a,2b-a
), then

\boldsymbol{\tau}=(2b-a)\cdot z+(-b+\frac{a}{2})\cdot q+(-b+\frac{a}{2})\cdot q^{*},

and

h=\frac{6-\kappa}{2\kappa}=-\frac{a\tau}{2},\quad h_{q}=\frac{(2-\kappa)(6-\kappa)}{16\kappa}=-h_{0,1/2},\quad\nu=0.

In this case, we have

M_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{2h_{q}t}\Big(\frac{w_{t}^{\prime}}{(1-w_{t})^{2}}\Big)^{(6-\kappa)/(2\kappa)}.

If
\kappa=2,
then

M=\frac{w^{\prime}}{(1-w)^{2}}

and its anti-derivative is
1/(1-w).

See the next example.

Case 2.

\tau_{q}^{+}+\tau_{q}^{-}=a.
Let
\tau_{q}=\tau_{q}^{+}.

It follows from the neutrality condition that

\boldsymbol{\tau}=-a\cdot z+\tau_{q}\cdot q+(a-\tau_{q})\cdot q^{*}.

Thus we have

M=(w^{\prime})^{(\kappa-2)/(2\kappa)}w^{a(-\tau_{q}+\frac{1}{2}a-b)}(1-w)^{-2/\kappa}|w_{q}^{\prime}|^{\tau_{q}^{2}-a\tau_{q}}.

Example
.

The fields
M
have no covariance at
q
if and only if
\tau_{q}=0
or
a.

In these cases, we have

M^{(-a,0;a,0)}=\Big(\frac{w^{\prime}}{w}\Big)^{(\kappa-2)/(2\kappa)}(1-w)^{-2/\kappa}

and

M^{(-a,0;0,a)}=(w^{\prime})^{(\kappa-2)/(2\kappa)}w^{(6-\kappa)/(2\kappa)}(1-w)^{-2/\kappa}.

For example, if
\kappa=6,
then

M^{(-a,0;0,a)}=\Big(\dfrac{w^{\prime}}{1-w}\Big)^{1/3},

and if
\kappa=2,
then both
M^{(-a,0;a,0)}
and
M^{(-a,0;0,a)}
produce the same observable

M^{(-a,0;a,0)}=1+M^{(-a,0;0,a)}=\frac{1}{1-w}.

•
Holomorphic differentials without spin at
q
.

Let
\tau=\tau^{+},

\tau^{-}=0.

A holomorphic 1-point field
M
is a 1-differential with respect to
z
if and only if

\tau=-2a
or
\tau=2(a+b).

Furthermore, if
M
has no spin at
q,
then
\tau_{q}^{+}=\tau_{q}^{-}.

(If
\tau=-2a
or
\tau=2(a+b),
then the other possibility
\tau_{q}^{+}+\tau_{q}^{-}=a
never happens because of the neutrality condition.)

Case 1.

\tau=-2a.

By the neutrality condition, we have

\boldsymbol{\tau}=-2a\cdot z+a\cdot q+a\cdot q^{*}

and

M=w^{\prime}w^{2/\kappa-1}(1-w)^{-4/\kappa}.

Example
.

If
\kappa=2,
then

M^{(-2a,0;a,a)}=\frac{w^{\prime}}{(1-w)^{2}}

and its anti-derivative is

1/(1-w).

See the previous example.

Example
.

If
\kappa=4,
then

M^{(-2a,0;a,a)}=w^{\prime}(1-w)^{-1}w^{-1/2}
and its anti-derivative is

\log(1+\sqrt{w})-\log(1-\sqrt{w}).

Its imaginary part is a bosonic observable for a twisted conformal field theory.

Case 2.

\tau=2(a+b).

By the neutrality condition, we have

\boldsymbol{\tau}=(2a+2b)\cdot z-(a+b)\cdot q-(a+b)\cdot q^{*}

and

M_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{t(\kappa+4)/8}w_{t}^{\prime}w_{t}^{-3/2}(1-w_{t}).

8.6.
Restriction formulas

Let
\kappa\leq 4.

We first consider the chordal
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
with

\boldsymbol{\beta}=a\cdot p+\sum\beta_{k}\cdot q_{k}+(2b-a-\sum\beta_{k})\cdot q,\qquad p,q,q_{k}\in\partial D.

Fix a hull
K.

Let
g_{t}
be the chordal
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
map with the hull
K_{t}.

On the event
K_{\infty}\cap K=\emptyset,
a conformal map
h_{t}:\Omega_{t}=g_{t}(D_{t}\setminus K)\to\mathbb{H}
is defined by

h_{t}=\widetilde{g}_{t}\circ\Psi_{K}\circ g_{t}^{-1},

where
\widetilde{g}_{t}
is a Loewner map from
\widetilde{D}_{t}=D\setminus\widetilde{K}_{t}
onto
\mathbb{H},

\widetilde{K}_{t}=\Psi_{K}(K_{t}),
and

\Psi_{K}
is the conformal map
(\mathbb{H}\setminus K,0,\infty)\to(\mathbb{H},0,\infty)
satisfying
\Psi_{K}^{\prime}(\infty)=1.

Let

M_{t}:=\frac{(Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}}\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\Omega_{t}})}{(Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}}\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{H}})}=\frac{\mathbf{E}\,(\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}\|\,\mathrm{id})(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))}{\mathbf{E}\,(\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\,\mathrm{id})(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))},

where

\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}=a\cdot\xi_{t}+\sum\beta_{k}\cdot q_{k}(t),\qquad\mathrm{d}q_{k}(t)=\frac{2}{q_{k}(t)-\xi_{t}}\,\mathrm{d}t,\qquad q_{k}(0)=q_{k}

and

\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}=\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\,\Lambda
is the effective one-leg operator.
Then the process
M_{t}
is expressed in terms of
h_{t},

\xi_{t},
and
q_{k}(t)
as

(8.14)

M_{t}=h_{t}^{\prime}(\xi_{t})^{\lambda}\prod_{j}h_{t}^{\prime}(q_{j}(t))^{\lambda_{j}}\Big(\frac{h_{t}(\xi_{t})-h_{t}(q_{j}(t))}{\xi_{t}-q_{j}(t)}\Big)^{a\beta_{j}}\prod_{j<k}\Big(\frac{h_{t}(q_{j}(t))-h_{t}(q_{k}(t))}{q_{j}(t)-q_{k}(t)}\Big)^{\beta_{j}\beta_{k}}.

We now present the conformal field theoretic proof for the restriction formula (
[
13
]
) of chordal
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]:

(8.15)

\mathrm{d}M_{t}=\frac{c}{6}S_{h_{t}}(\xi_{t})M_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t+\textrm{martingale terms.}

Let

F(z,\boldsymbol{q},t):=\frac{G(z,\boldsymbol{q},t)}{H(z,\boldsymbol{q})},\quad\begin{cases}G(z,\boldsymbol{q},t):=&\mathbf{E}\,(\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}\|\mathrm{id})(z,\boldsymbol{q})(=\mathbf{E}\,(\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathbb{H}}\|h_{t}^{-1})(z,\boldsymbol{q})),\\
H(z,\boldsymbol{q}):=&\mathbf{E}\,(\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\mathrm{id})(z,\boldsymbol{q}).\end{cases}

Recall that the driving process
\xi_{t}
satisfies

\mathrm{d}\xi_{t}=\sqrt{\kappa}B_{t}+\kappa\frac{H_{\xi}(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))}{H(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))}\,\mathrm{d}t.

It follows from Itô’s formula that the drift term of
\mathrm{d}M_{t}/M_{t}
equals

\Big(\frac{\dot{F}}{F}+\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{F_{\xi\xi}}{F}+\sum_{j}\frac{F_{q_{j}}}{F}\dot{q}_{j}(t)+\kappa\frac{F_{\xi}}{F}\frac{H_{\xi}}{H}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t

evaluated at
(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t),t).

We rewrite the drift term of
\mathrm{d}M_{t}/M_{t}
in terms of
G
and
H:

\textrm{the drift term of }\frac{\mathrm{d}M_{t}}{M_{t}}=\Big(\frac{\dot{G}}{G}+\frac{\kappa}{2}\Big(\frac{G_{\xi\xi}}{G}-\frac{H_{\xi\xi}}{H}\Big)+\sum_{j}\Big(\frac{G_{q_{j}}}{G}-\frac{H_{q_{j}}}{H}\Big)\frac{2}{q_{j}(t)-\xi_{t}}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Here we use

\frac{\dot{F}}{F}=\frac{\dot{G}}{G},\qquad\frac{F_{q_{j}}}{F}=\frac{G_{q_{j}}}{G}-\frac{H_{q_{j}}}{H},\qquad\frac{F_{\xi\xi}}{F}=\frac{G_{\xi\xi}}{G}-\frac{H_{\xi\xi}}{H}-2\frac{F_{\xi}}{F}\frac{H_{\xi}}{H}.

By Ward’s equations we have

\displaystyle\dot{G}(z,\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)=

\displaystyle-2h_{t}^{\prime}(\xi_{t})^{2}h_{t}^{\prime}(z)^{\lambda}\prod_{j}h_{t}^{\prime}(q_{j}(t))^{\lambda_{j}}\mathbf{E}\big(A_{\mathbb{H}}(h_{t}(\xi_{t}))\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathbb{H}}(h_{t}(z),h_{t}(q_{1}(t)),\cdots)\|\mathrm{id}\big)

\displaystyle+2\mathbf{E}\,(\mathcal{L}_{v_{\xi_{t}}}\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}\|\mathrm{id})(z,\boldsymbol{q}(t)).

It follows from conformal invariance that

\dot{G}(z,\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)=-2(\mathbf{E}\,A_{\Omega_{t}}(\xi_{t})\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}(z,\boldsymbol{q}(t))\|\mathrm{id})+2\mathbf{E}\,(\mathcal{L}_{k_{\xi_{t}}}\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}\|\mathrm{id})(z,\boldsymbol{q}(t)).

Sending
z
to
\xi_{t},

\dot{G}(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)=-2(\mathbf{E}\,A_{\Omega_{t}}*_{\xi}\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))\|\mathrm{id})+2\mathbf{E}\,(\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi_{t}}}\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}\|\mathrm{id})(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t)),

where
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi_{t}}}
does not apply to
\xi_{t}.

By the level two degeneracy equation for
\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}
with respect to
\xi
,

\frac{\kappa}{2}\Big(\frac{G_{\xi\xi}}{G}-\frac{H_{\xi\xi}}{H}\Big)=2\frac{(\mathbf{E}\,T_{\Omega_{t}}*_{\xi}\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))\|\mathrm{id})}{(\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))\|\mathrm{id})}-2\frac{(\mathbf{E}\,T_{\mathbb{H}}*_{\xi}\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathbb{H}}(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))\|\mathrm{id})}{(\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathbb{H}}(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))\|\mathrm{id})}.

It follows from Ward’s equation that

(\mathbf{E}\,T_{\mathbb{H}}*_{\xi}\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathbb{H}}(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))\|\mathrm{id})=\mathbf{E}\,(\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi_{t}}}\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\mathrm{id})(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t)).

The formula for Lie derivatives of differentials gives

\frac{\mathbf{E}\,(\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi_{t}}}\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}\|\mathrm{id})(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))}{(\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))\|\mathrm{id})}-\frac{\mathbf{E}\,(\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi_{t}}}\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\mathrm{id})(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))}{(\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathbb{H}}(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))\|\mathrm{id})}=-\sum_{j}\Big(\frac{G_{q_{j}}}{G}-\frac{H_{q_{j}}}{H}\Big)\frac{1}{q_{j}(t)-\xi_{t}}.

Combining all of the above, we have

\displaystyle\textrm{the drift term of }\frac{\mathrm{d}M_{t}}{M_{t}}

\displaystyle=-2\frac{(\mathbf{E}\,A_{\Omega_{t}}*_{\xi}\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))\|\mathrm{id})}{(\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))\|\mathrm{id})}\,\mathrm{d}t+2\frac{(\mathbf{E}\,T_{\Omega_{t}}*_{\xi}\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))\|\mathrm{id})}{(\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t))\|\mathrm{id})}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{6}S_{h_{t}}(\xi_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t.

We now present a conformal field theoretic proof for the restriction formula of radial
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]

(\kappa=8/3).

For this purpose, we let
\zeta,q_{k}\in\partial D,q\in D,

\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{\tau}+\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,},\quad\boldsymbol{\tau}=a\cdot\zeta-\frac{a}{2}\cdot q-\frac{a}{2}\cdot q^{*}\quad\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}=\sum\beta_{k}\cdot q_{k}+(b-\frac{\beta}{2})\cdot q+(b-\frac{\beta}{2})\cdot q^{*},\quad\beta=\sum\beta_{k},

and consider the effective one-leg operator
\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}},

\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}(\zeta):=\mathcal{P}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}\,\Lambda(\zeta)=C_{(b)}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot i\Phi[\boldsymbol{\tau}]}.

Fix a hull
K
such that
K
does not intersect
\zeta=1,q_{1},\cdots,q_{k},\cdots.

Let
g_{t}
be the radial
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
map with the hull
K_{t}.

On the event
K_{\infty}\cap K=\emptyset,
a conformal map
h_{t}:\Omega_{t}=g_{t}(D_{t}\setminus K)\to\mathbb{D}
is defined by

h_{t}=\widetilde{g}_{t}\circ\Psi_{K}\circ g_{t}^{-1},

where
\widetilde{g}_{t}
is a radial Loewner map from
\widetilde{D}_{t}=D\setminus\widetilde{K}_{t}
onto
\mathbb{D},

\widetilde{K}_{t}=\Psi_{K}(K_{t}),
and
\Psi_{K}
is the conformal map
(\mathbb{D}\setminus K,0)\to(\mathbb{D},0)
satisfying
\Psi_{K}^{\prime}(0)>0.

Let

(8.16)

M_{t}:=\frac{(Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}}\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\Omega_{t}})}{(Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}}\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{H}})},\quad\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}=a\cdot\zeta_{t}+(b-\frac{a+\beta}{2})\cdot q+(b-\frac{a+\beta}{2})\cdot q^{*}+\sum\beta_{k}\cdot q_{k}(t),

where
q_{k}(t)
satisfies
q_{k}(0)=q_{k}
and

\mathrm{d}q_{k}(t)=q_{k}(t)\frac{\zeta_{t}+q_{k}(t)}{\zeta_{t}-q_{k}(t)}\,\mathrm{d}t.

We now represent
M_{t}
in terms of the effective one-leg operator:

M_{t}=F(\zeta_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t),t),\quad F(z,\boldsymbol{q},t)=\frac{G(z,\boldsymbol{q},t)}{H(z,\boldsymbol{q})},

where
G(z,\boldsymbol{q},t)=\mathbf{E}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{D}}^{\mathrm{eff}}\|h_{t}^{-1})(z),
and
H(z,\boldsymbol{q})=\mathbf{E}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{D}}^{\mathrm{eff}}\|\mathrm{id})(z).

Theorem 8.4
.

We have

(8.17)

\textrm{the drift term of }\frac{\mathrm{d}M_{t}}{M_{t}}=-\frac{c}{6}\,\zeta_{t}^{2}\,S_{h_{t}}(\zeta_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t.

Proof.

Recall that

\mathrm{d}\zeta_{t}=i\sqrt{\kappa}\,\zeta_{t}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}-\frac{\kappa}{2}\zeta_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t-\kappa\zeta_{t}^{2}\Big(\frac{H_{\zeta}}{H}+\frac{h}{\zeta_{t}}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t=i\sqrt{\kappa}\,\zeta_{t}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}-\kappa\zeta_{t}^{2}\frac{H_{\zeta}}{H}\,\mathrm{d}t-3\zeta_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t.

It follows from Itô’s formula that

\mathrm{d}M_{t}=\dot{F}\,\mathrm{d}t-\frac{\kappa}{2}\zeta_{t}^{2}\,F_{\zeta\zeta}\,\mathrm{d}t+F_{\zeta}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta_{t}+\sum F_{q_{k}}\,\mathrm{d}q_{k}(t)

evaluated at
(\zeta_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t),t).

The drift term of
\mathrm{d}M_{t}/M_{t}
is

\Big(\frac{\dot{F}}{F}-\frac{\kappa}{2}\zeta_{t}^{2}\,\frac{F_{\zeta\zeta}}{F}-\Big(\kappa\zeta_{t}^{2}\frac{H_{\zeta}}{H}+3\zeta_{t}\Big)\frac{F_{\zeta}}{F}+\sum_{k}\Big(q_{k}(t)\frac{\zeta_{t}+q_{k}(t)}{\zeta_{t}-q_{k}(t)}\Big)\frac{F_{q_{k}}}{F}\Big)\Big|_{(\zeta_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)}.

We rewrite the drift term of
\mathrm{d}M_{t}/M_{t}
in terms of
G
and
H:

(8.18)

\Big(\frac{\dot{G}}{G}-\frac{\kappa}{2}\zeta_{t}^{2}\,\Big(\frac{G_{\zeta\zeta}}{G}-\frac{H_{\zeta\zeta}}{H}\Big)-3\zeta_{t}\Big(\frac{G_{\zeta}}{G}-\frac{H_{\zeta}}{H}\Big)+\sum_{k}\Big(q_{k}(t)\frac{\zeta_{t}+q_{k}(t)}{\zeta_{t}-q_{k}(t)}\Big)\Big(\frac{G_{q_{k}}}{G}-\frac{H_{q_{k}}}{H}\Big)\Big)
