evaluated at
(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t),t).

Using the similar method in
[
18
, Section 14.5]
, we represent
\dot{G}
in terms of the Lie derivatives:

(8.19)

\displaystyle\dot{G}(z,\boldsymbol{q},t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}(\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathbb{D}}\,\|\,h_{t+s}^{-1})(z)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}(\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathbb{D}}\,\|\,h_{t}^{-1}\circ f_{s,t}^{-1})(z)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{L}(v,\mathbb{D})\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathbb{D}}\,\|\,h_{t}^{-1})(z),

where
f_{s,t}=h_{t+s}\circ h_{t}^{-1}
and

(v\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{D}})=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}f_{s,t}=\dot{h}_{t}\circ h_{t}^{-1}.

We only need to compute the vector field
v.

We represent
v
as the difference of two Loewner vector fields associated with the flows in the domains
\mathbb{D}
and
\Omega_{t}.

Applying the chain rule to
h_{t}=\widetilde{g}_{t}\circ\Psi_{K}\circ g_{t}^{-1}
and computing the capacity changes, we have

\dot{h}_{t}(z)=|h_{t}^{\prime}(\zeta_{t})|^{2}v_{\widetilde{\zeta}_{t}}(h_{t}(z))-h_{t}^{\prime}(z)v_{\zeta_{t}}(z),\quad\Big(v_{\zeta}(z)=z\frac{\zeta+z}{\zeta-z}\Big),

where
\widetilde{\zeta}_{t}=h_{t}(\zeta_{t}).

By the above equation and
(v\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{D}})=\dot{h}_{t}\circ h_{t}^{-1},

(8.20)

(v\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{D}})(z)=|h_{t}^{\prime}(\zeta_{t})|^{2}v_{\widetilde{\zeta}_{t}}(z)-h_{t}^{\prime}(h_{t}^{-1}(z))v_{\zeta_{t}}(h_{t}^{-1}(z)).

It follows from (
8.19
) and (
8.20
) that

\displaystyle\dot{G}(z,\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)

\displaystyle=|h_{t}^{\prime}(\zeta_{t})|^{2}h_{t}^{\prime}(z)^{\lambda}\prod\big(h_{t}^{\prime}(q_{k}(t))\big)^{\lambda_{k}}\,\mathbf{E}\,\big(\mathcal{L}(v_{\widetilde{\zeta}_{t}},\mathbb{D})\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathbb{D}}(h_{t}(z)\big)\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{D}})

\displaystyle-\big(\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{L}(v_{\zeta_{t}})\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\Omega_{t}}\big)(z).

By Ward’s equation,

\displaystyle\dot{G}(z,\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)

\displaystyle=2|h_{t}^{\prime}(\zeta_{t})|^{2}\prod\big(h_{t}^{\prime}(q_{k}(t))\big)^{\lambda_{k}}h_{t}^{\prime}(z)^{\lambda}\widetilde{\zeta}_{t}^{2}(\mathbf{E}\,T_{\mathbb{D}}(\widetilde{\zeta}_{t})\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathbb{D}}(h_{t}(z))\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{D}})

\displaystyle-(\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{L}(v_{\zeta_{t}})\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\Omega_{t}}\big)(z).

It follows from conformal invariance that

\displaystyle\dot{G}(z,\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)

\displaystyle=2\zeta_{t}^{2}(\mathbf{E}\,T_{\Omega_{t}}(\zeta_{t})\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}(z)\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\Omega_{t}})-\frac{c}{6}\zeta_{t}^{2}S_{h_{t}}(\zeta_{t})\,(\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\Omega_{t}}\big)(z)

\displaystyle-(\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{L}(v_{\zeta_{t}})\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Omega_{t}}\,\|\,\mathrm{id}_{\Omega_{t}}\big)(z).

Sending
z
to
\zeta_{t}
and applying (
7.15
) (and
T*_{-1}\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}=\partial\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}},T*_{-2}\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}=h\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}
, see (
7.16
)),

\frac{\dot{G}(\zeta_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)}{G(\zeta_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)}=-\frac{c}{6}\zeta_{t}^{2}S_{h_{t}}(\zeta_{t})+\frac{L(\zeta_{t})G(\zeta_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)}{G(\zeta_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)}-\frac{\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta_{t}}}G(\zeta_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)}{G(\zeta_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)},

where
L(z)=2z^{2}L_{-2}(z)+3zL_{-1}(z)+L_{0}(z).

By (
8.18
) and the level two degeneracy equation, the drift of
\mathrm{d}M_{t}/M_{t}
simplifies to

\displaystyle-\frac{c}{6}\zeta_{t}^{2}S_{h_{t}}(\zeta_{t})

\displaystyle-\frac{\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta_{t}}}G(\zeta_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)}{G(\zeta_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)}+\Big(\frac{\kappa}{2}\zeta_{t}^{2}\,\frac{H_{\zeta\zeta}}{H}+3\zeta_{t}\frac{H^{\prime}}{H}+h\Big)\Big|_{(\zeta_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k}\Big(q_{k}(t)\frac{\zeta_{t}+q_{k}(t)}{\zeta_{t}-q_{k}(t)}\Big)\Big(\frac{G_{q_{k}}}{G}-\frac{H_{q_{k}}}{H}\Big)\Big|_{(\zeta_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)}.

It follows from the null vector equation that

\frac{\kappa}{2}\zeta_{t}^{2}\,\frac{H_{\zeta\zeta}}{H}+3\zeta_{t}\frac{H^{\prime}}{H}+h=\frac{\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta_{t}}}H}{H}.

Thus we find the drift term of
\mathrm{d}M_{t}/M_{t}
as

-\zeta_{t}^{2}\frac{c}{6}S_{h_{t}}(\zeta_{t})-\Big(\frac{\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta_{t}}}G}{G}-\frac{\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta_{t}}}H}{H}\Big)+\sum_{k}\Big(q_{k}(t)\frac{\zeta_{t}+q_{k}(t)}{\zeta_{t}-q_{k}(t)}\Big)\Big(\frac{G_{q_{k}}}{G}-\frac{H_{q_{k}}}{H}\Big)

evaluated at
(\xi_{t},\boldsymbol{q}(t),t).

Since

\frac{\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta_{t}}}G}{G}-\frac{\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta_{t}}}H}{H}=\sum_{k}\Big(q_{k}(t)\frac{\zeta_{t}+q_{k}(t)}{\zeta_{t}-q_{k}(t)}\Big)\Big(\frac{G_{q_{k}}}{G}-\frac{H_{q_{k}}}{H}\Big),

we have (
8.17
).
∎

9.
Conformal field theory with Neumann boundary condition

In this section we briefly implement a version of conformal field theory from background charge modifications of the Gaussian field with Neumann boundary condition.
In the last subsection we present the connection of this theory to the theory of the backward SLEs.

9.1.
Gaussian free field with Neumann boundary condition

The Gaussian free field
N
in a planar domain
D
with Neumann boundary condition is an isometry

N:\mathcal{E}_{N}(D)\to L^{2}(\Omega,\mathbf{P})

from the Neumann energy space
\mathcal{E}_{N}(D)
such that the image consists of centered Gaussian random variables.
Here
(\Omega,\mathbf{P})
is a probability space and
\mathcal{E}_{N}(D)
is the completion of smooth functions up to the boundary with mean zero (or the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0})
), compact supports in
D\,\cup\,\partial D,
and Neumann boundary condition
\nabla f\cdot n=0
(where
n
is normal to
\partial D
)
with respect to the norm

\|f\|^{2}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}}=\iint 2G_{N}(\zeta,z)\,f(\zeta)\,\overline{f(z)}~\mathrm{d}A(\zeta)\,\mathrm{d}A(z),

where
A
is the normalized area measure and
G_{N}
is the Neumann Green’s function for
D.

In the upper half-plane, we have

G_{N}(\zeta,z)=\log\frac{1}{|(\zeta-z)(\zeta-\bar{z})|}.

As in the Dirichlet case,
N
can be constructed from the Gaussian free field
\Psi
on its Schottky double
S=D^{\mathrm{double}}.

The Neumann energy space
\mathcal{E}_{N}(D)
can be embedded isometrically into
\mathcal{E}(S)
in a natural way.
For example, for
\mu
in the energy space
\mathcal{E}_{N}(\mathbb{H}),

\displaystyle\|\mu\|_{\mathcal{E}_{N}(\mathbb{H})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}}\log\frac{1}{|z-w|^{2}}\,\mu(z)\overline{\mu(w)}+\int_{\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}}\log\frac{1}{|z-\bar{w}|^{2}}\,\mu(z)\overline{\mu(w)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}}\log\frac{1}{|z-w|^{2}}\,\nu(z)\overline{\nu(w)}=\frac{1}{2}\|\nu\|_{\mathcal{E}(\widehat{\mathbb{C}})}^{2},

where
\nu=\mu
in the upper half-plane
\mathbb{H}
and
\nu=\mu^{*}
in the lower half-plane
\mathbb{H}^{*}

(
\mu^{*}(E)=\mu(E^{*}),

E^{*}=\{\bar{z},z\in E\}
).
For example, a test function
f
in
\mathbb{H}
with mean zero and Neumann boundary condition extends smoothly to
\mathbb{C}
such that
f(\bar{z})=f(z).

As a Fock space space, the Gaussian free field
N\equiv N_{D}
in
D
can be constructed from the Gaussian free field
\Psi\equiv\Psi_{S}:

N(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\Psi(z)+\Psi(z^{*})).

This field
N(z)
is formal and thus we need to require the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0})
on
N.
For example,
N(z,z_{0}):=N(z)-N(z_{0})
is a 2-variant well-defined Fock space field.
The formal field
N
has the formal correlation:

\mathbf{E}\,N(z)N(z_{0})=2G_{N}(z,z_{0}).

From the Schottky double construction, the current field
J_{D}^{D}=\partial\Phi_{D}
in
D
with Dirichlet boundary condition and the current field
J_{S}=\partial\Psi_{S}
are related as follows. For example,

J_{\mathbb{H}}^{D}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(J_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}(z)-\overline{J_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}(\bar{z})}\big).

On the other hand, the current field
J_{\mathbb{H}}^{N}
with Neumann boundary condition is related to
J_{\mathbb{C}}
as

J_{\mathbb{H}}^{N}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(J_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}(z)+\overline{J_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}(\bar{z})}\big).

In a similar way, stress tensors are related as

A_{\mathbb{H}}^{D}(z)=-\frac{1}{2}J_{\mathbb{H}}^{D}\odot J_{\mathbb{H}}^{D}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\big(A_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}(z)+\overline{A_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}(\bar{z})}+J_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}(z)\odot\overline{J_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}(\bar{z})}\big)

and

A_{\mathbb{H}}^{N}(z)=-\frac{1}{2}J_{\mathbb{H}}^{N}\odot J_{\mathbb{H}}^{N}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\big(A_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}(z)+\overline{A_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}(\bar{z})}-J_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}(z)\odot\overline{J_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}(\bar{z})}\big).

It is easy to check that
A^{N}
is a stress tensor for
N.

Indeed, as
\zeta\to z,
we have the following Ward’s OPE for
N:

A^{N}(\zeta)N(z)=-\frac{1}{2}J^{N}(\zeta)\odot J^{N}(\zeta)N(z)=-\mathbf{E}[J^{N}(\zeta)N(z)]J^{N}(\zeta)\sim\frac{J^{N}(\zeta)}{\zeta-z}\sim\frac{\partial N(z)}{\zeta-z}

in the identity chart of
\mathbb{H}.

Here we use

\mathbf{E}\,J^{N}(\zeta)N(z)=-\frac{1}{\zeta-z}-\frac{1}{\zeta-\bar{z}}

in
\mathbb{H}.

Formal fields
N^{\pm}(z)
with Neumann boundary condition are defined by

N^{\pm}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\Psi^{\pm}(z)+\Psi^{\mp}(z^{*})).

For example, we have

(9.1)

\mathbf{E}\,N(z)N^{+}(z_{0})=\log\frac{1}{z-z_{0}}+\log\frac{1}{\bar{z}-z_{0}},\qquad\mathbf{E}\,N(z)N^{-}(z_{0})=\log\frac{1}{z-\bar{z}_{0}}+\log\frac{1}{\bar{z}-\bar{z}_{0}},

and

\mathbf{E}\,N^{+}(z)N^{+}(z_{0})=\log\frac{1}{z-z_{0}},\qquad\mathbf{E}\,N^{+}(z)N^{-}(z_{0})=\log\frac{1}{z-\bar{z}_{0}}

in
\mathbb{H}.

As in the Dirichlet case, 2-variant fields
N^{\pm}(z,z_{0}):=N^{\pm}(z)-N^{\pm}(z_{0})
are well-defined multivalued Fock space fields.

9.2.
Modifications and OPE exponentials

We first define background charge modifications of the chiral bosonic fields with Neumann boundary condition.
Given double background charges

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\pm}=\sum_{k}\beta_{k}^{\pm}\cdot q_{k}
with the neutrality conditions
(\mathrm{NC}_{b^{\pm}})

in
D,
we define the formal fields
N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\pm}\equiv N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{\pm}
(
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}^{-}
) by

N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\pm}:=N^{\pm}+n_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\pm},

where
n_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}\equiv n_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{+}
is a
\mathrm{PPS}(b,0)
form (
b=b^{+}+b^{-}
) and
n_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-}\equiv n_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}^{-}
is a
\mathrm{PPS}(0,b)
form such that

(9.2)

\displaystyle n_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum\beta_{k}^{+}\log\frac{1}{z-q_{k}}+\sum\beta_{k}^{-}\log\frac{1}{z-\bar{q}_{k}}

\displaystyle n_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum\beta_{k}^{+}\log\frac{1}{\bar{z}-q_{k}}+\sum\beta_{k}^{-}\log\frac{1}{\bar{z}-\bar{q}_{k}}

in the
\mathbb{H}
-uniformization.
Furthermore,
n_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:=n_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}+n_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-}
is a
\mathrm{PPS}(b,b)
form.
For two divisors
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\pm}
satisfying the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}),
we define

N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]\equiv N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{-}]:=\sum_{j}\big(\tau_{j}^{+}N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}(z_{j})+\tau_{j}^{-}N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-}(z_{j})\big),

where
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{*}^{-}.

Compare this definition to the definition of
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]
in the Dirichlet case:

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]:=\sum_{j}\big(\tau_{j}^{+}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{+}(z_{j})-\tau_{j}^{-}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-}(z_{j})\big),

where
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{*}^{-}
satisfies the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{0}).

We now explain why the signs of
\tau_{j}^{-}
appear differently.
The choices of signs are consistent with the representation of
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\boldsymbol{\tau}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{*}^{-}
when the nodes are on the boundary.
For
z\in\partial D,
we have
\Phi(z)=0
and thus the formal field
\Phi[1\cdot z]
can be represented either by
\Phi^{+}(z)
or by
-\Phi^{-}(z).

On the other hand, the formal field
N[1\cdot z]
is represented by
N^{+}(z)=N^{-}(z).

The relation
N^{+}(z)=N^{-}(z)
for
z\in\partial D
is obvious:

\mathbf{E}\,N^{+}(z)N(z_{0})=\log\frac{1}{(z-z_{0})(z-\bar{z}_{0})}=\log\frac{1}{(\bar{z}-z_{0})(\bar{z}-\bar{z}_{0})}=\mathbf{E}\,N^{-}(z)N(z_{0})

in the
\mathbb{H}
-uniformization.

A stress tensor
A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\equiv A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{N}
for
N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is given by

A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=A+(b\partial-j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}})J,\qquad j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:=\mathbf{E}\,J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\qquad J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\equiv J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{N}=\partial N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

As
\zeta\to z,
in
\mathbb{H},
Ward’s OPE for
N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
holds:

A_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\zeta)N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z,z_{0})=\Big(A(\zeta)+\big(b\partial_{\zeta}-j_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\zeta)\big)J(\zeta)\Big)N(z,z_{0})\\
\sim\frac{J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)}{\zeta-z}+\frac{b}{(\zeta-z)^{2}}.

In the symmetric case
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,
the Virasoro field

T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=-\frac{1}{2}J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}*J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+b\partial J_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

gives rise to the Virasoro pair
(T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu).

The central charge
c
in this theory is given by

c=1+12b^{2}.

If the parameter
b
is related to the SLE parameter
\kappa
as

(9.3)

b=-a(\kappa/4+1),\qquad a=\pm\sqrt{2/\kappa},

then

c=13+12(\kappa/8+2/\kappa)\geq 25.

Modified multi-vertex fields or OPE exponentials
\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]
are defined by

\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}]=\frac{C_{(-ib)}[-i(\boldsymbol{\tau}+\boldsymbol{\beta})]}{C_{(-ib)}[-i\boldsymbol{\beta}]}\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot N[\boldsymbol{\tau}]}.

We denote by
\mathcal{F}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}
the OPE family of
N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}.

Theorem 9.1
.

Given two double background charges
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\pm},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{\pm}
with the neutrality conditions
(\mathrm{NC}_{b^{\pm}}),

\mathcal{F}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}
is the image of
\mathcal{F}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{-}}
under the insertion of
\mathrm{e}^{\odot N[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{-}]}.

Proof.

By Wick’s calculus, we have

\mathbf{E}\,N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{-}}(z,z^{\prime})\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot N[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{-}]}=n_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{-}}(z,z^{\prime})+\mathbf{E}\,N(z,z^{\prime})N[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{-}].

In the
\mathbb{H}
-uniformization, it follows from (
9.1
) and (
9.2
) that

\mathbf{E}\,N(z,z^{\prime})N[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{-}]=n_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}(z,z^{\prime})-n_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{-}}(z,z^{\prime}).

Since both sides are scalars, the above identity holds for any chart.
Thus we have

\mathbf{E}\,N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{-}}(z,z^{\prime})\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot N[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{-}]}=\mathbf{E}\,N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}(z,z^{\prime}).

As in the Dirichlet case, we use Wick’s calculus again to derive

\mathbf{E}\,N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{-}}(z_{1},z_{1}^{\prime})\odot\cdots\odot N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{-}}(z_{n},z_{n}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{e}^{\odot N[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{-}]}=\mathbf{E}\,N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}(z_{1},z_{1}^{\prime})\odot\cdots\odot N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}}(z_{n},z_{n}^{\prime}).

Differentiating with respect to
z_{j},z_{j}^{\prime}
, we complete the proof.
∎

Example
.

Given a marked boundary point
q\in\partial D,
we consider a conformal map
w:(D,q)\to(\mathbb{H},\infty).

For
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+}=2b\cdot q
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}=\boldsymbol{0},

N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z,z_{0})=N(z,z_{0})+2b(\log|w^{\prime}(z)|-\log|w^{\prime}(z_{0})|).

The function
(z,z_{0})\mapsto\log|w^{\prime}(z)|-\log|w^{\prime}(z_{0})|
does not depend on the choice of
w.

9.3.
One-leg operators

We consider a simply-connected domain
D
with two marked points
p\in\partial D,

q\in\partial D
in the chordal case, and
q\in D
in the radial case.
For a symmetric background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}
on
S=D^{\mathrm{double}}
with
\boldsymbol{\beta}(p)=a
and the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b}),
the one-leg operator
\Lambda_{p}
in the chordal case (the radial case, respectively) is defined by

\Lambda_{p}\equiv\Lambda(p)=\mathcal{O}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}[\boldsymbol{\tau}],\qquad\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}=\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\tau}

where
\boldsymbol{\tau}=a\cdot p-a\cdot q
in the chordal case (and
\boldsymbol{\tau}=a\cdot p-\frac{1}{2}a\cdot q-\frac{1}{2}a\cdot q^{*}
in the radial case, respectively).
The insertion of
\Lambda_{p}/\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{p}
is an operator

\mathcal{X}\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{X}}

on Fock space functionals/fields by the rules (
5.20
)
and the formula in the chordal case

\widehat{N}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(z,z_{0})=N_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(z,z_{0})-2a\big(\log|w(z)|-\log|w(z_{0})|\big)\big)

where
w:(D,p,q)\to(\mathbb{H},0,\infty)
is a conformal map.
In the radial case, the corresponding formula is given by

\widehat{N}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(z,z_{0})=N_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(z,z_{0})-2a\big(\log|1-w(z)|-\log|1-w(z_{0})|\big)\big)+a\big(\log|w(z)|-\log|w(z_{0})|\big)\big)

where
w:(D,p,q)\to(\mathbb{D},1,0)
is a conformal map.

We denote

\widehat{\mathbf{E}}\,\mathcal{X}:=\frac{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{p}\mathcal{X}}{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{p}}.

By Theorem
9.1
, we have

\widehat{\mathbf{E}}\,\mathcal{X}=\mathbf{E}\,\widehat{\mathcal{X}}

for any string
\mathcal{X}
of fields in
\mathcal{F}^{N}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}.

We now show the level two degeneracy equations for
\Lambda
if the parameters
a
and
b
satisfy
2a(a+b)=-1.

We remark that
a
and
b
in (
9.3
) have such a relation.

Proposition 9.2
.

Provided that
2a(a+b)=-1,
we have

(9.4)

T_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}*\Lambda=-\frac{1}{2a^{2}}\partial^{2}\Lambda.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{a}=-ia,\tilde{b}=-ib.

In terms of the action of Virasoro generators and current generators, the one-leg operators are Virasoro primary holomorphic fields of conformal dimension
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{a}^{2}-\tilde{a}\tilde{b}
and current primary with charge
q=\tilde{a}.

This implies the level two degeneracy equation (
9.4
) for the one-leg operator provided that
2\tilde{a}(\tilde{a}+\tilde{b})=1.

We here present an alternate but direct proof using Wick’s calculus.
For simplicity, we consider the standard chordal case
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}=2b\cdot q
only.
It is left to the reader as an exercise to prove (
9.4
) for general background charges.
Since the difference is a differential, it suffices to show (
9.4
) in
\mathbb{H}.

In the
\mathbb{H}
-uniformization we have

N_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}=N,\quad\partial N_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}=J_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}=J,\quad T_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}=T+b\partial J,\quad T=-\frac{1}{2}J\odot J,\quad\Lambda(z)=\mathrm{e}^{\odot aN^{+}(z,q)}.

Let us first compute
T*\Lambda:

T*\Lambda=T\odot\Lambda+a\partial J\odot\Lambda.

Indeed, as
\zeta\to z,
we have

T(\zeta)\Lambda(z)=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{a^{n}}{n!}J^{\odot 2}(\zeta)(N^{+})^{\odot n}(z,q)=T\odot\Lambda(z)+\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{II}+o(1),

where terms
\mathrm{I}
and
\mathrm{II}
come from 1 and 2 contractions, respectively.
It follows from

\mathbf{E}\,J(\zeta)N^{+}(z,q)=-1/(\zeta-z)
that

\mathrm{I}=\frac{a}{\zeta-z}J(\zeta)\odot N^{+}(z,q).

Thus its contribution to
T*\Lambda
is
a\partial J\odot\Lambda.

On the other hand, we have

\mathrm{II}=-\frac{1}{2}a^{2}\big(\mathbf{E}\,J(\zeta)N^{+}(z,q)\big)^{2}\Lambda(z)=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{a^{2}}{(\zeta-z)^{2}}\Lambda(z).

It has no contribution to
T*\Lambda.

To compute
T_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}*\Lambda,
we need to compute
\partial J*\Lambda.

We have

\partial J(\zeta)\Lambda(z)=\partial J\odot\Lambda(z)+o(1).

This implies that
\partial J*\Lambda=\partial J\odot\Lambda
and

T_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}*\Lambda=T\odot\Lambda+(a+b)\partial J\odot\Lambda.

Differentiating
\Lambda(z)=\mathrm{e}^{\odot aN^{+}(z,q)},
we have

-\frac{1}{2a^{2}}\partial^{2}\Lambda=-\frac{1}{2a^{2}}\partial(aJ\odot\Lambda)=-\frac{1}{2a^{2}}(a\partial J\odot\Lambda+a^{2}J\odot J\odot\Lambda)=-\frac{1}{2a}\partial J\odot\Lambda+T\odot\Lambda.

Now (
9.4
) follows provided that
2a(a+b)=-1.

∎

9.4.
Ward’s equations and BPZ-Cardy equations

We define the puncture operators by
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:=C_{(-ib)}[-i\boldsymbol{\beta}].

As in the Dirichlet case, Ward’s equations hold for the extended OPE family
\mathcal{F}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
of
N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

Since its proof is similar to the Dirichlet case, we leave it to the reader as an exercise.

Theorem 9.3

(Ward’s equations)
.

Let
Y,X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{F}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
and let
X
be the tensor product of
X_{j}
’s.
Then

\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)\,X=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{+}_{k_{z}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}X+\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{-}_{k_{\bar{z}}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}X,

where all fields are evaluated in the identity chart of
\mathbb{H}
and

2z^{2}\mathbf{E}\,T_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)\,X=\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{+}_{v_{z}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}X+\mathbf{E}\,\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{-}_{v_{z^{*}}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}X,

where all fields are evaluated in the identity chart of
\mathbb{D}.

For a symmetric background charge
\boldsymbol{\beta}
with the neutrality condition
(\mathrm{NC}_{b})
and a specific charge
a
at a marked boundary point
p\in\partial D,
we define the backward SLE partition function
Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
by

Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:=|\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|=|C_{(-ib)}[-i\boldsymbol{\beta}]|

and the effective one-leg operators by
\Lambda_{p}^{\mathrm{eff}}:=\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\,\Lambda_{p}.

Denote

(9.5)

R_{\xi}\equiv\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,\mathcal{X}:=\frac{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{\xi}\mathcal{X}}{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{\xi}}=\frac{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{\xi}^{\mathrm{eff}}\mathcal{X}}{\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda_{\xi}^{\mathrm{eff}}}.

Let
\xi\in\partial D
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}=\boldsymbol{\beta}-a\cdot p+a\cdot\xi.

Let
X
be the tensor product
X=X_{1}(z_{1})\cdots X_{n}(z_{n})
of fields
X_{j}
in
\mathcal{F}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}

(z_{j}\in D).

Combining Proposition
9.2
with Theorem
9.3
, we obtain the BPZ equations:

(9.6)

-\frac{1}{2a^{2}}\partial_{\xi}^{2}\mathbf{E}\Lambda_{\xi}^{\mathrm{eff}}X=\mathbf{E}\,\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}\Lambda_{\xi}^{\mathrm{eff}}X,\qquad k_{\xi}(z):=\frac{1}{\xi-z}

in the
\mathbb{H}
-uniformization.
In particular, the partition function
Z_{\xi}\equiv Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}}:=\big|\mathbf{E}\,\Lambda^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\xi}\big|=\big|C_{(-ib)}[-i\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}]\big|
satisfies the null vector equation

(9.7)

-\frac{1}{2a^{2}}\partial_{\xi}^{2}Z_{\xi}=\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}Z_{\xi}

in the
\mathbb{H}
-uniformization.
Here
\partial_{\xi}=\partial+\bar{\partial}
is the operator of differentiation with respect to the real variable
\xi
and and
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}
is taken over the finite notes of
X
and
\mathrm{supp}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\xi}\setminus\{\xi\}.

We obtain the following form of BPZ-Cardy equations in the chordal case.
The radial case can be derived in a similar way.

Theorem 9.4

(BPZ-Cardy equations)
.

Suppose that the parameters
a
and
b
satisfy

2a(a+b)=-1.

If
X=X_{1}(z_{1})\cdots X_{n}(z_{n})
with
X_{j}\in\mathcal{F}^{N}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}},
then we have

(9.8)

-\frac{1}{2a^{2}}\big(\partial_{\xi}^{2}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X+2(\partial_{\xi}\log Z_{\xi})\partial_{\xi}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X\big)=\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi}}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}\,X

in the identity chart of
\mathbb{H}.

In the
(\mathbb{D},0)
-uniformization we have

(9.9)

\kappa\Big(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+(\partial_{\theta}\log Z_{\zeta}\big)\partial_{\theta}\Big)\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}X=\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta}}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}X,\qquad\zeta=\mathrm{e}^{i\theta}.

9.5.
Connection to backward SLE theory

We now prove Theorem
1.6
and present Sheffield’s observables.
By definition, the backward radial (see (
1.11
) for the chordal case)
\mathrm{SLE}[\boldsymbol{\beta}]
map
f_{t}
from
\mathbb{D}
satisfies the equation

(9.10)

\partial_{t}f_{t}(z)=-f_{t}(z)\frac{\zeta_{t}+f_{t}(z)}{\zeta_{t}-f_{t}(z)},\qquad\zeta_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{t}}

driven by the real process
\theta_{t}:

\mathrm{d}\theta_{t}=\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\lambda(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,\quad\lambda(t)=(\lambda\,\|\,f_{t}^{-1}),\quad\lambda=\kappa\,\partial_{\theta}\log Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\zeta}},

where the partition function
Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\zeta}}
is given by
Z_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\zeta}}=\big|C_{(-ib)}[-i\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\zeta}]\big|,

\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\zeta}=\boldsymbol{\beta}+a\cdot\zeta-a\cdot p.

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

We first consider the chordal case.
For
\mathcal{X}=X_{1}(z_{1})\cdots X_{n}(z_{n}),X_{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(D),
denote

R_{\xi}(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})\equiv\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\xi}[X_{1}(z_{1})\cdots X_{n}(z_{n})].

We want to show that

M_{t}=m(\xi_{t},t),\quad m(\xi,t)=(R_{\xi}\,\|\,f_{t}^{-1})

is a local martingale on backward chordal SLE probability space.
By Itô’s formula, we compute
\mathrm{d}M_{t}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}M_{t}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\kappa}\,\partial_{\xi}\big|_{\xi=\xi_{t}}m(\xi,t)\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\kappa\,\partial_{\xi}\big|_{\xi=\xi_{t}}m(\xi,t)\,\partial_{\xi}\big|_{\xi=\xi_{t}}(\log Z_{\xi}\,\|\,f_{t}^{-1})

\displaystyle+\frac{\kappa}{2}\,\partial_{\xi}^{2}\big|_{\xi=\xi_{t}}m(\xi,t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}\big(R_{\xi_{t}}\,\|\,f_{t+s}^{-1}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Using the same argument in the proof of Theorem
1.5
, the last term can be rewritten as

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}\big(R_{\xi_{t}}\,\|\,f_{t+s}^{-1}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t=2\big(\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi_{t}}}R_{\xi_{t}}\,\|\,f_{t}^{-1}\big).

Thus we find the drift term of
\mathrm{d}M_{t}
as

\Big(\frac{\kappa}{2}\,\partial_{\xi}^{2}\big|_{\xi=\xi_{t}}m(\xi,t)+\kappa\,\partial_{\xi}\big|_{\xi=\xi_{t}}m(\xi,t)\frac{\partial_{\xi}|_{\xi=\xi_{t}}(Z_{\xi}\,\|\,f_{t}^{-1})}{(Z_{\xi}\,\|\,f_{t}^{-1})}+2\big(\check{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{\xi_{t}}}R_{\xi_{t}}\,\|\,f_{t}^{-1}\big)\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t=0

employing the BPZ-Cardy equations (
9.8
).

Next, we consider the radial case.
For
\mathcal{X}=X_{1}(z_{1})\cdots X_{n}(z_{n}),X_{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}\,}}(D),
denote

R_{\zeta}(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})\equiv\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\zeta}[X_{1}(z_{1})\cdots X_{n}(z_{n})].

As in the chordal case, the process

M_{t}=m(\zeta_{t},t),\qquad m(\zeta,t)=\big(R_{\zeta}\,\|\,f_{t}^{-1}\big)

is a local martingale by Itô’s formula and the BPZ-Cardy equations (
9.9
).
Indeed, we find the drift term of
\mathrm{d}M_{t}
as

\Big(\frac{\kappa}{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\kappa\frac{\partial_{\theta}(Z_{\zeta}\,\|\,f_{t}^{-1})}{(Z_{\zeta}\,\|\,f_{t}^{-1})}\partial_{\theta}\Big)\Big|_{\zeta=\zeta_{t}}~m(\zeta,t)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
-\ \big(\check{\mathcal{L}}_{v_{\zeta_{t}}}R_{\zeta_{t}}\,\|\,f_{t}^{-1}\big)\mathrm{d}t=0.

∎

Example

(Sheffield’s observables)
.

In the standard chordal case with

\boldsymbol{\beta}=a\cdot p+(2b-a)\cdot q,

the 1-point functions of the bosonic fields

\displaystyle n_{t}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}[N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z)\,\|\,f_{t}^{-1}]=-2a\log|f_{t}(z)-\xi_{t}|+2b\log|f_{t}^{\prime}(z)|

\displaystyle=-2a\log|z|+2\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{Re}\,\frac{1}{f_{s}(z)-\xi_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}B_{s}

were introduced as backward SLE martingale-observables in
[
34
]
.
Due to the following special case of Hadamard’s variation formula

(9.11)

\mathrm{d}G^{N}(f_{t}(z_{1}),f_{t}(z_{2}))=-4\,\mathrm{Re}\,\frac{1}{f_{t}(z_{1})-\xi_{t}}\,\mathrm{Re}\,\frac{1}{f_{t}(z_{2})-\xi_{t}}\,\mathrm{d}t=-\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{d}\langle n_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z_{1}),n_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z_{2})\rangle_{t},

the formal
2
-point functions

\mathbf{E}[N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z_{1})N_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z_{2})]=2G^{N}(z_{1},z_{2})+n_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z_{1})n_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(z_{2})

are martingale-observables for backward SLE.
Sheffield used (
9.11
) to construct a coupling of backward SLE and the Gaussian free field with Neumann boundary condition, see
[
34
]
.
